When the proper total can't be built for a *known* dual-class issuer, fail closed (suppress null) rather than publish the structurally-wrong product — the broken number is not a "fallback". And when summing per-class pieces against a combined total: read the combined at the SAME instant (companyfacts leads FSDS by ~a quarter), fail closed if Σ-pieces materially exceeds the combined (a real excess is a source/period mismatch, not a residual — never clamp-and-publish a broken invariant), cap the imputed residual as a minority of the total, and fall back across siblings for the combined so a missing fanout on one sibling can't break the identical-across-siblings invariant.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/xbrl_derived_stats.py::resolve_market_cap_basis` + `_assemble_total_company_cap` + `_build_total_company_cap` (#1662); shared gate `ownership_rollup.py::class_shares_usable`; data-engineer skill I20; metrics-analyst skill §"Market cap (live)"; spec `docs/specs/etl/2026-06-17-per-class-market-cap.md`.

### A source that lists the same shares under multiple owners (deemed/overlapping) is not additive — demote the whole source, don't heuristically de-dup it

- First seen in: PR (#1659, DEF 14A proxy double-count). The ticket asked to collapse name-keyed control-group restatements in the additive `def14a_unmatched` pie wedge (the third per-channel patch after #1644 family + #1652 insider-group). The retired heuristic plan (exact-value + non-round + ≥1M name clustering) looked safe on a 22-cluster sample — but a FULL-population dev scan (verify-before-assert, not a sample) falsified "FP ≈ 0": independent equal-grant executives share an exact non-round value (BXP "Bryan Koop" + "Michael LaBelle" @1,875,000; FOXA "Adam Ciongoli" + "Steven Tomsic" @2,750,000), and nothing in our data separates a deemed-joint-block from two independent equal holders — CIK is absent (proxy is name-only), `holder_role` matches across both, and the disambiguating footnote ("also deemed to own shares held by X") is free-text prose unstructured in EDGAR / edgartools / our parser. A name-clustering heuristic would have turned a display-only *over*-count into an *under*-count (dropping a real holder).
- Symptom: a class of double-count keeps spawning per-case heuristics (#1644, #1645, #1652, now #1659), each chasing the same overlapping-figure problem in a different channel. That recurrence is the signal that the SOURCE itself was mis-modelled as additive. Researching the source's own rules (SEC Item 403 / Rule 13d-3: the proxy "Security Ownership of Certain Beneficial Owners" table is deemed/overlapping BY DESIGN — "count each share once", footnote-disambiguated) + our own settled invariants (data-eng I14/I16 already said proxy = restatement, not independent) shows it was never an additive holdings source.
- Prevention: before adding a heuristic to de-dup a source feeding an additive aggregate, ask "is this source additive AT ALL, or does it list the same units under multiple owners?" If overlapping-by-design (proxy beneficial ownership, group/deemed disclosures), DEMOTE the whole source to a non-additive cross-check overlay (the existing funds/N-PORT memo-overlay pattern) — it still renders/exports for inspection, but contributes nothing to the pie/residual/concentration. That dissolves the entire double-count class at the source instead of chasing each channel. And NEVER trust a "FP ≈ 0" claim from a small sample when the disambiguating signal is absent from the data — scan the full population and hunt the dangerous shape (here: independent holders at the same exact value) before designing the de-dup. Drive the additive-vs-memo split off a single `denominator_basis` discriminator (`== "pie_wedge"` for additive, `!= "pie_wedge"` for overlay) so a new overlay is excluded everywhere by construction.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/ownership_rollup.py` def14a slice `denominator_basis="proxy_disclosure"` (#1659) + the basis-driven residual/concentration/sanity/per-class-guard filters; API + FE `denominator_basis` overlay rendering; data-engineer skill I21 (+ I14/I16); metrics-analyst skill §"DEF 14A"; spec `docs/specs/etl/2026-06-17-def14a-nonadditive-overlay.md`.

### A SQL view cannot reproduce a fail-closed multi-step policy — suppress in the view, compute the correct figure in the Python consumer that owns the policy

- First seen in: PR (#1664, ranking-view dual-class market cap). The `instrument_valuation` VIEW (sql/080) computed `market_cap_live = price × shares_outstanding` (the COMBINED all-class count) — the exact distortion #1662 retired on the display path, still live on the ranking path that `scoring.py` reads (and that drives the view's P/S, P/FCF, EV/EBITDA, P/B). The correct figure is the total-company cap (Σ class×price), which needs per-class prices + untraded-residual imputation + a stack of fail-closed guards (Σ-overage, residual cap, freshness, future-period) already implemented in `xbrl_derived_stats.py::_assemble_total_company_cap`. The tempting "materialize the total in the view" (issue option a) would have to RE-IMPLEMENT that multi-step policy in SQL → a second source of truth that silently drifts from the Python one.
- Symptom: a settled Python data-treatment policy (here total-company cap, #1662 / data-eng I20) is exposed through TWO read surfaces — a SQL view and a Python consumer — and the view still carries the pre-policy formula. Reproducing the policy in SQL duplicates load-bearing fail-closed logic; doing nothing leaves the view emitting a structurally-wrong, per-sibling-different number (GOOG $4.340T vs GOOGL $4.473T for one company) to any BI/operator-SQL reader.
- Prevention: keep ONE owner of a fail-closed multi-step policy (the Python helper). In the SQL view, take the fail-closed posture only — SUPPRESS (NULL) the columns the view cannot truthfully compute (here every column carrying a `price × combined-shares` term; KEEP the per-share/ratio columns that have no such term, e.g. P/E, debt/total-equity), detecting the condition with the SAME curated oracle the helper uses (presence in `instrument_class_shares_outstanding` by lpad-10 CIK, never a noisy shared-CIK sibling count). Then have the decision-grade consumer OVERLAY the correct figure via the helper, limited to the fields it consumes and can recompute correctly. Verify the distortion is consistent across every branch of the view before a blanket final-SELECT suppress: a column can be distorted in one CTE and clean in another — empirically confirm the per-CTE formula (here legacy `book_value` is per-share so legacy `pb` is distorted like new-pipeline, and legacy `debt_equity` = debt/total-equity is clean like new-pipeline — so the 8-column suppress list is correct in both CTEs). When the consumer's helper reads a table that may be absent in a partial test DB, wrap the call in its OWN savepoint + `UndefinedTable` guard, not just the original query's.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `sql/201_instrument_valuation_dual_class_suppress.sql` (view suppression) + `app/services/scoring.py::_apply_market_cap_basis` (Python overlay) + `_load_instrument_data` savepoint guard (#1664); reuses `xbrl_derived_stats.py::resolve_market_cap_basis` (#1662); data-engineer skill I20; spec `docs/specs/etl/2026-06-18-ranking-view-dual-class-market-cap.md`.

### A DB-backed test must use the canonical isolated test-cluster fixture — never hardcode the dev cluster's host/port/DB

- First seen in: PR (jobs/postgres health pass). `tests/test_filings_bulk_resolve.py` hand-rolled its own `conn` fixture pointing at `postgresql://postgres:postgres@127.0.0.1:5432/ebull_test` — the DEV cluster port (5432), plus a legacy plain `ebull_test` DB name that no longer exists (the suite long ago moved to a dedicated test cluster on `POSTGRES_TEST_PORT`=5433 with per-worker `ebull_test_<run>_<worker>` DBs cloned from `ebull_test_template`, #1444). Every time those tests were collected/run, the fixture's `psycopg.connect` hit the live dev Postgres → `FATAL: database "ebull_test" does not exist` in the dev log (4-per-burst, one per running test) — operator-visible noise that reads like a real failure. Worse, it was a latent foot-gun: the module docstring claimed "NOT the dev DB — destructive", yet the URL WAS the dev cluster; had an `ebull_test` DB ever existed on 5432, the destructive INSERT/TRUNCATE tests would have run against the operator's real data.
- Symptom: a hardcoded DB URL in a test file (host/port/dbname literal, or a bespoke `psycopg.connect` fixture) that bypasses `tests/fixtures/ebull_test_db.py`. It points at the wrong cluster (dev not test-cluster), spams `FATAL: database "…" does not exist` on collection/run, and silently relies on per-test `rollback()` for isolation instead of the suite's TRUNCATE-teardown + per-worker-DB model.
- Prevention: DB tests acquire their connection ONLY through the canonical fixture — depend on `ebull_test_conn` (or alias it: `@pytest.fixture\ndef conn(ebull_test_conn): return ebull_test_conn`, the established pattern in e.g. `test_ownership_observations_refresh_batch.py`). It targets the isolated 5433 cluster + per-worker DB, skips cleanly when the cluster is down (no FATAL), TRUNCATEs through `_assert_test_db` (the dev DB can never be wiped), and any new FK table it writes must be in `_PLANNER_TABLES`. Grep test files for `psycopg.connect(` and a `5432` / `/ebull_test"` literal during pre-flight — a DB test should never name a host/port/dbname.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `tests/test_filings_bulk_resolve.py` (converted to `ebull_test_conn`); the canonical `tests/fixtures/ebull_test_db.py::ebull_test_conn`; ops-monitor skill §"Orchestrator / sync health signals" (the FATAL-noise diagnosis).

### Chart series colours should read the resolved `theme`, not import `lightTheme` directly

- First seen in: PR #1671 (#591 risk drill page). New chart components imported `lightTheme` and used `lightTheme.down` / `lightTheme.accent[*]` for data-series fills/strokes while correctly using the `theme` from `useChartTheme()` for axes/grid. The review flagged it as a dark-mode bug.
- Symptom: a chart component calls `useChartTheme()` for axis/grid tones but hardcodes series colours to `lightTheme.*`. NOTE this is NOT a live colour bug today: `darkTheme` deliberately aliases `up` / `down` / `accent` / `indicator` / `regression` to the `lightTheme` references (`chartTheme.ts` — "Saturated accents reuse the light-mode references … preserve the operator's color memory"), so `lightTheme.down === theme.down` and dark mode renders identically (verified on the live page). The issue is fragility + consistency: if a saturated slot is ever de-aliased in `darkTheme`, every `lightTheme.*` series silently stops following the theme.
- Prevention: in any component that calls `useChartTheme()`, reference the returned `theme` for ALL colours (series included), not `lightTheme`. Do NOT add a blanket `grep lightTheme\. in useChartTheme files → assert zero` CI gate (the bot's suggestion): ~8 existing chart files (dividends/fundamentals/insider/ChartWorkspaceCanvas) already use `lightTheme.accent` for exactly the aliased-slot reason, so an assert-zero would false-positive on shipped code. Fix new components at write time; a repo-wide sweep of the existing files is a separate opt-in cleanup, not a merge gate.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `frontend/src/components/risk/riskCharts.tsx` (uses `theme.*` throughout); `.claude/skills/frontend/operator-ui-conventions.md` §"Color semantics".

### A savepoint-guarded optional read must catch the SAME exception breadth as the sibling pattern it mirrors

- First seen in: PR #1677 (#1633 realized-risk penalty). A new savepoint-guarded enrichment query in `scoring.py::_load_instrument_data` (reads `instrument_risk_metrics_current`) caught only `psycopg.errors.UndefinedTable`, while the sibling valuation savepoint immediately above caught `(UndefinedTable, UndefinedColumn)`. A partial schema where the table EXISTS but a selected column is absent raises `UndefinedColumn`, which would have propagated unhandled instead of degrading to `row = None`.
- Symptom: two savepoint-degrade blocks in the same function guard structurally-similar reads but catch different exception sets; the narrower one breaks on a partial-schema variant (table-present, column-absent) the wider one tolerates.
- Prevention: when adding a savepoint-guarded optional read that mirrors an existing one (graceful-degrade-to-None on a partial/test DB), match the sibling's FULL exception tuple — for a `SELECT col, … FROM table`, that is `(psycopg.errors.UndefinedTable, psycopg.errors.UndefinedColumn)`, because either the table or any selected column may be absent. At self-review: grep `except psycopg.errors.UndefinedTable:` (bare, no `UndefinedColumn`) and confirm each site either selects `*`/no columns or intentionally omits the column case.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/scoring.py::_load_instrument_data` (risk-metrics savepoint now catches both).

---

### Two writers sharing an advisory lock must use a BYTE-IDENTICAL lock-key SQL — pin it
- First seen in: PR #1681 (#1590 FSDS bulk dimensional facts). The bulk writer and #554's `replace_accession_rows` serialise their check-then-insert against each other with a shared `pg_advisory_xact_lock`. The lock only serialises them if BOTH compute the SAME key — i.e. the SQL template is identical. A future edit to one site (e.g. changing the hash expression, or adding a `::text` cast to one) would silently desync the keys, reopening the bulk/per-filing double-count window with NO test failure (each writer still works in isolation; only their mutual exclusion breaks).
- Symptom: two independent code paths take "the same" advisory lock by convention, but nothing enforces that their lock-key SQL actually matches. Also: an advisory key that casts a `BIGINT` id with `%s::int` overflows for ids > 2^31 (use `hashtext(%s::text || ':' || %s::text)::bigint` — both params `::text` so `||` never depends on psycopg3 OID inference).
- Prevention: when two writers must serialise via a shared advisory lock, (1) make the lock-key SQL byte-identical and (2) PIN it with a tripwire test that greps both source files for the `pg_advisory_xact_lock` template and asserts equality — a desync then fails CI, not production. Never cast a BIGINT id to int4 in a lock key.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/fsds_dimensional_facts.py::_write_bulk_accession` + `app/services/dimensional_facts_store.py::replace_accession_rows` (identical bigint-safe template); `tests/test_fsds_dimensional_facts.py::test_lock_templates_byte_identical` (the tripwire).

### A raw-UPDATE status transition that bypasses the shared finish path loses the retry/self-heal it would have scheduled

- First seen in: hotfix (orphaned jobs sat red on the admin console after every daemon restart). `reap_orphaned_job_runs` transitions restart-interrupted `running` job_runs → `failure` via a raw `UPDATE` that set status + error_category but NOT `next_retry_at`/`attempt`. The natural failure path (`record_job_finish` → `_retry_plan`) is what schedules a retry for a transient (`self_heal`) category, and `INTERNAL_ERROR` (the reaper's category) IS self_heal — but the reaper never called it. So the `jobs_retry_sweeper` had nothing to re-fire; the job sat red until its next natural cadence. A restart-interruption is the MOST transient failure there is, yet it was the one that never self-healed.
- Symptom: two code paths reach the same terminal state (`failure`), but only one routes through the shared finish/retry helper; the bypassing path silently drops every side effect that helper owns (retry scheduling, attempt counting, audit). The gap is invisible until you ask "why didn't it retry?".
- Prevention: any code that writes a terminal job/sync status outside `record_job_finish` MUST still run the retry plan (`_retry_plan` → set `attempt` + `next_retry_at`) for transient categories, or it forfeits self-healing. When adding a new terminal-transition site, ask "does this need to schedule a retry, and am I reusing the one place that knows how?" Prefer routing through the shared helper over re-implementing the UPDATE.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/ops_monitor.py::reap_orphaned_job_runs` (now stamps attempt + next_retry_at via `_retry_plan`); `tests/test_ops_monitor.py::TestReapOrphanedJobRuns::test_reaped_row_gets_auto_retry_scheduled`.

### An empty-string fetch body ("") is NOT None — a `if x is None` guard before `store_raw` lets it through and crashes the whole tick

- First seen in: hotfix (eBay insider sales 3 months stale). `sec_insider_transactions_backfill` failed EVERY run (48× in 48h) with `payload is required (empty payload would defeat re-wash / leave no hash)` from `raw_filings.store_raw`. `_process_candidates` guarded the fetched Form 4 body with `if xml is None:` (tombstone+skip), but `fetcher.fetch_document_text` returns `""` for an HTTP 200 with an empty body — `"" is not None` → it slipped past the guard into `store_raw`, which rejects any falsy payload → uncaught `ValueError` failed the ENTIRE backfill tick. The same poison accession was re-selected every run → all Form 4 ingest wedged. (Form 3 ingest had the identical latent bug; the manifest parser `insider_345.py` already used `if not xml` and was unaffected.)
- Symptom: a fetch-then-`store_raw` ingester that distinguishes "missing" with `is None` while the downstream store rejects `not payload`. The two guards disagree on the empty-string case, and because the store raises (not returns), one poison row kills the whole batch — invisible until a real 200-empty-body lands, then permanent.
- Prevention: guard fetched bodies with `if not xml:` (or `not body`), never `is None`, anywhere the body then flows into `store_raw`/`store_raw_filing` (which both `raise` on a falsy payload). The guard's truthiness MUST match the store's `not payload` check. Other ingesters with the same fetch→store shape and an `is None` guard are latent (`business_summary.py`, `blockholders.py`, `def14a_ingest.py`, `dividend_calendar.py`) — sweep them when touched. Per-row isolation is the deeper fix: a single poison candidate should tombstone itself, never fail the tick.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/insider_transactions.py::_process_candidates` + `app/services/insider_form3_ingest.py` (both now `if not xml`); `tests/test_insider_transactions_ingest.py::test_empty_body_200_tombstones_instead_of_crashing_the_tick`.

### Reconstructing a per-period series from SEC duration facts: the dominant issuer omits the standalone Q4 — residual-fill it, don't drop the overlapping annual
- First seen in: PR (#1635 SEC total return). To reinvest dividends we rebuild a per-period DPS stream from `financial_facts_raw` (us-gaap per-share dividend/distribution duration facts). The naive "keep the finest tier, reject any coarser fact that overlaps" tiling looked right on a sample but a full-pop scan falsified it: the DOMINANT US pattern is 3 quarterly 10-Q facts + 1 annual 10-K fact with NO standalone Q4 (Apple, KO, JPM, HD, MSFT all show 9 quarterly + 3 annual per 3y). Rejecting the overlapping annual drops Q4 entirely → 9/12 coverage → the whole stream wrongly reads "incomplete" and Q4's cash is silently lost from total return.
- Symptom: a per-period reconstruction that prefers fine periods and discards overlapping coarse periods undercounts every issuer that reports YTD-cumulative + annual instead of a discrete final period. The undercount is invisible on quarterly-only payers (the easy sample) and only bites the majority pattern.
- Prevention: use RESIDUAL tier-fill — accept fine periods, then for each coarser fact add only `coarse − Σ(finer periods WHOLLY CONTAINED in its span)` (= the implied Q4, the documented SEC FY=YTD+Q4 treatment, #682). Guard the residual with an epsilon (rounding/restatement noise) and require full containment (a finer period that only straddles the boundary must NOT reduce the residual). Two corollaries that bit in the same PR: (a) a coverage/quality GATE must count what the computation actually consumes — if the gate counts periods the index never reinvested (e.g. dividends before the first priced day), gate and result disagree; align both on the same effective lower bound. (b) When summing per-period values onto a compounding index, group same-date entries and apply ONCE — sequential application adds a spurious cross-product (a residual can land on the same ex-date as a fine period).
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/risk_metrics.py::select_dividend_stream` (residual fill + full-containment) + `total_return_index` (same-date summing) + `compute_instrument_risk` (gate/reinvest lower-bound alignment); metrics-analyst skill; `tests/test_risk_metrics_total_return.py`.

### A summary VIEW that only rows matching entities encodes its answer in row ABSENCE — read absence, don't default it to "unknown"
- First seen in: PR (#1635). `instrument_dividend_summary` (sql/050) is a VIEW whose `FROM` requires a positive dividend ever (`latest` CTE filters `dps_declared > 0 OR dividends_paid > 0`). So a missing row is not "unknown classification" — it is a CONFIRMED never-payer. A review flagged "missing summary → treat as unknown → tr_incomplete", which (combined with the fact that the view covers only ~1,900 of ~5,200 scoped instruments) flooded 3,247 genuine non-payers into `tr_incomplete` and nearly emptied the trustworthy `no_dividends` class.
- Symptom: defaulting a missing view row to a conservative "unknown" state without reading the view's own membership predicate mislabels the majority and starves the very state the feature relies on.
- Prevention: before treating a missing row as "unknown", read the view/table's membership rule. If membership encodes a fact (here: "has ever paid"), absence IS that fact's negation — use it. Keep an explicit override for the genuinely-ambiguous case (here: the flag is `True` but our independent fact stream is EMPTY → a real payer we failed to ingest → don't claim "exact").
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/risk_metrics.py::tr_status` (`tr_incomplete if has_dividend is True else no_dividends` for an empty stream); data-engineer skill FAQ.

### A capacity/throughput premise must name WHICH constraint binds — verify on the full population before designing the fix
- First seen in: #1686 (manifest backlog drain). The issue stated "~1.46M pending at the shared 10 req/s will take a very long time" — pinning the rate ceiling as the bottleneck. A full-population check falsified it: each SEC fetch is latency-bound (~700-900ms ≈ 1 req/s serial, `concurrent_fetch.py:4-6`), so the serial worker used <10% of the 10 req/s budget. The real constraints were the `max_rows=100`/tick cap + 5-min cadence + serial fetch — AND ~43% of the backlog was pre-retention rows the parser tombstones with NO HTTP at all, drained 74-at-a-time through full per-row dispatch.
- Symptom: a fix aimed at the named constraint (e.g. "raise the rate limit" / "more rate budget") would have moved nothing, because a different constraint (per-tick cap, serial latency, free-work-in-the-hot-path) was actually binding.
- Prevention: before speccing a throughput fix, MEASURE which constraint binds on the full population, not a sample — rate ceiling vs per-tick batch cap vs serial-latency vs cadence vs free-work-crowding-out-paid-work. Compute the achieved-vs-budget utilisation (here: 0.087 req/s vs 10) and split the work-list by cost class (no-HTTP tombstone vs real fetch). The cheapest, highest-leverage lever is usually pulling free work OUT of the rate-limited hot path (a bulk SQL transition), not enlarging the budget.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `docs/specs/ingest/2026-06-20-manifest-drain-capacity.md` (Problem section carries the falsification + utilisation math).

### A prefetch/optimization hook that mirrors a gated path must mirror EVERY pre-gate, or it wastes the very budget it was added to save
- First seen in: #1686 Codex ckpt-2 (HIGH). The Phase-2 concurrent prefetch hook `_insider_fetch_url` returned a URL whenever `primary_document_url` was present — but the Form 3/4/5 parsers tombstone BEFORE fetch on missing `instrument_id`, missing `filed_at`, and past-retention (`form4_within_retention` 3y / `form5_within_retention` 18mo). So the prefetch would hit SEC for rows the parser then discards, burning the rate budget the prefetch existed to conserve.
- Symptom: an "optimization" layer that gates on a SUBSET of the real path's pre-conditions does redundant expensive work (HTTP, compute) for inputs the real path rejects — net-negative under a shared budget.
- Prevention: when a prefetch/cache/batch hook front-runs a gated consumer, it MUST replicate ALL of the consumer's pre-work gates (reusing the SAME predicate functions, never a re-derived copy). Returning "skip" too often is safe (cache miss → consumer runs normally); fetching too eagerly is the leak. Add a test that the hook returns None for each pre-gate the consumer enforces.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/manifest_parsers/insider_345.py::_insider_fetch_url` (mirrors instrument_id/filed_at + form4/form5 retention via the parsers' own predicates); `tests/test_manifest_worker_prefetch.py::test_insider_fetch_url_mirrors_prefetch_gates`.
- Multi-stage corollary (#1700): a multi-doc prefetch (13F = index.json → primary_doc.xml) must mirror the consumer's gate that sits BETWEEN the stages too. The 13F parser tombstones when EITHER `primary_name` OR `infotable_name` is absent from the index (before fetching the primary), and gates `infotable.xml` on a POST-primary-parse retention check — so the pass-2 expander returns the primary ONLY when BOTH names are present, and never prefetches the infotable (it would fetch large docs for rows the parser discards). A per-stage gate is still a pre-fetch gate for the NEXT stage's URL.

### A best-effort prefetch/optimization hook must isolate its own failures (savepoint + catch) so one bad row can't abort the whole batch
- First seen in: #1700 Codex ckpt-2 (P2 then P1, two rounds). The Phase-2 prefetch added per-row hooks (`fetch_url`, `expand_urls`) that run on the shared worker connection BEFORE the per-row `_dispatch_rows` loop. Two failure-isolation gaps: (a) a hook raising (malformed `index.json` → `parse_archive_index` raises; DEF 14A's `def14a_within_cap` DB read errors) escaped the prefetch and aborted the ENTIRE fairness tick — converting one bad accession from a per-row `failed` into zero manifest transitions for every selected row; (b) catching the exception alone was not enough — a DB-error raise left the non-autocommit `connect_job()` connection in `InFailedSqlTransaction`, so every later hook read AND the serial dispatch failed anyway.
- Symptom: an optimization layer that front-runs a per-item error-handled loop, but is itself outside that loop's try/except, turns a single-item failure into a whole-batch abort; and a swallowed DB error silently poisons a shared transaction for all downstream work.
- Prevention: a best-effort pre-pass over a shared connection must (1) wrap each item's hook in try/except and SKIP on failure (the downstream per-item path still runs and applies its own error handling — never let prefetch be load-bearing), AND (2) run any DB-touching hook inside a SAVEPOINT (`with conn.transaction():`) so a raised DB error rolls back to the savepoint and leaves the outer transaction usable. Catch-without-rollback on a non-autocommit shared conn is a latent `InFailedSqlTransaction` bug. Self-review prompt: for any new pre-dispatch/pre-commit hook on a shared conn, ask "if this raises, does the per-item loop still run?" and "if this raises a DB error, is the conn still usable?".
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/jobs/sec_manifest_worker.py::_prefetch_bodies` (savepoint-wrapped `fetch_url`, try/except-skip on both hooks); `tests/test_manifest_worker_prefetch.py::test_prefetch_fetch_url_raise_skips_row_not_tick` + `::test_prefetch_expand_raise_skips_pass2_not_tick`.

### A chokepoint-lint guard that counts a GENERIC symbol is defeated by a second legitimate call site — pin the exact call form
- First seen in: #1686. `check_form4_retention.sh` asserted `_parse_form4` keeps its retention gate by counting `form4_within_retention(` call-sites `>= 1` in `insider_345.py`. The new prefetch hook added a SECOND legitimate `form4_within_retention(filed)` call, so stripping the PARSER's gate would still leave count `>= 1` → the guard would pass while the enforced chokepoint was gone (the hook only gates prefetch, not the parser's serial fetch).
- Symptom: a count-based invariant guard silently weakens the moment a second valid caller of the counted symbol appears — it can no longer distinguish "the enforced gate" from "any mention".
- Prevention: pin chokepoint guards to the ENFORCING call's exact form (here `form4_within_retention(filed_at.date())` via `count_literal`), not the bare symbol name, so an unrelated legitimate second call site cannot mask removal of the real gate. When you add a second call site of a lint-guarded symbol, re-read the guard and re-confirm it still catches the original violation.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/check_form4_retention.sh` (manifest-gate check counts the parser's exact gate literal); `tests/test_check_form4_retention_lint.py::test_guard_fails_when_manifest_gate_stripped`.

### An exclusion that treats two CAUSALLY-ORDERED attributes as independent leaves a provably-safe optimization unbuilt — find the source rule that fixes the ordering, verify on the full population
- First seen in: #1703 (extends #1686). The #1686 pre-retention bulk-tombstone sweep EXCLUDED `sec_13f_hr` reasoning *"a `filed_at` sweep would wrongly tombstone in-scope filings whose period is recent"* — i.e. it treated `filed_at` and `period_of_report` as independent. They are causally ordered: a 13F is filed AFTER the quarter it reports (SEC Rule 13f-1(a) — within 45 days of quarter end), so `filed_at >= period_of_report` ALWAYS. Therefore `filed_at < retention_cutoff` ⟹ `period_of_report < cutoff` ⟹ out-of-retention, with ZERO false tombstones. The over-conservative exclusion left ~121k out-of-retention 13F rows (≈96% of dispatched 13F/tick) being fetched (index + primary) only to tombstone post-parse — the actual per-tick wall. (It also falsified #1703's own stated premise: the infotable is fetched for only ~2 in-retention rows/tick, not the wall.)
- Symptom: a sweep/gate/filter excludes a source citing "attribute X can't safely proxy attribute Y", leaving expensive work in a rate-limited hot path that a one-line SQL pass could remove — when X in fact bounds Y by a documented source rule.
- Prevention: when an exclusion claims attribute X can't proxy attribute Y, ask whether X and Y are CAUSALLY ORDERED (file-after-period, accept-after-submit, settle-after-trade). Find the SOURCE RULE that fixes the ordering (here SEC Rule 13f-1(a)), then VERIFY it on the FULL population before relying on it (0 / 62,374 filings had `filed_at < period_of_report`; min gap 0d, p50 42d = the 45-day window). A provably-safe SUBSET of a stricter gate is still safe; use the stricter gate's own cutoff as the bound (the maximum-safe value) and reuse its cutoff function (single source of truth). Distinguish from the INVERSE hazard (#1639, I15): comparing TWO filings' periods across amendments via filed-time IS wrong (a late-filed amendment for an old period); bounding ONE filing's own period via its own `filed_at` is sound.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/manifest_pre_retention_sweep.py::gated_cutoffs` (`sec_13f_hr` via `thirteen_f_retention_cutoff`, docstring carries the proof + the #1639 distinction) + `docs/specs/ingest/2026-06-21-manifest-13f-pre-retention-sweep.md`; `tests/test_manifest_pre_retention_sweep.py::test_13f_filed_cutoff_is_max_safe_and_boundary`.

### Dev-verify a throughput-config change on the NORMAL daemon cadence — never a barrage of heavy ad-hoc worker ticks
- First seen in: #1703 PR2 (2026-06-21) + this PR. Tuning the manifest worker's `max_rows` tempts you to "just run a few worker ticks against dev and time them". Two self-inflicted failures resulted: (a) running `run_manifest_worker` manually WHILE the daemon was still up → the graceful-stop's heavy in-flight tick was still running, two ticks picked the same row → illegal `parsed->parsed` transition crash; (b) firing several back-to-back heavy `max_rows=150/200` ad-hoc ticks (plus probes) ON TOP of the live daemon → over-drove SEC's process-global 10 req/s shared rate gate (`_PROCESS_RATE_LIMIT_LOCK`/`_CLOCK`, `sec_edgar.py:58-85`) into a multi-thousand-hit 429 storm (the daemon self-recovers per #1698, but the timing was invalidated AND the burst was abusive).
- Symptom: throughput timings collected by hammering dev with concurrent/back-to-back heavy ticks are both wrong (contention + rate-gate stalls inflate them) and harmful (429 storm against the shared SEC budget; row-state races against the live daemon).
- Prevention: dev-verify a throughput-config change by running it on the **normal daemon cadence** (one tick / 5 min, bounded 10 req/s) — restart the daemon onto the fixed code and READ the tick log over time. If you must force a heavy regime for a single point-measurement, do it with the daemon **STOPPED + confirmed-down** (READ the down-confirmation — "no app.jobs process" + "singleton fence released" — BEFORE launching; a prior session's bg waiter returned without confirming and it launched onto a still-running daemon) and a **SINGLE** tick, never a barrage. Corollary from #1700: do NOT merge a throughput VALUE before a full-day verify at cadence — the #1700 premature-merge cost 3 PRs.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; the manifest-worker raise procedure in `docs/proposals/etl/1703-manifest-max-rows-raise.md` (Rollout/dev-verify + Process lesson sections).

### A per-job override that fixes a CADENCE-CLASS problem must be DERIVED from the cadence, not a hardcoded name list — and you must audit the whole population when you add it
- First seen in: #1707 → #1710. #1707 fixed `orchestrator_full_sync` being starved (its ~1.75s lane-acquire window expired under `orchestrator_high_frequency_sync`'s 2-5s gate hold → skipped to next DAY, 7/7) by giving it a patient ~11.5s backoff via `_LANE_BACKOFF_OVERRIDES = {orchestrator_full_sync: ...}`. The code comment even stated the GENERAL rule ("a job's acquire patience should scale with its cadence — daily jobs wait"), but the implementation hardcoded the single named job from the operator complaint. Every OTHER daily job on the 5-min grid kept the default and was silently starved by the identical mechanism: `raw_data_retention_sweep` (02:00) + `fundamentals_sync` (02:30) skipped 2+ days (last success 06-21, observed 06-23) before an operator noticed the admin panel still showed "schedule missed".
- Symptom: a fix for a problem that is a property of a CLASS (here "daily cadence on a contended lane") is wired to one member of the class via a hand-maintained name map; the map is the operator's bug report, not the invariant. New members of the class inherit the bug, and the original "fix" looks complete because the one named job is green.
- Prevention: when the root cause is a cadence/role/type CLASS property, DERIVE the behaviour from that property (here `cadence.kind ∈ {daily,weekly,monthly,yearly}` via the `SCHEDULED_JOBS` registry — `_lane_backoff_for(job_name)`), never a literal name set that a future job silently misses. Then AUDIT the full population the moment you add it (prevention-log §"cross-cutting gate": `SELECT job_name, status, COUNT(*) FROM job_runs WHERE started_at > NOW() - INTERVAL '2 days' GROUP BY 1,2` reveals silent-skip patterns immediately; here a `/system/jobs` verdict scan showed all 3 starved daily jobs at once). A registry-driven test (`for job in SCHEDULED_JOBS: assert _lane_backoff_for(job.name) is patient iff coarse cadence`) keeps the derived set pinned to the schedule forever. Same family as "question the MODEL not the case" (CLAUDE.md) and the #1357 cross-cutting-gate audit rule.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/jobs/runtime.py::_lane_backoff_for` + `_PATIENT_BACKOFF_CADENCE_KINDS` (cadence-derived, replaces the hardcoded `_LANE_BACKOFF_OVERRIDES`); `tests/test_lane_busy_retry.py::test_every_daily_or_coarser_job_gets_patient_backoff` (full-population registry guard + explicit pins for the two jobs #1707 missed).

### Never fabricate audit attribution with a `?? "operator"` / `?? "system"` fallback — block or raise when identity is absent
- First seen in: #1231 (frontend kill-switch toggle, PR #1716 review WARNING). The toggle sent `activated_by: operator?.username ?? "operator"`. The backend `KillSwitchRequest` validator only checks the field is non-empty, so the literal `"operator"` passes — silently writing a FALSE `runtime_config_audit` row that names no real person. The PR even claimed "attribution can't be spoofed from the form" as a security property; the fallback broke exactly that guarantee.
- Symptom: a field that feeds an audit/attribution column (`activated_by`, `deactivated_by`, `decision_by`, `updated_by`, `created_by`) is defaulted with `?? "<role-literal>"` so it always satisfies a non-empty server check — turning "we don't know who did this" into a confident, wrong audit record.
- Prevention: audit attribution MUST resolve to a real identity or the action MUST be blocked/raised — never defaulted to a role literal. In UI: gate `canSubmit` on `identity !== null`, early-return in the handler, and show a fixed-phrase blocker ("No authenticated operator — cannot attribute this action"). In services: raise rather than substitute. Grep `?? "operator"` / `?? "system"` / `?? "unknown"` and for each ask "does this value land in an audit/attribution column?" — if yes, replace the fallback with a guard. (NB: a HARDCODED literal for a non-audit config field — e.g. `updated_by: "operator"` on a display-currency PATCH — is a separate, lower-stakes pattern; this rule is about FALLBACKS that mask a missing identity on an audited safety action.)
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/frontend/safety-state-ui.md` (attribution rule); `frontend/src/components/admin/KillSwitchSection.tsx` (`operatorName = operator?.username ?? null`, `canSubmit` gates on it, blocker alert) + `KillSwitchSection.test.tsx::"blocks the transition (no fabricated attribution) when no operator"`.

### Parallelising per-entity pipelines deadlocks on SHARED ON CONFLICT indexes — order the hot shared write + retry the victim
- First seen in: #1274 (2026-06-25 dev-verify). Fanning the serial 13F filer sweep across an 8-worker pool (each filer its own conn) deadlocked **38/60 filers** at concurrency=8. Root cause: concurrent `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` on a **shared** unique index. The per-filer writes that looked "independent" mostly were — `institutional_holdings` / ingest-log are accession-keyed (one accession = one filer) and `ownership_*_observations` is filer-keyed, all disjoint — but `unresolved_13f_cusips (cusip)` is a hot SHARED table (many institutions report the same unresolved CUSIPs; `observation_count + 1` on the same rows; 88k upserts in one slice). Two worker transactions inserting overlapping CUSIPs in **opposite order** form a lock cycle (`ShareLock on transaction … blocked by …`, "while inserting index tuple") → Postgres aborts one as the deadlock victim. The existing per-accession `pg_advisory_xact_lock` (`acquire_13f_accession_write_lock`) is filer-disjoint and does NOT cover the shared-cusip path.
- Symptom: a serial per-entity (filer/issuer/instrument) ingest is parallelised and starts logging `deadlock detected … while inserting index tuple`; failures are isolated per-entity (good) but the crash RATE is high (here 63%), and an "idempotent re-run" writes non-zero rows because the first run never finished the deadlocked entities (masquerades as non-idempotency — it isn't).
- Prevention: before parallelising per-entity transactions, enumerate every write and classify each table as **disjoint** (key includes the entity id → safe) or **shared** (key omits the entity → contended). For each shared `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT` / multi-row upsert, (1) acquire its row locks in a **deterministic global order** — sort the batch by the conflict key so all transactions lock shared rows in the same order and no cycle can form (a hot counter like `observation_count+1` is the tell); and (2) add a bounded **deadlock/serialization retry** around the whole entity transaction (`psycopg.errors.DeadlockDetected` / `SerializationFailure` → rollback + retry on a fresh conn; the contender has committed by then) as the safety net for residual cross-table contention. Ordering makes deadlocks rare; retry makes them harmless. A per-accession/per-entity advisory lock does NOT substitute — it only serialises within one entity, not across the shared rows two entities both touch. Verify on the FULL concurrent path (dev-verify at the real concurrency), not a 2-worker unit test — the contention is load-dependent.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/institutional_holdings.py::_ingest_single_accession` (unresolved CUSIPs upserted `sorted(... key=cusip)`); `app/services/sec_filer_concurrency.py::make_filer_runner` (`_RETRYABLE_TX_ERRORS` retry loop, `DEFAULT_MAX_DEADLOCK_RETRIES`); `tests/test_sec_filer_concurrency.py::test_make_filer_runner_retries_deadlock_then_succeeds` + `…_exhausts_deadlock_retries_returns_crashed`; `docs/specs/etl/2026-06-25-parallel-filer-ingest.md` §"Shared-write deadlock avoidance".

### A lint/scan gate whose target glob is non-recursive silently excludes whole subtrees — couple scope to the file tree (find), not a top-level glob
- First seen in: #1801 (2026-06-28). `scripts/check_shellcheck.sh` (#1257) gated `scripts/*.sh` and its own docstring claimed it covered "the whole shell-script tree" — but `*.sh` is non-recursive, so `scripts/autonomy/*.sh` and `scripts/perf_bench/*.sh` were never linted. A real shell bug then shipped in `scripts/autonomy/supervisor.sh` (the preflight wedge #1801 fixed) in a script the gate believed it covered. The `cd "$REPO"` SC2164 warning sat unflagged in supervisor.sh the whole time.
- Symptom: a chokepoint gate (shellcheck / a grep-based invariant / a format check) passes green and its comment says "whole tree", but a glob like `dir/*.ext` / `dir/*.py` only matches the top level; files in subdirectories are silently out of scope and accumulate exactly the defects the gate exists to catch.
- Prevention: scope a tree-wide gate by ENUMERATING the tree, not by a single-level glob. Use `find <root> -name '*.ext' -type f` (bash-3.2-portable on macOS — no `mapfile`/`globstar`/`**`: collect with `while IFS= read -r f; do arr+=("$f"); done < <(find …)`), and make the gate's echo/docstring state the actual recursive scope. When you touch any gate, sanity-check its target list against `find` to confirm the glob isn't dropping a subtree. Same family as the #1707 "derive scope from the property, not a hand-maintained list" rule.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/check_shellcheck.sh` (recursive `find scripts -name '*.sh'`); `tests/test_check_shellcheck_lint.py` (docstring + clean-tree test now exercise subdir scripts).

### A new non-executable recommendation/action value must get a status OUTSIDE every execution selector's WHERE filter
- First seen in: #1820 (2026-06-29). The portfolio review previously discarded blocked unheld BUY candidates (`portfolio.py:1122` appended only `if should_buy`). The fix persists them as a new `CONSIDERED` action so the operator sees the universe was evaluated. The execution pipeline selects work purely by status — Phase 0 timing + Phase 1 guard both `WHERE status = 'proposed'` (`scheduler.py:3441/3596`), the work-signal is `status IN ('proposed','approved')` (`scheduler.py:589`), and `_load_recommendation`/`_write_audit` flip status to approved/rejected with NO action gate (`execution_guard.py`). Inserting CONSIDERED with the default `status='proposed'` would have routed an informational row straight into timing → guard → (a stray manual call could) execution.
- Symptom: a new recommendation action/state is added for display/evidence only, but it inherits the default executable status, so an execution selector that filters on status (not action) silently picks it up.
- Prevention: when adding a recommendation action that must NOT be executed, give it a dedicated status that no execution selector matches (here `status='considered'`, derived in `_insert_recommendation` from `action`), and add a defense-in-depth guard at the executor entry (`evaluate_recommendation` raises on `action not in EXECUTABLE_ACTIONS`). Enumerate every `WHERE status = …` / `status IN (…)` consumer (grep `FROM trade_recommendations`) and confirm the new row is invisible to all of them before merge. Same family as "missing data on a hard-rule path silently passes".
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/portfolio.py` (`_insert_recommendation` status-by-action); `app/services/execution_guard.py` (`EXECUTABLE_ACTIONS` + raise); `.claude/skills/ranking-engine/SKILL.md`.

### Rebuild a CHECK constraint from the LIVE `pg_get_constraintdef`, not the last migration's source — code may already write values the constraint forbids
- First seen in: #1820 (2026-06-29). Extending `chk_recommendation_status` to add `'considered'`, the spec first said "match sql/028's value list". Codex + a `pg_get_constraintdef` query on dev showed the live constraint was sql/028's 8 values — but `order_client.py:1115` writes `'execution_pending'` on a broker-pending live order, a value ABSENT from that list. Re-adding the constraint from the migration source alone would have re-baked the latent gap (any pending live order would 23514). The migration was rebuilt from the live vocabulary + the code-written-but-missing value + the new one.
- Symptom: a migration drops and re-adds a CHECK using the value set copied from the last migration that defined it, omitting values that other code paths legitimately write (because those paths never fired on the constrained env), so the constraint silently forbids a real production state.
- Prevention: before DROP+ADD of a CHECK, capture the LIVE definition (`SELECT pg_get_constraintdef(oid) FROM pg_constraint WHERE conname=…`) AND grep every writer of the column (`grep -rn "status = '" / status writes`) — the new constraint's value set = (live values) ∪ (all code-written values) ∪ (the new one). VALIDATE in a separate statement so any out-of-vocab legacy row surfaces as a clean error. Same family as "grep CREATE + ALTER for the full constraint picture".
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `sql/209_considered_and_completeness.sql` (full live vocabulary + `execution_pending` gap-fix + `considered`).

### A ratio `end/start - 1` over two date-resolved closes returns a FAKE 0% when both legs resolve to the same row — gate on coverage (a date), not the value
- First seen in: #1817 (2026-06-28, FE-QA on `/reports`). `reporting.py::_benchmark_closes` computed the benchmark period return as `close_end / close_start - 1`, where `close_start` = latest close STRICTLY BEFORE `period_start` and `close_end` = latest close AT-OR-BEFORE `period_end`. When the benchmark has **no close inside `[period_start, period_end]`** (stale data — SPX500 stuck at 2026-06-04 / exactly 1000 rows, #1818), `close_end` collapses onto the SAME pre-period row as `close_start`, so the ratio is exactly `0`. The report then rendered a confident **"S&P 500 0.00%" + a bogus excess** for a window the benchmark has no data for — contradicting the helper's own docstring ("null when closes are missing → FE portfolio-only notice"). The missing-data case `close_end.date < period_start` slipped through as a real zero. This is the SAME class the codebase already names `benchmark_missing` for risk metrics (settled-decisions §risk-metrics) — "no data" must never be shown as a computed zero.
- Symptom: a rate/return/delta computed as `f(end) - f(start)` (or `end/start - 1`) where each side is resolved by a "latest row ≤ date" / "latest row < date" query silently yields `0` (or "no change") when the data is stale or sparse enough that both queries land on the **same row**. The 0 is indistinguishable on screen from a real flat period, and any derived figure (excess, alpha, variance) inherits the fabrication.
- Prevention: a computed change is only honest when the data actually COVERS the window. Resolve each endpoint WITH its key (the `price_date`, the snapshot id), and gate the computation on coverage — e.g. require the end row's date `>= period_start` (a close actually fell inside the span) before computing; otherwise return null so the UI shows "—" / "unavailable", never `0`. Distinguish "no data" from "real zero" the same way the risk layer does with `benchmark_missing`. When two SQL lookups feed a difference, ask "can both resolve to the same row?" — if yes, that path needs an explicit coverage guard. Verify on a window the data does NOT cover (stale/sparse fixture), not just a healthy one.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/sql-correctness.md` ("two date-resolved lookups can collapse to one row"); `app/services/reporting.py::_benchmark_closes` (returns `price_date`; `return_pct` null unless `end_row.date >= period_start`); `tests/test_reporting_v2_db.py::test_benchmark_stale_no_in_window_close_returns_null` + `…_close_exactly_on_period_start_computes`.

### A value ALREADY in percent units, formatted by a uniform "×100 = percent" renderer, is double-scaled 100×
- First seen in: #1827 (2026-06-29, FE-QA on `/instrument/AAPL`). The instrument Key-stats pane rendered **Dividend yield 27.45%** for AAPL (true ≈0.27%) and **133.94%** for JPM (true ≈1.34%). `key_stats.dividend_yield` is `instrument_dividend_summary.ttm_yield_pct = (ttm_dps / price) * 100` — **already a percent** (the `*100` is in the SQL view, `sql/050:170`), surfaced verbatim (`app/api/instruments.py:3762`). But its sibling stats `roe`/`roa`/`revenue_growth_yoy` are raw **fractions** (0.277), and `KeyStatsPane.tsx` formatted the whole block with one `{percent:true}` policy that does `num * 100`. So the already-percent yield got ×100 a second time → 100× overstatement. The SAME field rendered correctly elsewhere (`dividendsShared.formatYieldPct`, treats it as already-percent) — two renderers silently disagreed on the field's unit.
- Symptom: a percentage/ratio shows up ~100× too large (or a fraction shows ~100× too small) on one surface but correct on another. The tell is a mixed-unit payload: some fields are fractions (0.27), one or two are pre-multiplied percents (27.0), and a formatter assumes a single convention for all of them.
- Prevention: when a payload mixes unit conventions, the unit is a property of the FIELD, not the block — format each field by its own documented unit, don't apply one `×100` policy across a heterogeneous stats block. Before formatting a "percent-looking" field, confirm at the source whether the `*100` is already baked in (grep the view/SQL + the producing service). When two renderers display the same field, they must agree on its unit — cross-check against the canonical renderer (here `formatYieldPct`). A cross-instrument sanity check catches it instantly: a 27% or 134% "dividend yield" is absurd on its face (verify on the FULL set of payers, not one). Document the unit at the metric's source-of-truth entry (`metrics-analyst/SKILL.md`).
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/metrics-analyst/SKILL.md` (Dividend yield UNIT caveat); `frontend/src/components/instrument/KeyStatsPane.tsx` (`formatDecimal` `alreadyPercent` opt + comment); `frontend/src/components/instrument/KeyStatsPane.test.tsx` (already-percent regression test).

### A read-path symbol must be guaranteed fresh by an ingest scope — a symbol a read path resolves but no refresh job maintains silently freezes
- First seen in: #1818 (2026-06-29). `/reports` compares the portfolio against `reporting.py::BENCHMARK_SYMBOL = "SPX500"`, resolved by symbol at generation time. SPX500 (eToro's S&P 500 price-index instrument, id 27) is tier-3 and never held, and was **absent from `scheduler.py::BENCHMARK_SYMBOLS`** — the one scope that candle-refreshes benchmark series regardless of coverage tier. So no refresh path covered it: its closes froze at 2026-06-04 (exactly 1000 rows — one stale fetch, never re-fetched), and the report rendered "benchmark unavailable" (the #1817 null-guard firing correctly on dead data). The risk layer's own benchmark (SPY) sat in `BENCHMARK_SYMBOLS` and stayed current — only the reporting benchmark diverged. The spec's "closes observed 2022-07→present" was a dev-data observation explicitly NOT a maintained invariant; nothing made it one.
- Symptom: a read path resolves data by a symbol / id / key, and that key is NOT in any ingest/refresh scope, so its data is whatever a one-time historical load left behind — frozen at some past date. The freeze is invisible until a downstream honesty-guard (null-on-stale) or an operator notices the "unavailable" notice. Tell: a constant naming a symbol/entity in a read module (`BENCHMARK_SYMBOL`, a hardcoded ticker, a benchmark id) with no corresponding entry in the job that keeps that class of data fresh.
- Prevention: when a read path pins a specific symbol/entity, that symbol must appear in (or be derivable from) the ingest scope that maintains its data — and a test should pin the cross-reference (`assert BENCHMARK_SYMBOL in BENCHMARK_SYMBOLS`). Two constants that must agree (a read-path key + an ingest-scope set) are a divergence waiting to happen; bind them with an invariant test, not a comment. When you add a refresh-scope set "for purpose X", grep every read path for OTHER symbols of the same class that also need freshness. Don't assume "the data is there in dev" means a job keeps it there.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/workers/scheduler.py::BENCHMARK_SYMBOLS` (SPX500 added + why-comment); `tests/test_daily_candle_refresh.py::test_reporting_benchmark_symbol_is_always_refreshed` (binds `reporting.BENCHMARK_SYMBOL` ⊆ `BENCHMARK_SYMBOLS`).

### Adding a lightweight-charts API call (new `timeScale()`/series method) breaks that chart's per-test `vi.mock` — and `test:unit` won't catch it
- First seen in: #1841 (2026-06-29). Wiring `chart.timeScale().applyOptions({ tickMarkFormatter })` into `PortfolioValueChart` (so the dashboard chart uses the app's adaptive month/day tick labels instead of lightweight-charts' default, which rendered March as a bare "2"). Local `pnpm --dir frontend typecheck` + `test:unit` both passed, so the branch was pushed — and CI's full `test` job failed with `TypeError: chart.timeScale(...).applyOptions is not a function`. The chart's own test file hand-rolls a `vi.mock("lightweight-charts")` whose `timeScale: vi.fn(() => ({ fitContent }))` had no `applyOptions` (the real API has it), and the `ValueCanvas` render test that exercises it lives in the **full** tier, which `test:unit` excludes (per CLAUDE.md test-tiering). So the gate that would have caught it never ran locally.
- Symptom: an FE change that calls a charting-lib method the component didn't call before passes typecheck + `test:unit` locally but fails CI `build` (full `pnpm --dir frontend test`) with `X is not a function` from the per-test mock — the mock implements only the subset of the lib API the component used at mock-authoring time.
- Prevention: when a chart component starts calling a NEW lightweight-charts method (`timeScale().applyOptions`, a new series method, a new chart option accessor), update that chart's `vi.mock` to expose the method (mirror the canonical mock — `PriceChart.test.tsx` exposes `timeScale: vi.fn(() => ({ ..., applyOptions }))`), AND run the FULL `pnpm --dir frontend test` locally, not just `test:unit` — chart integration tests (`ValueCanvas`, `ChartPage`, render harnesses) are excluded from `test:unit` and are the only ones that execute the mock. Treat "I touched a chart's lib-API surface" as the trigger to run the full FE tier before pushing.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `frontend/src/components/dashboard/PortfolioValueChart.test.tsx` (timeScale mock gains `applyOptions`); `.claude/skills/frontend/async-data-loading.md`.

### A contained-scroll viewport must be `position:relative` — an absolutely-positioned descendant (e.g. an `sr-only` label) with no positioned ancestor escapes the `overflow` clip and extends document scroll
- First seen in: #1858 (2026-06-29, FE-QA on `/rankings`, large display). The page scrolled ~1207px past its pinned pagination footer into dead space. Static read looked correct: `RankingsPage` root `flex h-full flex-col`, `Section scrollable` body `min-h-0 flex-1 overflow-auto`, `<main> flex-1 overflow-auto`, AppShell `h-screen overflow-hidden` — a clean contained-scroll chain. Live measurement (Playwright on the authed DOM) showed `document.documentElement.scrollHeight = 2242` but `document.body.scrollHeight = 1050` — the overflow was at the `<html>` level, not inside `<body>`. Cause: each of the ~50 `RankDeltaCell` rows renders a Tailwind `sr-only` span (`position:absolute`). NONE of its ancestors up to `<body>` was `position:relative`, so each span's containing block resolved to the initial containing block (the viewport). Absolutely-positioned elements are NOT clipped by an ancestor's `overflow` unless that ancestor is their containing block (i.e. positioned) — so the spans escaped every `overflow-auto`/`overflow-hidden` in the chain and sat at their static y-positions (1101→2242px, one per row down the 1932px-tall table), inflating `<html>` scrollHeight and creating the dead scroll.
- Symptom: a page with a correct-looking contained-scroll layout still has page-level dead scroll below a footer/viewport. `documentElement.scrollHeight` exceeds `innerHeight` while `body.scrollHeight` equals it (overflow is between `<html>` and `<body>`). The escaping content is some `position:absolute` element (an `sr-only` a11y label, a tooltip anchor, an absolutely-positioned badge) deep inside a tall list whose scroll container is `position:static`.
- Prevention: any contained-scroll viewport (`overflow-auto`/`overflow-hidden` claiming flex space) that may hold absolutely-positioned descendants MUST be `position:relative` so those descendants are scoped to it and clipped by its overflow. Fix the SCROLL PRIMITIVE, not the leaf cell (`Section`'s scrollable body is now `relative` — covers every consumer, current and future). `relative` with no insets is a zero-visual-change containment fix. Self-review prompt: "does this scroll container have a `position:absolute` descendant, and is the container itself positioned?" Verify on the LIVE authed DOM at a tall viewport — measure `window.scrollY` after `scrollTo(0, 99999)` (must be 0) and compare `documentElement.scrollHeight` vs `body.scrollHeight` (a gap means out-of-flow escape); jsdom can't catch this (no layout), so the unit test pins the CSS contract and the live measurement is the real proof. Same FE-QA gap class as #1817/#1827: "function verified, layout integrity / scroll-to-bottom not".
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `frontend/src/components/dashboard/Section.tsx` (scrollable body `relative` + why-comment); `frontend/src/components/dashboard/Section.test.tsx` (pins the relative-overflow-auto contract); `.claude/skills/frontend/operator-ui-conventions.md`.

### A dedup/idempotency scan over `gh` issue/PR comments must `--paginate` — the GraphQL `--json` form caps at ~100
- First seen in: #1866 (2026-06-30, PR #1870 bot WARNING). `unblock_dependents.sh`'s duplicate-notice guard scanned for a marker with `gh issue view "$D" --json comments -q '.comments[].body'`. That GraphQL form returns only the FIRST page (≤100 comments); on a busy ticket the prior marker could fall off the page, so a manual `safe_merge` re-run would double-post the unblock notice — defeating the exact guard meant to prevent it. Mirrors the earlier `safe_merge.sh` file-list trap (#1863): `gh pr view --json files` likewise caps at the first page, which is why the doc-only fast path reads the COMPLETE list via `gh api --paginate "repos/{owner}/{repo}/pulls/$PR/files"`.
- Symptom: an idempotency / "have I already done X to this issue/PR?" check reads comments (or files, or any list) via `gh {issue,pr} view --json <list>` and works in testing (small ticket) but can silently miss an earlier entry once the list exceeds ~100 — re-running the action then duplicates a side effect the guard exists to suppress.
- Prevention: any correctness-bearing scan over a `gh` comment/file/review list MUST read the complete set — use `gh api --paginate "repos/{owner}/{repo}/issues/$N/comments" --jq '.[].body'` (or the `pulls/$N/files` equivalent), not the `--json <list>` GraphQL accessor. Reserve `--json` for fields you only need the head of. A marker-based dedup whose correctness depends on the marker being IN the scanned window is only as good as the window — paginate or the guard is a coin-flip on long threads.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/autonomy/unblock_dependents.sh` (marker scan via `gh api --paginate .../issues/$D/comments`); precedent `scripts/autonomy/safe_merge.sh` (file list via `gh api --paginate .../pulls/$PR/files`).

### `set -e` in shell scripts is per-script-TYPE, not blanket — and when used, guard intentionally-nonzero commands
- First seen in: #1874 (2026-06-30, PR #1875 bot WARNING). `worktree_gc.sh` shipped with `set -uo pipefail` (no `-e`) while its sibling `setup_worktree.sh` used `set -euo pipefail`; the inconsistency meant a `git worktree prune` failure was swallowed and the script proceeded to branch-deletion as if prune had succeeded. The bot's PREVENTION ("grep new scripts; assert `set -` contains `e`") is right as a DEFAULT but wrong as a universal rule for this repo: `supervisor.sh` deliberately omits `-e` (a long-running loop where any stray nonzero would kill the whole supervisor), and the `test_*.sh` harnesses omit it (they inspect exit codes by hand). So the lesson is type-scoped, not blanket.
- Symptom: a new shell script either (a) omits `-e` where it should have it — a short fire-and-forget utility that silently continues past a failed step as if it succeeded; or (b) adds `-e` to a script whose loop legitimately runs commands that return nonzero (a refused `git branch -D` on a worktree-held branch, `grep` with no match, `((x++))` when x was 0), so `-e` aborts mid-run and skips the rest.
- Prevention: choose `set -e` by script type. **Short fire-and-forget utilities** (setup/gc/install scripts) → `set -euo pipefail`, abort loudly on any unhandled failure. **Long-running loops** (the supervisor) and **exit-code-testing harnesses** (`test_*.sh`) → deliberately omit `-e`; add a one-line comment saying why. When you DO use `-e`, wrap any command whose nonzero is expected/handled so it can't trip it: put it in an `if` condition (consumes the exit code), or append `|| true`/`|| continue`. Concretely for the gc loop: `if git branch -D "$b" >/dev/null 2>&1; then ...; fi` — the `if` keeps a refused delete from aborting the run. Prefer assignment-form arithmetic (`n=$((n+1))`, always exit 0) over `((n++))` (exit 1 when n was 0) under `-e`.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/autonomy/worktree_gc.sh` (`set -euo pipefail` + guarded per-branch `-D` in an `if`, why-comment); contrast `scripts/autonomy/supervisor.sh` (`set -uo pipefail`, loop must survive stray nonzero) and `scripts/autonomy/test_*.sh` (no `-e`, assert on exit codes).

### A mocked-module rename after a connection-helper migration leaves the mock patching a name nobody calls — real DB leaks through silently
- First seen in: #1887 (2026-07-02, filed as "ebull_test_template contaminated"). `tests/test_fundamentals_sync.py` and `tests/test_daily_research_refresh_dedupe.py` patched `scheduler.psycopg`/`scheduler.psycopg.connect` to fake every DB connection `fundamentals_sync()`/`daily_research_refresh()` open. Sometime after #1690 (per-job bounded `statement_timeout`) migrated every connection site in `app/workers/scheduler.py` from raw `psycopg.connect(settings.database_url)` to `connect_job()` (`app.jobs.job_connection`, imported by name into `scheduler`), these two test files were never updated. The mocks became dead code: `scheduler.psycopg.connect` is patched but never called, while the real `connect_job()` — which resolves `app.config.settings.database_url` via its OWN module-level import, entirely unaffected by `patch.object(scheduler, "settings", stub_settings)` — silently opened REAL connections to the operator's live dev Postgres (port 5432) on every `-m db` run of these files. The filed symptom ("eligible=341/342" mismatching a test's seeded 2-3 rows) was the real dev DB's actual coverage-eligible row count leaking into a supposedly-isolated unit test, not template contamination — `ebull_test_template` and the per-worker test DB were confirmed empty throughout.
- Symptom: a scheduler/job test patches a connection factory (`psycopg`, `psycopg.connect`, or any wrapper) via `patch.object(module, "name")`/`monkeypatch.setattr`, the test passes locally in isolation sometimes but produces assertion values that look like REAL production data (row counts, symbol counts, ids) rather than the small fixture the test seeded, and/or a `call_count`/`call_args` assertion on the mock reads `0` or unexpectedly low — the production code path stopped calling the patched name after a refactor and the mock is now inert.
- Prevention: when a helper migrates every call site from a raw client call (`psycopg.connect(...)`) to a wrapper (`connect_job()`, `get_conn()`, any project-specific factory), grep every test file that mocks the OLD name (`grep -rn 'patch.*psycopg\|psycopg\.connect' tests/`) as part of that migration PR and update them to mock the new name — a test mocking a name the production code no longer calls is not a passing test, it's a leak wearing a green checkmark. When writing/reviewing a job-body test: after wiring the mock, assert the mock was actually invoked (`mock.assert_called()`/a nonzero `call_count`) as a canary BEFORE asserting on downstream values — a `call_count == 0` on the connection mock is the tell that the isolation is fake. Never assume `patch.object(scheduler, "settings", stub_settings)` redirects `settings.database_url` repo-wide: any module with its OWN `from app.config import settings` import (like `app.jobs.job_connection`) reads its own reference, untouched by a patch scoped to a different module's name.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `tests/test_fundamentals_sync.py::_stub_two_connect_ctxes` (mocks `scheduler.connect_job`, docstring names the #1887 failure mode); `tests/test_daily_research_refresh_dedupe.py::_base_mocks` (same); `.claude/skills/engineering/test-quality.md` §"Mock the name the code actually calls".

### A pie/memo ownership slice must not render a zero-share holder — it reads as a duplicate row
- First seen in: #1916 (2026-07-04, ownership graph QA sweep). `ownership_*_current` is keyed on `(instrument_id, holder_identity_key, ownership_nature)`, so a person's `direct` and `indirect` lots are separate rows; when the latest `indirect` observation reports 0 shares (a stale lot the holder has since zeroed), `_current` keeps that 0-share row and `_reconcile_owner_once`'s additive path (`_source_rows_and_total`) kept BOTH lots as display holders. AAPL's Katherine Adams therefore rendered TWICE in the insiders slice — a live 175,408-share `direct` row beside a 0-share `indirect` row from a 2-year-old accession. Full-population dev scan: 5,209 zero-share additive rows across 2,018 instruments, plus 3,778 zero-share institutional rows across 2,661 instruments — the operator's "insiders/institutions feel duplicated" complaint.
- Symptom: an owner appears more than once in a single pie/memo slice, one of the rows carrying 0 shares (or a slice's `filer_count` overcounts holders that contribute nothing to `total_shares`). The extra row changes no figure (0 contributes 0 to total/pct/residual) but reads as an unexplained duplicate.
- Prevention: drop non-positive-share holders when building any slice — a holder whose reconciled current holding is 0 is not a holder of the issuer. Figure-neutral by construction (verify no `ownership_*_current` row is strictly negative before relying on this — a negative row currently reduces a total, so a `> 0` filter would move it). Do it at the single slice-build chokepoint (`_build_slice`), and skip a slice that becomes empty after the filter (mirror the "no holders → no slice" convention) rather than emitting a zero-holder slice. Distinct from the SUM-vs-MAX regime (prevention-log "Same owner across reporting channels"): this is a display-noise filter, not an aggregation change.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/ownership_rollup.py::_build_slice` (positive-share filter + why-comment) + `_bucket_into_slices` (`_add` skips empty built slices); `tests/test_ownership_zero_share_holders.py` (pure-logic: drop, skip-empty, memo-fund, figure-neutral).

### An identity-keyed collapse/dedup at a shared chokepoint must be scoped to the slices whose identity semantics guarantee it — not applied to every caller
- First seen in: #1942 (2026-07-04, Codex ckpt-2 HIGH). `_collapse_owner_lots` merges an owner's additive `direct`+`indirect` Section-16 lots into one ownership-card line, keyed on `_identity_key` (CIK-first). It was called unconditionally inside `_build_slice`, the chokepoint every slice funnels through. The safety proof ("two same-key rows came from ONE `_reconcile_owner_once` group, so never two distinct owners") holds ONLY for the insiders slice — the one built from reconciled holders. The `funds` slice bypasses reconcile and is intentionally one row per `fund_series_name` while every series of a trust shares one `fund_filer_cik`; `def14a_unmatched` is one row per proxy nature. A CIK-keyed collapse there merges distinct holders. Full-population dev scan: **221,540** `(instrument, fund_filer_cik)` groups hold ≥2 distinct fund series — all would have been wrongly collapsed, undercounting `filer_count` on the funds memo across most of the universe.
- Symptom: a dedup/collapse/merge helper keyed on an identity (CIK, name, id) is dropped into a shared build/render chokepoint and "works" for the slice it was designed for, but silently corrupts a sibling slice that funnels through the same chokepoint whose rows legitimately share that identity key (per-series funds under one manager CIK, per-nature proxy rows under one filer, per-lot anything). The figure that breaks is a count (`filer_count`) or a row list, not the primary total, so it passes a total-only smoke check.
- Prevention: before placing an identity-keyed collapse at a chokepoint, enumerate EVERY slice/category that reaches it and confirm each one's identity semantics make same-key-⇒-same-holder true there. If the invariant is guaranteed by an upstream pass (`_reconcile_owner_once`), the collapse belongs behind a gate on the category that pass produced — `if category == "insiders":` — not in the shared body. State the caller contract in the helper docstring AND enforce it at the call site. Self-review prompt: "which OTHER callers hit this chokepoint, and does any of them legitimately have two distinct rows sharing my collapse key?" Verify on the full population (`GROUP BY instrument, <key> HAVING count(DISTINCT <real-identity>) >= 2`) for every non-target slice, not just the target.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/ownership_rollup.py::_build_slice` (collapse gated to `category == "insiders"` + why-comment) and `_collapse_owner_lots` (docstring CALLER CONTRACT); `tests/test_ownership_collapse_owner_lots.py::test_build_slice_does_not_collapse_funds_slice`.

### A nullable-filter SQL param must be cast to its column type — an untyped NULL 500s the whole statement
- First seen in: #1961 (2026-07-04, FE-QA of Portfolio → Activity tab). #1926 (`5616d68b`) added an optional `instrument_id` filter to both queries in `get_activity` (`app/api/portfolio.py`): `AND (%(instrument_id)s IS NULL OR instrument_id = %(instrument_id)s)`. The per-instrument Positions tab passes a concrete int, so Postgres infers the param type and it works. The Portfolio-level Activity tab passes NO `instrument_id` → the param binds as `None` → an untyped NULL (OID 0). psycopg3's extended-protocol send gives Postgres no type context and `$N IS NULL OR col = $N` cannot be resolved: `psycopg.errors.AmbiguousParameter: could not determine data type of parameter $2`. The entire endpoint 500'd — the whole Activity tab was dead — yet no test caught it because the only exercised path always passed a concrete id.
- Symptom: an endpoint/query with an optional filter written as `%(x)s IS NULL OR col = %(x)s` returns 500 `AmbiguousParameter: could not determine data type of parameter $N` for the caller that omits the filter (passes `None`), while the caller that supplies a concrete value works fine. Green in every test that exercises the filtered path; broken only on the default/no-filter path.
- Prevention: cast a nullable-filter param to its column type at EVERY occurrence — `%(x)s::bigint IS NULL OR col = %(x)s::bigint` (match the column: `::bigint`, `::int`, `::text`, `::date`). Grep the type from `information_schema.columns` before casting, don't guess. When adding an optional filter param, always smoke the `None`/no-filter path, not just the value path — that path is the one production hits by default and the one tests skip. Distinct from the `col IS %s` illegality above: this is type-inference on a genuine NULL binding, not operator-form.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/sql-correctness.md` §Parameterisation (nullable-filter cast bullet); `app/api/portfolio.py::get_activity` (both queries cast `%(instrument_id)s::bigint`).

### An unbounded regex quantifier over an alternation/modifier group in a filing-HTML parser is a ReDoS — bound the repeat and fuzz a long non-matching tail
- First seen in: #1967 (2026-07-04, PR #1968 bot NITPICK). The DEF 14A SCT name/position splitter (`_split_name_position` → `_POSITION_ROLE_RE`, `app/providers/implementations/sec_def14a.py`) grew a leading-title-modifier prefix `(?:(?:senior|former|acting|interim)\s+|co-?\s*)*` so "Senior Executive Vice President" would split at "Senior". The `*` is unbounded: `re.search` retries the group at every start position, and on a long run of modifier tokens with NO trailing role keyword ("Senior Senior … X") it consumes-then-backtracks across the whole run at each position → quadratic. Empirical fuzz: `'Senior '*5000` = 1.9s, `'Co-'*5000` = 3.7s in one `_split_name_position` call. Real SCT cells are short, but the parser runs on untrusted SEC filing HTML, so a pathologically-rendered cell could stall the manifest worker. The bot's "confirm with a large fuzzed cell" nitpick was correct — the fuzz found the blowup.
- Symptom: a parser regex adds a `(?:...)*` or `(?:...)+` over an alternation/modifier group (leading modifiers, repeated separators, optional prefixes) as a "make the match start earlier / swallow the repeats" convenience; it passes all short unit fixtures because real inputs are tiny, but a synthetic input of N repeated group-matching tokens followed by a char that fails the rest of the pattern takes seconds (super-linear in N). The tell is an unbounded quantifier wrapping alternation where the tail after the group can fail.
- Prevention: bound the repeat to a small constant that covers real data (`{0,3}` here — real titles carry ≤2 leading modifiers) instead of `*`/`+`. Whenever you add or widen a `(?:...)*`/`(?:...)+` over alternation in a parser that runs on untrusted/external input, FUZZ it before shipping: `t=time.perf_counter(); regex_fn(token*5000 + "X"); assert dt < ~50ms` for each group-matching token, with a tail char that defeats the rest of the pattern. Add a bounded-input regression test (`test_*_run_is_bounded`) so a later revert to `*` re-hangs a test rather than production. Cheap check during pre-flight review: grep new/changed regexes for `)*`/`)+` following `(?:` with `|` inside, and confirm each is either anchored so it can't backtrack or bounded `{0,k}`.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/providers/implementations/sec_def14a.py::_POSITION_ROLE_RE` (`{0,3}` bound + why-comment); `tests/test_sec_def14a_sct_parser.py::test_split_name_position_modifier_run_is_bounded`.

### A per-row batch loop that claims "one bad row never aborts the batch" must catch EVERY exception the per-row compute can raise — not just DB error classes
- First seen in: #2019 (2026-07-12, #2009 PR-A, bot WARNING). `refresh_fair_value_band_batch` (`app/services/fair_value_band.py`) ran each instrument's pass-2 compute + write-through under a `with conn.transaction()` savepoint and caught only `psycopg.errors.UndefinedTable/UndefinedColumn/DataError`. But the pure compute path enforced fail-closed data invariants with bare `assert` (`combine_across`'s `assert bear <= base <= bull`, `synth_multiple`'s narrowing asserts) and also raises `ValueError` (`to_per_share` unavailable metric, `percentiles` empty, `combine_across` empty). `AssertionError`/`ValueError` were OUTSIDE the except clause, so a single invariant violation would propagate out of the per-row loop and abort the ENTIRE full-universe batch — the exact opposite of the loop's "one bad row never aborts the batch" contract. Worse, `assert` is stripped under `python -O`, so in an optimized run the fail-closed guarantee silently vanished and a mis-ordered band could persist.
- Symptom: a batch/loop persists per-row results under a savepoint and advertises row-level isolation ("one bad row skipped, the rest continue"), but its `except` names only DB/IO error classes. A pure-layer invariant guard (`assert`, `raise ValueError`, `raise KeyError`) in the called compute path throws → the exception escapes the per-row handler → the whole batch dies on row K, leaving rows K+1..N unprocessed. Compounded when the guard is `assert`: green in tests, silently absent under `python -O`.
- Prevention: (1) enforce fail-closed DATA invariants with an explicit `raise ValueError(...)` (or a domain exception), NEVER bare `assert` — `assert` is for internal never-happens programmer checks and is stripped under `-O`. (2) The per-row `except` must cover every exception type the called compute can raise: grep the entire per-row call path for `raise`/`assert` and list each type in the handler. Include `ValueError` (and any domain exception) alongside the DB classes. (3) Do NOT broaden to bare `except Exception` — a genuine programming bug (TypeError, AttributeError) must still surface and fail loud. Self-review prompt: "for every `raise`/`assert` reachable from this loop body, is its type in my `except` tuple?"
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/python-hygiene.md` §"Batch loops catch every per-row raise"; `app/services/fair_value_band.py::combine_across`/`synth_multiple` (explicit `raise ValueError` / `-O`-safe `is not None` guards, no asserts) + `refresh_fair_value_band_batch` (except tuple includes `ValueError` + why-comment).

### A CIK→instrument resolver must return EVERY in-universe instrument for the CIK — `DISTINCT ON (cik)` silently drops share-class siblings
