### A repair migration's `COALESCE(derived_value, default)` hides the "nothing to derive from" case — assert the fallback was never taken
- First seen in: #2192 review (2026-08-02), PR #2194 WARNING + NITPICK + PREVENTION. `sql/243` derived a per-instrument fiscal-year offset from that instrument's own sane rows and applied `COALESCE(o.fy_offset, 0)`. On the actual corpus the fallback was unreachable (inner join + NOT NULL columns), but the SHAPE is the bug: it reads as though "no offset available" is a supported case handled by assuming zero. For a non-calendar-fiscal issuer whose rows were all corrupted, a defaulted zero would mis-label every repaired row — and if the wrong label happened to land inside the new CHECK's range, the post-repair range assertion would pass and the mis-repair would ship silently.
- Symptom: a data-repair migration computes a per-entity correction factor from that entity's own surviving good data, then defaults the factor when none exists. The default is indistinguishable in the output from a *measured* value of the same magnitude. A post-hoc validity check (range CHECK, NOT NULL, row count) cannot separate them, because a wrongly-defaulted repair is usually still *valid* — just wrong. Compounded when the offset source is one table but the repair also writes a sibling table, so an entity whose good rows live only in the sibling silently takes the default.
- Prevention: in a repair migration, represent "cannot derive" as ABSENCE (entity missing from the derivation CTE), never as a defaulted value, and add an explicit pre-repair assertion that every entity about to be repaired HAS a derived value — `LEFT JOIN derivation ON ... WHERE derivation.key IS NULL` → `RAISE EXCEPTION`. Run it BEFORE the UPDATE, not after: a post-hoc check only catches repairs that are out of range, not repairs that are merely wrong. Derive the factor from the union of every table the repair writes, so a sibling-only entity is not silently orphaned. Keep the post-repair range check as a backstop, not as the primary guard.
- Related: editing an ALREADY-APPLIED migration trips the #1333 content-drift guard. On an unmerged PR the correct reset is: drop the objects the migration created, `DELETE FROM schema_migrations WHERE filename = ...`, re-run — do NOT add an `NNN+1` patch migration for a file no other environment has seen.
- Enforced in: this log; `sql/243_fiscal_year_range_check.sql` (the pre-repair orphaned-instrument `DO` block, the `UNION ALL` offset source across both derived tables, and the removal of `COALESCE`).

### The FE test harness renders without `StrictMode`, so mount-only effect bugs are structurally invisible
- First seen in: #2207 (2026-08-02). Deep-linking `/instrument/AAPL/chart?compare=MSFT,JPM` rendered the compare chips and entered compare mode, but the compare series never got data (tooltip `MSFT — JPM —`); adding the identical tickers interactively worked. The ticket's hypothesis — "the URL-restore path seeds the symbol list without triggering the fetch" — was **falsified**: a bare-render test of the same URL passed (`MSFT:2,GOOG:2`). Only wrapping the render in `<StrictMode>` reproduced it (`MSFT:0,GOOG:0`).
- Symptom: an effect combines a **dedup ref** ("have I already started work for this key?") with a **cancellation flag** ("did I unmount before the promise resolved?"). Each is individually correct; together they deadlock on mount under `StrictMode`. Run 1 stamps the ref and starts the fetch; the simulated unmount sets `cancelled = true`, so run 1's `.then` discards its own results; run 2 sees the key already stamped and returns before fetching. Nobody ever calls the setter. It manifests **only at mount** (StrictMode double-invokes effects there and nowhere else), which is exactly why the interactive path — which changes the key — looked healthy and hid the bug. The UI asserts the data is present (chips/labels read the URL directly) over a permanently empty series.
- Prevention: TWO rules. (1) Any effect that stamps a "work started" ref MUST release that stamp in its cleanup when the work it stamped is being abandoned — `if (keyRef.current === key) keyRef.current = ""` — guarded on equality so a teardown racing a newer run cannot clear that run's stamp. A dedup ref whose lifetime is longer than the fetch it guards is a latch, not a dedup. (2) `main.tsx` mounts the app inside `<React.StrictMode>` but every test in the suite renders a bare `<MemoryRouter>`, so **production runs effects twice at mount and the tests never do**. When a bug reproduces in the browser but not under test, wrap the render in `<StrictMode>` before doubting the repro. Any page whose mount-time effects fetch, subscribe, or stamp a ref needs at least one StrictMode-wrapped test — see `ChartPage.test.tsx::renderPageStrict`.
- Generalises to: any "have I done this already?" ref, in-flight registry, or `useRef` idempotence latch guarding an async side effect. Ask "if this run is abandoned, who un-stamps it?" — and note that a chip/label rendered from URL params is not evidence the data behind it loaded.
- Enforced in: this log; `.claude/skills/frontend/async-data-loading.md`; `frontend/src/pages/ChartPage.tsx` (the compare-fetch cleanup releases `compareFetchKeyRef`); `frontend/src/pages/ChartPage.test.tsx` (`renderPageStrict` + the "compare deep-link hydration (#2207)" block, which asserts row COUNTS handed to the canvas, not just that chips rendered).

### A display-unit transform applied to one overlay family and not its sibling puts two units on one axis
- First seen in: #2209 (2026-08-02). `ChartWorkspaceCanvas` compare mode re-bases the primary series to % change from the first bar. The **trend** overlays (regression + channel) branched on that (`compares.length > 0 ? normalizeToPercent(rawCloses)… : rawCloses`); the **indicator** overlays (SMA/EMA) had no such branch and kept plotting absolute price. One right-hand price scale then spanned dollars and percent at once: measured −80…80 with indicators off, −200…350 with them on, compressing the actual compare series into ~15% of the canvas.
- Symptom: the #1955 sibling-gap class in the **rendering** layer. Two families of overlay draw onto one shared axis; a later change teaches one family about a display-unit mode and misses the other. Nothing fails — both series render, the axis just silently auto-scales to the union of two incompatible units, so an axis label like `250.00` means percent in the lower region and dollars in the upper one with nothing marking the boundary. Worse than the overlays being meaningless: turning them on destroys the readability of the comparison the operator opened compare mode to see. Typecheck and unit tests are blind to it (the numbers are all valid `number`s) and lightweight-charts cannot render in jsdom, so only a browser look catches it.
- Prevention: when a chart introduces a display-unit mode (% change, log scale, rebased-to-100, currency conversion), grep every `addSeries` / `setData` call site that targets the SAME price scale and confirm each one takes the branch — a shared axis means the transform is a property of the AXIS, not of the series that happened to prompt the change. Pin it with a test asserting the union range of (primary ∪ every overlay) equals the primary's range, i.e. enabling an overlay cannot widen the axis. Prefer deriving the branch from ONE named flag (`compareMode`) over re-deriving `compares.length > 0` per effect, so a reader can grep the flag and enumerate every consumer.
- Enforced in: this log; `.claude/skills/frontend/design-system.md`; `frontend/src/pages/components/ChartWorkspaceCanvas.tsx` (both the indicator and trend effects branch on `compareMode`); `frontend/src/pages/components/ChartWorkspaceCanvas.test.tsx` ("normalized indicators — compare mode invariants (#2209)").

### A documented caveat became a blindfold — "expected" is a claim about frequency

- First seen in: #2217 (found 2026-08-03 during an unrelated accuracy audit).
- Symptom: `metrics-analyst` documents that treasury may legitimately push the ownership chart `oversubscribed` on a stale-13F + fresh-Form-4 mix. When the golden panel returned `oversubscribed=true` for JPM and HD, the documented caveat was accepted as the explanation and the observation was dropped. Sampling 20 large caps instead of 5 returned **9** — 45%. The real cause was arithmetic: `_compute_residual` subtracted `treasury_shares` from `shares_outstanding`, a base that already excludes treasury (shares issued = shares outstanding + treasury). GS holds treasury equal to 199.9% of outstanding, so the residual went negative before any holder was counted, clamped to zero, and public float rendered as **ZERO** for Coca-Cola, Goldman, JPM, P&G and Exxon. 12 lines below, `_compute_concentration` already excluded treasury correctly — the right treatment existed in the same file.
- Prevention: a caveat states that a condition CAN occur; it says nothing about how often, and the author usually never measured. **"Expected" at 2% is a caveat; the same condition at 45% is a defect wearing the caveat as cover.** On meeting a documented-expected condition in live data, spend the one query it costs to get its RATE across the population before accepting it. The dangerous shape is precisely a caveat that explains away an observation you would otherwise have investigated. Also: the 3-5 instrument golden panel is not evidence for operator-visible METRIC correctness, not just for parsers — a panel too small to carry a rate cannot falsify a caveat.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/full-population-ab.md` ("A documented caveat is a hypothesis about FREQUENCY"). `metrics-analyst` treasury/residual entry to be corrected when #2217 lands.

### Asserting a cause without checking whether the effect is a settled decision

- First seen in: #2213 (2026-08-03), caught by a Codex pass over the assessment, not by any test.
- Symptom: a ticket was filed claiming an OpenFIGI resolution stall was "the direct cause of `coverage.state = unknown_universe` on the ownership card for all five golden-panel instruments". It is not. `ownership_rollup._read_universe_estimates` returns hard-coded `None` for every category, and its docstring records committee verdict #790 (disposed 2026-06-17): *"`unknown_universe` IS the truthful state — faking a denominator is the lie."* The state is unconditional and correct. Left standing, the claim would have given the next session an acceptance signal that can never move, and pointed them at reopening a closed decision.
- Root cause of the miss: working-order step 2 ("read `docs/settled-decisions.md`") was being applied to tickets being *worked*, not to causal claims made while *investigating* — which is where it matters most, because a cause-claim written into a ticket becomes the next session's acceptance criterion.
- Prevention: before writing "X causes Y" about any operator-visible symptom, (1) grep `docs/settled-decisions.md` for Y, and (2) read the docstring of the function that produces Y — in this repo the disposal rationale is usually written there at length by whoever settled it. **A symptom you find surprising is as likely to be a decision you have not read as it is to be a bug.**
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/CLAUDE.md` ("Never assert a CAUSE without checking whether the effect is a settled decision").

### A line beginning `#<digits>` renders as an H1

- First seen in: 2026-08-03, in `.claude/skills/engineering/full-population-ab.md` (4 occurrences) and caught again in a CLAUDE.md edit the same day.
- Symptom: prose wrapped so that an issue reference lands at the start of a line (`#2164 changed a role-heading regex...`) is parsed by markdown as an ATX heading and renders as giant text, silently corrupting the document structure of a skill file. Invisible in the editor's plain-text view; only the markdown linter (MD018) flags it.
- Prevention: never let a line start with `#` followed by a digit. Prefix the word `Issue` or rewrap. Markdown lint warnings on `.claude/**` and `docs/**` are worth reading rather than dismissing as cosmetic — this class silently breaks the rendering of the very files agents read for instruction.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; the four occurrences in `full-population-ab.md` are fixed.

### A/B whose two arms read the live dev DB is measuring ingest, not the change

- First seen in: #2217 (2026-08-03), caught by the harness's own invariants, not by review.
- Symptom: control and treatment were run as two sequential ~4-minute processes against the live dev DB, 15 minutes apart. The diff reported four invariant failures — residual decreasing on 42 instruments, zero-treasury control instruments moving, and `concentration` changing on 465 — none of which the one-line arithmetic change could produce. The jobs daemon had rewritten **2,175 `ownership_institutions_current` rows** in the window between the arms, so the two sides simply saw different data.
- Root cause of the miss: the existing guidance ("run both sides concurrently, use a worktree for the control") addresses code isolation, and is silent on DATA isolation. It assumes the offline re-parse path, where both sides read frozen payloads. A metric computed at read time has no stored artefact to re-parse, so both arms necessarily hit the live database — which never stops moving.
- Prevention: **an invariant failing on a quantity the change cannot affect means suspect the harness before the fix.** For read-time metrics, pair the arms: one process, and for each item evaluate BOTH inside a single `snapshot_read` (REPEATABLE READ) from one set of inputs. Extract the control body verbatim via `git show origin/main:<path>` and `exec` it rather than retyping or deriving it analytically — that keeps "never simulate the control" intact while removing the contamination. Re-run paired, all four invariants passed and the controls were byte-identical.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/full-population-ab.md` §Mechanics.

### A closed vocabulary declared in three places is validated in none of them

- First seen in: #2229 (2026-08-03). Found by curling the live dev endpoint for Definition-of-Done clause 8, not by any test.
- Symptom: a new `CorrectionApplied` kind (`superseded_by_later_13f_hr`) was added to the service layer. `_CorrectionAppliedModel.kind` in `app/api/instruments.py` is a closed Pydantic `Literal` and `frontend/src/api/ownership.ts::OwnershipCorrectionKind` is a closed TS union; neither was updated. **Every `/instruments/{sym}/ownership-rollup` request that fired the new correction returned 500** — 364 corrections on AAPL, 691 on HD — while `ruff`, `pyright`, the fast tier, the smoke test, both DB-backed supersession suites and `test_api_instruments.py` were all green.
- Root cause of the miss: the service constructs the dataclass with a plain `str` field; the Literal is only evaluated at *serialization*. Service-layer tests assert on the dataclass and never serialize, and no API test exercised an instrument that had a correction. The two declarations were coupled by convention only. This is the #1955 class ("contract-field wired into one model not its sibling") in its most expensive form, because the failure is a hard 500 rather than a wrong number.
- Prevention: when a vocabulary is declared in more than one place, **derive one from the other in a test** rather than adding a case per value. `tests/test_correction_kind_vocab_contract.py` reads every `CorrectionApplied(kind=...)` out of the service by AST and asserts the API Literal and the FE union cover all of them, plus the reverse so a rename cannot leave stale vocabulary behind. Two guards on the extractor itself were load-bearing and both fired while it was being written: a `>= 6` lower bound caught it missing a conditional-expression call site (matching bare `ast.Constant` made BOTH branches of `"a" if src == "x" else "b"` invisible), and a subsequent blind `ast.walk` over-collected `"x"` — the comparison operand in that conditional's test. **A coverage assertion built on an extractor needs a test that the extractor found anything, or it passes vacuously** — the `x <> ALL('{}')` failure mode in test form.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `tests/test_correction_kind_vocab_contract.py`; the docstring on `_CorrectionAppliedModel` names its sibling explicitly.

### A read-time metric needs a LATENCY arm, not just a correctness A/B

- First seen in: #2229 (2026-08-03).
- Symptom: the supersession predicate was correct — the paired full-population A/B would have confirmed it — and made the operator-facing ownership-rollup endpoint **11-20x slower**: AAPL 214ms → 2,390ms, HD 180ms → 3,675ms. The rollup is computed at read time and stored nowhere (`metrics-analyst`: "Storage: not stored"), so a predicate added to it runs on every request. Nothing in the correctness harness, the test suite or the review checklist measures wall time.
- Root cause of the miss: `full-population-ab.md` measures whether the FIGURE changed. For a metric with no stored artefact, "cost per request" is a second axis the same change can regress, and it is invisible to every existing gate.
- Prevention: for any change to a read-time-computed metric, **time control vs treatment on the real endpoint before pushing** — same `git show origin/main` control extraction the paired A/B already uses, just measuring `perf_counter` instead of the figure. Three findings came out of doing it here, none of which correctness testing would surface: (1) a subquery scoped to the *instrument* was being re-evaluated per *row* and belonged in a CTE; (2) the better data source measured 3x WORSE until it had a covering index — `idx_inst_current_filer (filer_cik)` alone made `MAX(period_end)` heap-fetch every row of a filer like Vanguard, 7,651ms, against 25ms with `(filer_cik, period_end DESC)`; (3) **a missing index can make you reject the correct source.** Measure the source and the index together, or the index decision silently becomes a data-model decision.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/full-population-ab.md`; `sql/245_inst_current_filer_period_idx.sql` records the measurement in its header.

### zsh eats backticks in ANY double-quoted argument, not just `git commit -m`

- First seen in: #2235 (2026-08-03), generalising the `git commit -m` case already
  recorded in the operator's global notes and the pre-push-hook entry above.
- Symptom: `gh issue comment 2235 --body "… a \`_validate_writer_output\` identity
  assertion gives a retry-once …"` posted with the function name **missing entirely** —
  the comment read "a  identity assertion", and zsh reported
  `command not found: _validate_writer_output`. The comment was already public before
  the mangling was noticed; the `command not found` line is the only tell, and it is
  easy to miss next to a successful-looking URL in the same output.
- Root cause of the miss: the existing guidance was filed against `git commit -m` and
  against pre-push hook echoes, so it read as being about *those two commands*. It is
  not — it is about **zsh double quotes**, and every CLI that takes prose as an argument
  is exposed: `gh issue comment --body`, `gh pr create --body`, `gh pr comment`,
  `gh issue create --body`. Technical prose is exactly the kind that contains backticked
  identifiers, so the exposure is highest where the text matters most.
- Prevention: **never pass prose containing backticks in a double-quoted argument.** Use
  a quoted heredoc, which disables all expansion:
  `gh issue comment N --body "$(cat <<'EOF' … EOF\n)"` — note the quoted `'EOF'`, which
  is what turns substitution off; an unquoted `EOF` still expands. The same form works
  for `git commit -F -`. If a comment has already posted mangled, `gh issue comment
  --edit-last` repairs it in place rather than leaving a correction comment.
- Enforced in: this prevention log. No automated gate — the failure is in the shell, not
  in the repo, so the guard is the habit of reaching for the heredoc whenever the body
  contains a code identifier.

### A discriminator run against the WRONG pair of columns returns the opposite verdict

- First seen in: #2243 (2026-08-04).
- Symptom: S3 asked whether `QuoteUpdate.last` is a real trade print. The spike's own stated method was the textbook one — a print can trade outside the quote, a derived value cannot — so: does `last` ever leave `[bid, ask]`? Run against eToro's `Bid`/`Ask`, the FX arm came back with `last` sitting mid-spread (mean normalised position 0.399, median 0.425, never equal to bid or ask), which reads as **a derived mid**. Run against the `BidDiscounted`/`AskDiscounted` columns in the same payload, `last` sits **exactly on the bid** in 97.8% of the same observations. The two runs support opposite conclusions about the same field on the same data.
- Root cause of the miss: `Bid`/`Ask` are eToro's **marked-up** quote; the `*Discounted` fields are the underlying one. A test of the form "is X inside the interval [A, B]" silently assumes A and B are the interval the source actually derives X from. Nothing about the column names says which pair that is, and the obvious pair was the wrong one. The same shape recurs anywhere a vendor publishes both a raw and an adjusted/marked-up/net figure — the discriminator is only as good as the reference series it is measured against.
- Prevention: before running a containment or excursion discriminator, **enumerate every candidate reference series in the payload and run the test against all of them**, then report the full matrix rather than the one pair that looked canonical. A discriminator that yields different verdicts per reference pair has not answered the question — it has found that the reference is the actual unknown. Corollary, from the same spike: the eventual verdict was settled not by the containment test at all but by **reconstruction** — rebuilding the vendor's own published artefact (its `OneMinute` candle) from each candidate series and scoring which one reproduces it. Reconstruction beats containment because it has a ground truth; containment only has a bound.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/data-sources/etoro-api.md` (§WS rate semantics carries the "run it against `BidDiscounted`/`AskDiscounted`" warning inline).

---

### A "disagreement" count with no catch-up control measures LATENCY and reports it as data loss

- First seen in: #2243 (2026-08-04).
- Symptom: to test whether eToro's sparse WS deltas drop price updates, a second connection took a forced full snapshot every 20s and the accumulated delta-state was compared against it. **3,435 of 4,566 field comparisons disagreed — 75%.** Read at face value that is a stream losing three quarters of its updates, and it would have justified abandoning WS-built bars outright. Adding a look-ahead control — for each disagreement, does the streaming session *ever* reach the snapshot's value? — showed **100% caught up within 30s and 0 never did.** The feed is lossless; the holder was sub-second behind a fresh read.
- Root cause of the miss: comparing a continuously-updated accumulator against a point-in-time authoritative read conflates two different failure modes — "never received it" and "had not received it *yet*". At any sampling instant a healthy stream is legitimately a few hundred ms stale, so the disagreement rate is a function of tick rate and sampling latency, not of correctness. The measurement looked decisive and pointed the wrong way.
- Prevention: whenever a freshness or completeness check compares live-accumulated state against an authoritative snapshot, **the disagreement count is not the finding — the unresolved-after-lookahead count is.** Always pair it with "did the accumulator subsequently converge to the snapshot value, and within what window", and report both. Same family as the narrowing-gate rule: the raw count is silent about the thing you actually care about.
- Enforced in: this prevention log. The S3 probe scripts attached to #2243 carry the control as `s3_position.py` §D6.

---

### A parser that requires a COMPLETE payload from a delta feed silently sees under half the data

- First seen in: #2252 (2026-08-04), root-caused in #2243.
- Symptom: `_parse_rate_content` read `payload["Bid"]` and `payload["Ask"]` as bare subscripts inside a `try`, so any push missing either field raised `KeyError` and returned `None` — the same value the function returns for "this is not a rate message at all". eToro's `Trading.Instrument.Rate` push is a **field-level sparse delta**: only 16.8% of messages carry `Bid`+`Ask` together, so **58.1% of price-CHANGING messages were discarded as if malformed**, and the `quotes` table (and the live chart reading it) ran 1.5–2.6× staler than the feed already provided. No error, no log line, no failing test — the shape was believed to be priceless, so dropping it looked correct.
- Root cause of the miss: two distinct outcomes were collapsed into one return value. `None` meant both "not a rate message" (correct to skip) and "rate message I could not fully use" (a real update being thrown away). Nothing downstream could tell them apart, so the loss was unobservable by construction. The upstream measurement compounded it: #2241 recorded "23% of inner rate messages parse" and it was read as *77% are inert heartbeats*, when only ~60% are — the parse rate was taken as a property of the wire rather than of our parser.
- Prevention: **before writing or sizing a parser for a push feed, census the actual field combinations on the wire — do not infer the message shapes from the ones your parser already handles.** Then: (1) a partial/incomplete payload must be a *distinct* outcome from "not my message type", never the same sentinel; (2) if the feed is delta-shaped, the consumer owns per-entity state and merges — statelessness is not an option, it is a silent 50%+ data loss; (3) track field **presence** separately from field **value** where absent and null mean different things (an absent `LastExecution` means "unchanged"; a present `<= 0` means "NULL per #1429" — one nullable field cannot carry both); (4) never emit on a payload carrying no value at all, or `quoted_at` advances with nothing behind it and freshness is overstated. Corollary for sizing any ingest: **measure the parse rate against the wire, not against itself** — "X% of messages parse" says nothing about what fraction of *information* is captured.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/data-sources/etoro-api.md` (§WS rate semantics carries the merge requirement, the presence-vs-value rule and the measured before/after); `tests/test_etoro_websocket.py::TestRateStateStore` table-tests every observed wire shape.

### Adding a fail-fast assert inside a builder widens the exception surface of every CALLER

- First seen in: #2249 (2026-08-04).
- Symptom: `_seal_frame` was given a hard `raise ValueError` if a built WS frame exceeded the byte limit — correct in itself, since the breach is silent and costs the whole connection. But two call sites built their frames *just outside* the `try:` that wrapped the send. Those call sites (`add_instruments` / `remove_instruments`) run on the SSE request path and have a deliberate contract of "log the failure and let the next reconnect resubscribe from the ref counts". A sizing bug would therefore have bypassed that contract entirely and surfaced as an unhandled 500 on the operator's stream — a strictly worse outcome than the silent-drop the assert was added to prevent.
- Root cause of the miss: the assert was reasoned about locally ("this must never ship a bad frame") without asking what the *callers* already promised to do about failure. A helper that previously could not raise, and now can, has changed its contract with everyone who calls it — and the diff at each call site shows no change at all, so nothing draws the eye.
- Prevention: when adding a raise to an existing helper, **grep every call site and check each one's error boundary in the same commit**. If a caller wraps the helper's *use* in a try, the *construction* belongs inside that try too. Corollary: prefer putting the new raise where the existing boundary already is, rather than adding boundaries to match a new raise.
- Enforced in: this prevention log. Caught by the review bot on PR #2256, not by any test — a sizing assert that is unreachable by construction has no natural test to fail.

---

### Register the pending entry BEFORE the await that can deliver its reply

- First seen in: #2249 (2026-08-04).
- Symptom: the WS op-frame sender read `await ws.send(frame.payload)` then `self._register_pending(frame)`. The `await` yields to the event loop, so the receive loop — running concurrently in the same loop — could take that frame's ack and call the resolver **before** the registration happened. The resolver popped nothing (no entry yet), the registration then inserted an entry whose ack had already been and gone, and 10s later the reaper reported a **genuinely acked** frame as `NEVER ACKED`. The detector added to catch a silent drop would have been crying wolf on the healthy path.
- Root cause of the miss: the two lines read as a sequence — "send it, then remember it" — and in synchronous code that ordering is fine. Under asyncio the `await` is a scheduling point, so anything the reply can race is already live by the time the next line runs. The bug is invisible in a test that sends and acks in separate steps; it only appears if the ack is delivered from *inside* the send.
- Prevention: **whenever a send registers state that the response clears, register before the send, not after** — and de-register on send failure, since nothing will reply to a frame that never reached the wire. Catch `BaseException` for that cleanup, not `Exception`: a cancellation mid-send must clean up too. Generalises past WS frames to any request/response bookkeeping across an await — in-flight maps, correlation ids, timeout registries.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/etoro_websocket.py::EtoroWebSocketSubscriber._send_frames` is the single chokepoint all three send sites go through, so the ordering cannot be got wrong per-site; `tests/test_etoro_websocket.py::TestAckCorrelation::test_ack_arriving_during_send_still_clears_the_entry` acks from inside `send` to reproduce the tightest form of the race.

---

### A coverage number built on `EXISTS(row)` overstates usable coverage for any continuously-refreshed table

- First seen in: #2246 (2026-08-04); the defect it exposed is #2254.
- Symptom: the TA-milestone design doc, the spike ticket and the roadmap all carried *"5,221 of 12,684 tradable instruments have price history"* as the measured constraint on every surface in the milestone. The query behind it was `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM price_daily WHERE instrument_id = i.instrument_id)`. Re-measured on **freshness** instead — `max(price_date) >= current_date - 7` — the real figure is **1,406 (11.1%)**. 3,523 of the 3,838 "priced" Tier-3 instruments had not been updated in 30 days, and every crypto, FX and commodity series in the database was two months stale. A heat map built on the 5,221 figure would have rendered ~3.7x more instruments than it had live prices for.
- Root cause of the miss: `price_daily` is refreshed by a job whose scope query excludes anything that already has a row (`NOT EXISTS (price_daily)` — the branch is a *seeder*, not a refresher). So "has a row" and "is being maintained" are not merely different, they are close to **mutually exclusive** for the tail. Nothing failed while this was true: the job reported `success`, the rows existed, and the staleness was invisible to every automated check — the same self-consistent-health class as #2218.
- Prevention: **for any table written by a scheduled job, a coverage claim must be an as-of measurement, not an existence one.** Report `count(*) FILTER (WHERE max(as_of) >= now() - <cadence>)` beside the raw row-existence count, and state which one a downstream number uses. Two corollaries: (1) when reading a refresh job's scope query, check specifically whether the selection **excludes rows it has already written** — that shape means the branch only ever seeds; (2) a stale value is worse than a missing one, because a missing value renders as "—" while a stale one renders, scores and backtests as if live.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/metrics-analyst/SKILL.md` §3 "Daily candles (OHLCV)" carries the two denominators and the US-only tier caveat; `.claude/skills/data-engineer/SKILL.md` §6 carries the seed-only scope mechanism; fixed in #2254 — see the next entry for the three traps that fixing it exposed.

---

### Replacing a seed-only job scope: the exclusion must EXPIRE, and a seeding gate must not be carried onto the maintenance arm

- First seen in: #2254 (2026-08-04), fixing the defect the entry above describes. Three separate ways the "obvious" fix reintroduces the bug.
- Symptom that would have resulted: a rewritten `_T3_CANDLE_SELECT` that still leaves most of the population frozen, while the job continues to report `success`.
- Root cause of each trap:
  1. **A latching exclusion.** `NOT EXISTS (price_daily)` is a *latching* predicate — success is what removes an instrument from scope. Any replacement of the form "rows this job has not written yet" is another seeder under a new name. Only a **freshness** predicate expires: `last_bar < <boundary>` re-admits tomorrow whatever it refreshed today.
  2. **Two boundaries that can drift.** The scope query and the per-item skip (`_candles_are_fresh`) both decide "is this current". A scope predicate looser than the skip burns requests on rows that are then skipped; tighter, and the rows it excludes are never refreshed at all. They must be the *same* value — here `_most_recent_trading_day`, computed once in Python and passed in as `%(fresh_through)s`.
  3. **Carrying the seeding gate onto maintenance.** The two arms answer different questions. The seed gate ("has fundamentals, or is a class that never will") answers *is a first fetch worth spending*, which is already settled for an instrument that has bars. Measured on the full population, applying it to the maintenance arm would have stranded **274 priced `us_equity` T3 with no `fundamentals_snapshot` row (259 already >30d stale)** — a hole invisible from the admit side, found only by enumerating what the gate REJECTS.
- Also settled here: a `LIMIT` on a maintenance branch is a **safety ceiling, not a rationing device**. A cap below the population size means permanent partial coverage with a rotating fresh set, which is the same defect at a smaller radius. Size the cap above the population, order **stalest-first** so a bound cap fixes the worst rows first, and log a WARNING when it binds — a silently truncated sweep reads as "everything is current".
- Prevention: when converting a seeder into a maintainer, state for the new predicate (a) what makes a row leave scope and **when it comes back**, (b) which other predicate defines the same boundary, and (c) what the old gate rejects that the new arm must not.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/workers/scheduler.py::_T3_CANDLE_SELECT` (both arms, with the reasoning inline); `tests/test_daily_candle_refresh_crypto_eligibility.py` pins all four — stale re-admitted, current excluded, priced-without-fundamentals maintained, stalest-first ordering.

---

### A published census is only as good as the SQL behind it — the classifier must be the written rule

- First seen in: S7 / #2247 (2026-08-04), Codex checkpoint 1 on the spike verdict. Two independent instances in one census, both invisible from the output.
- Symptom: a full-population census that reads as authoritative and is wrong, because the measurement SQL implements a *different* rule from the one the accompanying prose defines. Nothing in the numbers looks off — a rejection census is plausible at almost any magnitude, so there is no self-consistency check that fires.
- Root cause of each instance:
  1. **The rule was restated, and only the prose was updated.** The spike started with a raw `high/low >= 10` range test, then argued in writing that raw `high/low` is *wrong* (it rejects CNTM 2025-09-17, a real +3,000% day) and replaced it with a wick test, `min(open,close)/low >= 3`. The SQL was never changed. The census published wick-rule counts computed by the ratio rule.
  2. **A verdict split was declared in prose and not in the query.** The verdict separated `return_usable` from `range_usable` and stated that the containment and wick rules affect range only. The SQL still OR-ed all four bar rules into one `bar_bad` column, which fed the transition and window quarantine — so range-only defects were rejecting *returns*. Over-rejection was 821 transitions instead of 214, and 0.301% of windows instead of 0.225%.
- Generalises to: any measurement artefact where the analysis narrative and the query are edited separately — full-population A/B arms, rejection censuses, coverage tables, acceptance queries pasted into a ticket. The risk is highest exactly when the rule is being *refined mid-analysis*, which is when the prose moves first.
- Related but distinct from the strip-then-split entry above: there the guard and the slice disagreed inside one function; here the specification and its implementation disagree across two artefacts, and only a human comparing them line by line can see it.
- Prevention: before publishing any census, **re-read the query against the rule table clause by clause** and confirm each named rule appears with the same predicate, the same threshold, and the same downstream consumer. If the artefact declares more than one verdict class (usable-for-X vs usable-for-Y), the query needs one column per class — a single boolean is the tell that the split exists only in the prose. Where the rules will be shipped, express them once as a pure function and have both the census and production call it, so the divergence cannot reopen.
- Also caught in the same pass, same shape: `NOT (volume > 0 AND ...)` evaluates to **NULL**, not TRUE, when `volume IS NULL`, so every volume-less row silently fell through to the "good" branch of a `CASE`. In a corroboration gate that is precisely inverted — the *least* verifiable population was admitted as if corroborated. Any three-valued predicate used as an exclusion needs `coalesce(..., FALSE)`. The tell was a downstream count that disagreed with the census (10 instruments where the enumeration said 59), not the flag itself.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/full-population-ab.md` (census-matches-rule check); S7 verdict §10 on #2247 records the corrected numbers alongside the originals.

---

### A docstring that describes transaction semantics goes stale the moment those semantics are corrected — grep the phrasing, not the call site

- First seen in: #2261 (2026-08-04), PR #2263. Codex checkpoint 2 caught one instance; the review bot then found a SECOND one in the same file that the fix had not touched.
- Symptom: two docstrings in one module making contradictory claims about the same transaction. `refresh_price_quarantine` originally claimed per-instrument commits; `conn.transaction()` there is a SAVEPOINT, not a top-level commit, because the reads in `_series_batches` have already opened the implicit transaction. Codex flagged it, the docstring was corrected to "the whole run is one transaction" — and `_series_batches`'s own docstring, which said "the writes below commit per instrument", was left behind. A reader arriving at the generator first would take the false version as authoritative, and a future maintainer optimising it would preserve the wrong invariant.
- Root cause: the fix was scoped to the function Codex named. A transaction-shape claim is rarely written in only one place — it is explained wherever the *reason* for a structural choice is recorded, which is typically a helper, not the function that owns the transaction.
- Generalises to: any correction to a cross-cutting invariant stated in prose — transaction and locking shape, ordering guarantees, idempotency, "this is the only writer", fail-open vs fail-closed defaults. A review finding names ONE site; the claim usually lives in several.
- Prevention: when a review or a second-opinion pass corrects a semantic claim in a comment or docstring, **grep the module (and its tests) for the claim's distinctive phrasing before pushing the fix** — here `commit per instrument` / `one transaction` / `savepoint`. Fixing only the flagged line is how the second instance survives. Where the claim is genuinely cross-cutting, state it ONCE in the owning function and have the other sites point at it by name rather than re-describing it, so there is only one place to correct next time.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/price_quarantine_store.py` (`_series_batches` now defers to `refresh_price_quarantine` for the transaction shape instead of restating it).

---

### On a SPARSE table, the storage predicate is part of the census definition — and it is the part nobody reads

- First seen in: #2261 (2026-08-04), PR #2263 review WARNING. Second instance of the same shape in the same PR, both caught by someone other than the author.
- Symptom: a published figure that is wrong by three orders of magnitude while the census SQL is correct, the rule is correct, and the two agree with each other. `transitions_provisional_deferred` reported **16,907** against a stated rule that yields **3**. The query (`COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE provisional AND cardinality(rules) = 0)`) faithfully counted the rows present. The rows present were the problem.
- Root cause: the verdict tables are SPARSE — a row exists only when the evaluator judged it worth storing — so a third artefact sits between the rule and the census: the **`notable` predicate deciding what gets written**. `notable` treated `provisional` alone as sufficient, which stored every ordinary transition inside the trailing correction window. The census then counted exactly what it was given. Neither reading the rule nor reading the query reveals this; only reading all three together does.
- The earlier instance in the same PR: T3-ADMITTED transitions were not `notable`, so they were never written, so the corroboration census reported `spike: 1` instead of `34`. **A narrowing gate's denominator is the set it admitted** — storing only the rejected side makes the gate unmeasurable while every stored number stays internally consistent.
- Generalises to: any figure counted over a table whose rows are conditionally written — quarantine/exception tables, `*_observations` rows written only on change, audit rows written only on failure, "problems" lists. The tell is that the count is a fraction of a population the table does not itself contain, so the denominator lives somewhere else.
- Distinct from the "census must be the rule you wrote down" entry above: there the SQL disagreed with the prose. Here the SQL and the prose agree, and the WRITER disagrees with both.
- Prevention: when a census counts over a sparse table, **state the storage predicate next to the rule and check it as a third clause** — "which rows are written?" alongside "what does the rule say?" and "what does the query count?". A published figure with a name (`..._deferred`, `..._rejected`, `..._admitted`) must have a one-line answer to "what is excluded from this count, and is that exclusion the same one the name implies?". Cheapest check available: assert the figure against the number the PR description claims, on the full population, BEFORE writing the description — the 16,907-vs-3 gap was visible in the census output the whole time.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/full-population-ab.md`; `app/services/price_quarantine.py::TransitionVerdict.notable` (both rules stated in the docstring, with the counts each mistake produced); `tests/test_price_quarantine_rules.py::TestT3LevelBreak::test_admitted_transition_is_still_stored_as_census_evidence` and `TestProvisionalTrailingWindow::test_ordinary_provisional_transition_is_not_notable` pin both directions.

---

### A comparison arm where two candidates COINCIDE cannot discriminate them — and it looks like a clean pass

- First seen in: #2243 (2026-08-04), the US-equity arm of the WS `LastExecution` spike. Not caught by review; caught by having kept a second arm.
- Symptom: the US arm scored `ws-bid` **45/45** on full OHLC against eToro's own 1-minute candle, and `ws-last` **45/45** as well. Read alone, that is a 100% pass for both candidates and licenses either column. It is not evidence at all: on US equities eToro's markup is zero, so `last == bid` **exactly**, and the two "candidates" are one series wearing two names.
- The discriminating arm was FX, where the markup is non-zero: there `Bid` reproduced 4–7 of 9 closes and `LastExecution` reproduced **0 of 9, on every pair**. Same test, opposite verdict, and only the arm where the candidates separate carries any information.
- Near-miss: the spike's stated acceptance criterion was "`ws-bid` beating `ws-last`". On the US arm that criterion is unsatisfiable — they tie by construction — so a US-only run would have failed its own acceptance while actually confirming the verdict, or (worse, if read the other way) passed while proving nothing.
- Generalises to: any A/B where the two arms' inputs are equal on part of the population — a parser vs its replacement on filings both handle identically; two ID-resolution strategies on issuers with one identifier; a new scoring input on instruments where it is NULL. A tie on that subset is not agreement, it is absence of signal, and it inflates the apparent pass rate.
- Prevention: before running a discriminating comparison, **identify where the candidates are identical by construction and exclude that subset from the score — or state its size next to the result.** The question to ask is not "did the candidates agree?" but "on how many observations could they possibly have disagreed?". If that number is zero, the arm proves nothing regardless of the percentage it reports. Pair it with the existing "compare A/B arms only on entities measurable in ALL arms" rule (#2252): that one removes entities an arm cannot see, this one removes entities where the arms cannot differ.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/data-sources/etoro-api.md` (§WS rate semantics — the "keep an FX arm in any re-measurement" warning states the per-arm scores that make the tie legible).

### "Redundant now that X covers it" — check X's SCOPE, not just its existence

- First seen in: #2271 (measured 2026-08-04; defect introduced by #502, `d569af84`, ~3 months earlier). `fx_rates_refresh` dropped its Phase 2 batch-quote fetch with the rationale: *"The WS live-tick pipeline (#274) writes to the `quotes` table directly for every instrument an SSE stream subscribes to, making the batch-quote path redundant for visibility-driven workflows."* Every clause of that is true. It is also the whole bug: the WS is visibility-driven **by design** (#498 — "Boots quiet: a fresh process authenticates the WS but sends no Subscribe frame until an SSE stream lands"), so it writes nothing unless a human has the page open. The replacement's scope was a strict subset of the removed writer's, and the qualifier that says so — "for visibility-driven workflows" — was sitting in the sentence.
- Blast radius: `quotes` was left with NO scheduled writer at all. Its only other writer, `market_data._upsert_quote`, is reachable only via `refresh_market_data(..., skip_quotes=False)`, and every caller in the repo passes `True` — so that path was unreachable in production too. Meanwhile eight headless consumers read the table (`scoring`, `portfolio`, `execution_guard`, `position_monitor`, `valuation`, `transaction_cost`, `coverage`, the quotes-gated valuation view). Measured before the fix: **107 rows for a ~12k universe**, `quoted_at` scattered across weeks (1 row from 07-21, 1 from 07-23 …) — the signature of a table that only records what someone happened to look at. All five held positions were being marked, spread-gated and scored from quotes **14h41m to 22h12m old**.
- Why nothing caught it for three months: no test asserts that a table has a writer, every reader kept returning rows, and the job that stopped writing them still reported success — it just does something else now. The stale comment at the *other* end (`scheduler.py`, "quote freshness is owned by the hourly fx_rates_refresh job") was the only artefact pointing at the gap, and it pointed at an owner that had retired.
- Prevention: when removing a writer/fetch/job because "Y now covers it", **write down Y's scope and diff it against what you are removing.** Specifically: (1) does Y fire on the same trigger, or does it need a human/request/event the old path did not? (2) enumerate the CONSUMERS of the data and check each against Y's scope — a consumer set is a grep, not a guess; (3) if the rationale contains a qualifier ("for visibility-driven workflows", "for the tiers we score", "when the operator is active"), that qualifier IS the gap — name who falls outside it. Self-review prompt for a table that matters: `grep -rn "INSERT INTO <table>"` and confirm at least one writer is reachable from a scheduled path with no human in the loop.
- Corollary — two writers of the same row must agree on the ordering guard. `etoro_websocket.upsert_quote` was monotonic (`WHERE quotes.quoted_at IS NULL OR EXCLUDED.quoted_at >= quotes.quoted_at`); `market_data._upsert_quote` overwrote unconditionally. Harmless only because the second was unreachable — restoring it on a schedule would have let an hourly REST snapshot clobber a live tick mid-session. Before making a dormant writer live, diff its conflict clause against the live writer's.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/market-data/SKILL.md` (two-writer invariant + "no reader bounds `quoted_at`"); #2271 adds the `quotes_refresh` job, the monotonicity guard, and tests pinning registry + invoker + non-5-min-aligned slot.

### `lsof` against the uvicorn pids cannot see the app's sockets under `--reload`

- First seen in: #2271. The ticket opened with "The API process holds ZERO outbound sockets, so the WS subscriber … is not connected", and the first diagnosis pass repeated it after re-measuring the same way. Both were wrong. Under `uvicorn --reload` the application runs in a **multiprocessing-spawned worker**, which `pgrep -f uvicorn` does not match — it matches the `uv run uvicorn` parent and the uvicorn supervisor, neither of which holds application sockets. Measured: `pid=60224 etoro_sockets=1` (spawned worker), `pid=61071 etoro_sockets=0`, `pid=61124 etoro_sockets=0`. `ws.etoro.com` resolves to `104.18.34.56`/`172.64.153.200`, and the established socket to that address was on the worker the entire time.
- Why it is dangerous: the measurement fails in the direction that manufactures a bug. Zero sockets reads as "the subsystem is dead", which is a compelling, specific, actionable — and false — finding, and it points the investigation away from the real defect. Here it nearly cost the whole ticket: the actual problem (`quotes` has no scheduled writer) has nothing to do with connectivity.
- Prevention: to check whether the app holds a socket, find the process that **owns the application object**, not the process named in the command line — `pgrep -f "multiprocessing.spawn"` alongside the server name, or resolve the target host and grep every pid for its address (`lsof -nP -i TCP | grep <resolved-ip>`). Better: ask the app. `GET /_debug/etoro-ws` reports `ws_state` (CONNECTING/OPEN/CLOSING/CLOSED) from inside the process that owns the connection, which no external process inspection can get wrong. General rule: **prefer an in-process liveness report over external process inspection**, and when an external measurement says a subsystem is dead, confirm it in-process before writing it into a ticket.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/api/_debug_ws.py` (`ws_state` field + the comment recording this trap).

### A correct measurement of the WRONG POPULATION is more dangerous than a wrong measurement

- First seen in: #2240 TA strategy spec (2026-08-04, caught by the operator, not by any check). The spec declared two hard constraints — *"survivorship bias is unfixable for historical backtests"* and *"~4 years of history is the hard per-instrument ceiling"* — and built its whole strategy catalogue, sequencing and expectations around them. Both figures were **measured correctly**: 5 of 12,691 non-tradable instruments have price history; no instrument exceeds ~1,059 bars. Both conclusions were **wrong**, because the population measured was *our Postgres corpus* and the conclusion drawn was about *the market*. The eToro API had been silently treated as the boundary of the obtainable.
- What was actually available, found in ~20 minutes once the question was asked: SEC EDGAR full-index (`Archives/edgar/full-index/{year}/QTR{n}/form.idx`) lists Form **25** / **25-NSE** delisting notices — 622 in 2023 QTR1 alone, CIK + date, back to 1993, free, using fetch discipline the repo already has. Public price feeds retain delisted names (`FRCB`, First Republic, failed May 2023: 3,933 bars through 2026-08-03, last close $0.0004). Public history runs ~10× deeper (AAPL 11,502 bars to 1980-12-12 vs our 1,059). And the two price series proved interchangeable for TA — return correlation 0.963–0.996, median RSI-14 difference 0.12–0.19 points, SMA-200 regime agreement 99.4–100%, with a consistent −0.15% level bias that is just the Bid half-spread.
  - ⚠ **Corrected 2026-08-05 (#2284).** Two of those four are wrong, and the next entry is about why. "622 in 2023 QTR1" counts index **rows**, not filings — EDGAR indexes each 25-NSE under both the exchange CIK and the issuer CIK, so QTR1 is **329 filings**. And "public price feeds retain delisted names" is **false at cohort scale**: over all 382 common-equity delistings of 2023, the free feed serves **0%** whose series stops at the delisting (87.4% absent; the 12.6% that resolve are the ticker's *current* occupant). The history-depth and series-interchangeability findings both stand.

### A free "archive" is not an independent source — fingerprint it before you trust it

- First seen in: #2284 (2026-08-05). Searching for a survivorship-free price corpus turned up several free bulk archives, and the obvious read was "the data is out there, we just have to download it". Measured: **every free source is Yahoo, or downstream of Yahoo.** The Hugging Face archive `paperswithbacktest/Stocks-Daily-Price` (525 MB, 25.8M rows, 7,693 symbols, 1962→2026) names no upstream source on its dataset card and scored **92.4% absent / 0 clean stops** on the 382-name Form 25 cohort — *worse* than Yahoo itself. The proof it is a Yahoo scrape: for every cohort name present in both, the **first-bar date matched exactly, 29 of 29 (100%)**, including Yahoo's idiosyncratic artefacts (`ATCX` first bar 2026-01-09, `SAFE` 1989-11-16 — iStar's history under Safehold's ticker). Microsoft `qlib`'s US bundle is literally `scripts/data_collector/yahoo/collector.py`; `defeatbeta/yahoo-finance-data` is named for its source; the popular Kaggle sets are Quandl-WIKI or Yahoo derived.
- Why it is dangerous: a repackaged feed **launders provenance**. It arrives as a clean Parquet file with a row count and a date range and no visible lineage, so it reads as corroboration of the original rather than a copy of it. Two sources agreeing is normally evidence; here it is the same source counted twice. It also silently transfers the upstream's licence problem — Yahoo's ToS §2.4(i) — to a file whose own licence field reads `"other"`.
- The tell: the dataset card says what the data *is* and never says where it came from. Vendors with genuinely independent collection say so, loudly, because it is the expensive part.
- Prevention: before treating any free archive as a source, **fingerprint it against the incumbent** — pull the same per-symbol scalar from both (first-bar date works well; it is idiosyncratic, stable, and not something two independent collectors would agree on to the day) and compare exactly. Identity across the pathological cases means it is a copy. Corollary for evaluation order: **size the API against the corpus before assessing coverage or licence** — `instruments × history ÷ rate limit` disqualifies most candidates in one arithmetic step (Alpha Vantage free = 25 req/day = **507 days** for one pass over our 12,684; Marketstack free = 100 req/month and 1 year deep). Bulk history is a transfer problem, not an access problem.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/data-sources/research-price-corpus.md` (the measured landscape, the fingerprint technique, and the 382-name acceptance test to run before adopting any vendor).

### A CORRECTION is a claim too — it needs the same population discipline as the thing it corrects

- First seen in: #2240 §0 → #2284 (2026-08-05). The entry directly above records a wrong-population error and the correction that fixed it. **The correction repeated the same failure mode.** To refute *"survivorship is unfixable"* it asserted *"public price feeds retain delisted names"* — evidenced by **one hand-picked ticker**, `FRCB`. Population of one. Measured properly in #2284 against every 2023 Form 25 common-equity delisting (382 tickers, issuer from the filing XML, symbol from the pre-delisting cover-page `dei:TradingSymbol`): **334 (87.4%) absent entirely; 48 (12.6%) resolve; 0 (0.0%) have a series that stops at the delisting.** Of the 48, ten begin *after* the delisting date — `SI` (Silvergate Capital, failed 2023) now returns a series starting 2025-07-31, a different company on the ticker. `FRCB` was the 9.9% OTC-continuation case, i.e. the single most flattering example available.
- Why it is dangerous: a correction arrives with momentum. It has just overturned something, the operator has just been proved right, and the relief of "we are not blocked after all" is doing argumentative work that the evidence is not. The original error got caught because someone challenged the framing; the correction got a **merged spec, two follow-up tickets and a prevention-log entry** built on it before anyone asked how many tickers `FRCB` was standing in for.
- The tell: the correcting claim was stated with the same universal grammar as the claim it replaced — *"public price feeds retain delisted names"*, not *"one delisted name I checked was retained"*. Same sentence shape as *"survivorship is unfixable"*. **The grammar of a claim should match the breadth of its evidence**, and in both directions.
- Prevention: when a constraint is overturned, hold the replacement to the standard the original failed. Concretely: (1) a refutation by example refutes *"always"* and establishes nothing about *"generally"* — one counter-example is enough to kill "impossible" and is never enough to establish "available"; (2) before the correction propagates into a spec or a ticket, run it on the full population, which for this class means building the real cohort (here: EDGAR full-index → rule-provision filter → cover-page symbol resolution, ~40 minutes) rather than reusing the example that prompted the insight; (3) treat the *first* example as the most flattering case available, because it usually is — it was found by looking for a case that works.
- Corollary — this compounds with the existing rule that the spec's own §5 gates cannot catch it. Both the original constraint and its correction passed every arithmetic check in the document. What separates them from the truth is not accuracy, it is **n**.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `docs/proposals/ta/strategy-catalogue-and-backtest-validity.md` §0.1 (the correction-to-the-correction, in situ next to the claims it supersedes); `.claude/skills/data-sources/sec-edgar.md` §Form 25 (the cohort-construction recipe, so the next person builds the population rather than picking an example).
- Why it is dangerous: **every number checks out.** A wrong measurement gets caught by re-measuring; a right measurement of the wrong population survives every arithmetic check, every review that verifies the figures, and hardens into a design constraint. This one had already shaped a merged spec's catalogue, its sequencing, and its explicit lowering of what the milestone could achieve. A reviewer verifying the figures would have confirmed all of them.
- The tell, in hindsight: the constraint was stated as a property of *the world* (`"survivorship is unfixable"`, `"the hard ceiling"`) while every supporting query read one internal table. Whenever a sentence about what is *possible* is supported only by `SELECT … FROM <our table>`, the scope is unexamined.
- Prevention: before writing a constraint into a spec as fundamental, **name the population the measurement covers and ask whether it is the population the claim is about.** Specifically: (1) if the claim is about what data *exists* or what is *achievable*, an internal query cannot establish it — check whether an external or authoritative source supplies it (for this repo: EDGAR full-index by form type, public market data, the vendor landscape) before declaring the limit; (2) distinguish "our provider does not give us X" from "X is unavailable" — the first is a procurement question, the second is a law of nature, and only one of them is a constraint; (3) state the boundary explicitly in the doc ("measured on our corpus; not a statement about available market data") so a later reader can see what was and was not checked. Self-review prompt: grep a spec's constraint statements for the words *unfixable / impossible / hard ceiling / cannot*, and for each one confirm a source outside our own database was checked.
- Corollary — this is the inverse of the existing "verify before asserting" rule and needs its own check. That rule guards against asserting *without* evidence. This one fires when there IS evidence, it is accurate, and it answers a narrower question than the one being asked. Re-measuring does not catch it; only re-scoping does.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `docs/proposals/ta/strategy-catalogue-and-backtest-validity.md` §0 (the correction) and §10 (the lesson in situ); `.claude/skills/data-sources/etoro-api.md` (measured eToro-vs-public comparison + "never infer 'this history is unavailable' from `price_daily`"); `.claude/skills/ta-analyst/SKILL.md` (scope boundary).


## Positional indexing of a GROUP BY result crashes on the CLEAN case (#2282 2c, 2026-08-05)

- What happened: `--census` in `scripts/build_2282_form25_register.py` unpacked a `GROUP BY resolved` result as `bias[0]` / `bias[1]`, assuming both a resolved and an unresolved group always exist. Correct for the 2023 cohort; `IndexError` for any year whose cohort resolves completely.
- Why it is dangerous: the failure mode is **inverted**. Full, messy data produces both groups and the code works; a narrower or *healthier* slice produces one group and it raises. It therefore survives every run against the corpus it was developed on, and fails first in the situation everyone would call success.
- Prevention: never unpack a `GROUP BY` result positionally. Key it on the grouping value with an explicit zero default (`{bool(row[0]): ... }` + `.get(key, (0, 0))`), and guard the all-empty case separately. Applies to `GROUP BY status` / `provision_class` / `is_tradable` — anywhere a grouped result feeds a fixed-arity unpack.
- Related, same PR: `digits.lstrip("0").zfill(10) or None` — the `or None` was dead, because `"".zfill(10)` is `"0000000000"` and truthy. A guard that cannot fire reads as protection and is not. When a fallback depends on falsiness, check what the expression actually returns in the empty case.
- Enforced in: `.claude/skills/engineering/sql-correctness.md` ("Never index a GROUP BY result positionally").


## "Found nothing" and "could not look" collapse quietly — and they collapse in EVERY branch, not one (#2279, 2026-08-05)

- What happened: `app/services/price_structure.py` was written with an explicit, documented tri-state (`fired` / `not_fired` / `not_evaluable`) precisely to avoid the vacuous-truth class. Its module docstring names the hazard in the second numbered paragraph. It then got the distinction wrong in **three separate functions at once** — `detect_swings`, `cluster_levels` and `find_break_and_retest` — all in the same direction: when masked (quarantined) bars made the evidence undecidable and nothing was found, each returned `not_fired`. Caught by Codex at checkpoint 2; no test caught it, because every test asserted a *positive* path or a *too-short-series* path.
- The worst of the three was `find_break_and_retest`, which did not merely mislabel: a `not_evaluable` bar mid-sequence was skipped with `continue`, so a break before a quarantined bar could pair with a retest and confirmation after it. That **emits a positive signal spanning hidden evidence** — and the hidden bar could have been the opposite-side close that invalidated the setup.
- Why it is dangerous: the state is a *label on an empty result*, so nothing looks wrong. `swings == ()` is correct in both cases; only the accompanying word differs, and that word is what a phase-3 win-rate denominator reads. A quarantined bar then enters the statistics as an **observed miss** rather than as absent evidence — which is the exact defect the quarantine exists to prevent, arriving through the front door.
- The tell: **the author had already written the rule down.** Knowing the hazard and naming it in a docstring is not protection, because the decision is made independently at each `return` and each one looks locally reasonable. Three call sites, three chances, three misses.
- Prevention: **do not compute a tri-state at the call site.** Put it in one helper that takes both halves of the question — `_state_for(found: bool, *, blinded: bool)` — so "did anything fire" and "was any evidence hidden" cannot be answered in different places by different code. Then the rule is written once and every caller must supply the blinding signal explicitly. Test shape that catches it: for every detector, assert the **blinded-negative** case (evidence masked, nothing found → `not_evaluable`) *and* the **partially-blinded positive** (something found despite a mask → still `fired`). A suite that only tests happy paths and empty inputs will pass throughout.
- ⚠ **Extended after review round 1 of the same PR, which found a FOURTH instance the helper could not have caught.** `cluster_levels` took `swings: Sequence[Swing]` — so "the detector found no swings" and "the detector could not evaluate the series" both arrived as `()`, and the distinction was destroyed at the call boundary before the function ran. No care inside it could recover the state, and `_state_for` could not help because the blinding signal was never passed in. **A single helper is necessary and not sufficient: when a function's result depends on whether upstream could evaluate, take the upstream RESULT, not its payload.** Passing `SwingSeries` instead of `Sequence[Swing]` makes the collapse unrepresentable in the type, which is the only version of this fix that a future caller cannot undo by accident. Corollary for test helpers: a fixture that fabricates a plausible-looking status for hand-made input defeats the check — derive it from the same rule the producer uses, or the test asserts nothing.
- Generalises beyond this module: any function returning "result + status" where the status has a "could not evaluate" member. Check the SIGN of the failure — a masked input must never be able to produce a stronger claim than an unmasked one.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/price_structure.py::_state_for` (single helper + the rationale); `tests/test_price_structure.py` ("Blinded tri-state" section); `.claude/skills/ta-analyst/SKILL.md` (the tri-state is listed as load-bearing).

## A fix that is correct in isolation can break the thing it fixes, and only the REAL config shows it (#2286, 2026-08-05)

- What happened: `Settings` gained an `alias_generator` so every field accepts both `EBULL_<NAME>` and the bare `<NAME>` — the correct fix for six documented environment variables the app read with nothing. Tested against a synthetic empty `.env`: all six now resolve, bare names still work, suite green. Run against the **real** `.env` it moved `service_token` from a 64-character credential to `""`.
- Mechanism: the working `.env` had a blank `EBULL_SERVICE_TOKEN=` (copied from `.env.example` and never filled) *above* a bare `SERVICE_TOKEN=<value>`. A blank variable is **present**, so it won the `AliasChoices` race, and the real token two lines below was never consulted. Fixed with `env_ignore_empty=True`.
- Why it is dangerous: **no fixture has that duplicate.** It exists only because a human copied an example file and filled in one of two spellings — which is the single most likely state for any real `.env` to be in, and the least likely state for a test to construct. Every synthetic test passed. The failure would have surfaced as bearer-token auth breaking on the next restart, with the merge that "fixed environment variable handling" as the last thing anyone would suspect.
- Second instance in the same change, opposite direction: `alias_generator` without `populate_by_name=True` makes a `validation_alias` **replace** the field name as an input key, so `Settings(database_url=...)` was silently discarded by `extra="ignore"`. That is the ticket's own defect — a value you set that does nothing and says nothing — reintroduced one layer up by its fix. No test constructed `Settings` with field kwargs, so the suite was silent; Codex found it at checkpoint 2.
- Prevention: **a config change is verified against the real config file, not a fixture.** After any `app/config.py` change, instantiate `Settings()` against the real `.env`, `git stash` the change, instantiate again, and diff the security-relevant fields. Print **lengths and booleans, never values**. Generalises past config: whenever a change alters how *inputs are resolved* (aliases, precedence, defaults, fallback order), the bug lives in input states that only production has — duplicates, blanks, stale keys, both-spellings-present — and a fixture author does not think to build those.
- Corollary: when adding an alias or precedence rule, enumerate what happens when **both** sources are present and one is empty. "First match wins" and "first non-empty match wins" differ only on inputs nobody writes a test for.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/CLAUDE.md` ("A CONFIG change is verified against the REAL config file"); `app/config.py` (`env_ignore_empty` / `populate_by_name` comments record the measured before/after); `tests/test_config_env_contract.py::test_field_name_kwargs_still_construct_settings`.

### A widened CHECK constraint applies clean while silently dropping a member (#2218, 2026-08-05)

- Symptom: `sql/254` widened `job_runs_status_check` to add `'degraded'`. The new list was written by copying the most recent rewrite that turned up in a grep — `sql/020` — which predates `sql/137`, so it **dropped `'cancelled'`**. Four migrations had rewritten that one constraint (014 → 020 → 137 → 254) and only the database held the union. The migration applied with no error and no warning, because `select status, count(*) from job_runs group by 1` on the dev DB returns only `success` / `skipped` / `failure` / `running` — there was no cancelled row for Postgres to validate against. The constraint would then have rejected the next cancelled run, at the moment a job was cancelled, in a code path nobody was watching.
- Why it is dangerous: Postgres validates a new CHECK against **existing rows only**, so the check that would have caught this is exactly the one the absence of the state prevents. It is silent in the direction that hides it, and it hardens: the wrong constraint is now the newest migration, so the next person copying "the latest one" inherits the loss. Nothing in lint, typecheck, the fast tier or the DB tier exercises a status that no row currently has.
- The tell: `grep -rn "<constraint_name>" sql/*.sql` returning **more than two** files. A constraint rewritten repeatedly is one whose current definition is not in any single file.
- Prevention: build the new list from the **live constraint**, never from a migration file — `select pg_get_constraintdef(oid) from pg_constraint where conname = %s`. Then make it not depend on care: add a contract test that parses the constraint out of the migration text and asserts the Python constants equal it (`tests/test_job_terminal_status_contract.py`), so a future member cannot be added in one place and missed in the others. ⚠ The same test must also cover the **read** side — the vocabulary lived in five hand-spelled SQL `IN (...)` filters across four modules, and pyright cannot see inside a query string, so adding a member type-checked clean while three latest-terminal-run queries silently excluded it. A `degraded` row would have been written and then skipped in favour of an older, greener run: the ticket shipped and nothing changed.
- Caught by: Codex checkpoint 2, both findings, before first push. No gate saw either, and no test would have.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/sql-correctness.md` ("Widening a CHECK constraint" + "A closed vocabulary lives in the READ paths too"); `tests/test_job_terminal_status_contract.py`; `app/services/ops_monitor.py::TERMINAL_STATUS_SQL`.

### A Python validator "mirroring" a SQL CHECK admitted a state the CHECK rejects (#2240 3c, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `LedgerRow.__post_init__` is written to mirror `strategy_signals`' constraints so a bad row fails at construction with a named field rather than as an integrity error mid-batch. The fill constraint was mirrored as `has_fill = fill_bar_date is not None and fill_price is not None` / `if (verdict == "fired") != has_fill`. A `not_fired` row carrying **only** a `fill_bar_date` scores `has_fill = False`, matches `verdict != "fired"`, and **passes** — while `strategy_signals_fill_matches_verdict` requires BOTH fill columns NULL on a non-fired row and rejects it.
- Why it is dangerous: the defect is in the direction that makes it invisible. Every test written for the mirror used a WHOLE fill or NO fill, because that is what the writer produces — `resolve_fills` sets both fields together or neither, so no test input could ever reach the half-fill state. The mirror looked exhaustive, passed a nine-case rejection table, and the only observable difference from a correct mirror is a state the happy path cannot construct. The cost is not a wrong row (the DB still rejects it) but the loss of the mirror's entire point: the two no longer agree, so a caller learns about the disagreement as an aborted batch rather than a named field.
- The tell: an `and` / `or` between two nullity tests standing in for a SQL CHECK that names the columns separately. `a IS NOT NULL AND b IS NOT NULL` is three states in SQL (both, neither, exactly one) and two in the Python expression.
- Prevention: **count the fields, do not AND them** — `(a is not None) + (b is not None)` compared against the arity the verdict requires (0 or 2). Then test the arity-1 cases explicitly, since the writer cannot generate them: `TestLedgerRowRejects` now parametrises "only a fill date" and "only a fill price" on both a fired and a non-fired row, and the revert probe injects the ANDed form and confirms exactly those cases fail.
- Caught by: Codex checkpoint 2, before first push. Neither the type checker, the nine-case rejection table, nor a 6.7M-bar full-population sweep could see it — the sweep exercises the writer, and the writer never emits a half fill.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/signal_ledger.py::LedgerRow.__post_init__` (comment carries the failing expression).
- ⚠ **Update (#2240 4b, 2026-08-06) — the arity-1 test only detects the ANDed form on ONE SIDE of the predicate, and writing it on the other side produces a probe that reports CAUGHT while proving nothing.** The mirror is `count(fields) == (N if <predicate> else 0)`. Injecting the ANDed form and testing it on a row where the predicate is TRUE still raises — one field absent scores `False` against an expected `True` — so the test passes, the probe says CAUGHT, and the defect is untouched. It is only on rows where the predicate is FALSE that the ANDed form silently accepts, because half a pair also scores `False`. Concretely on `strategy_outcomes`: half a booked pair must be tested on an `ambiguous`/`unresolved` row (predicate false), not on a `tp_hit` one; half an exit location must be tested on an `unresolved` row, not on `ambiguous`. Both wrong-side tests were written first here and would have shipped a green probe. **Rule: for every counted-arity mirror, the rejection case goes on the class where the required arity is ZERO.** Enforced in `tests/test_outcome_ledger.py::TestOutcomeRowRejects` (comment names the asymmetry) and `scripts/probe_2240_outcome_ledger.py` (probes 1-2 select exactly those tests). The exhaustive Python-vs-Postgres matrix in `tests/test_outcome_ledger_db.py` catches the class regardless of which side anyone remembers, which is why it is enumerated rather than sampled.

### A derived artefact's key must carry the version of every pipeline that can change it, not just the deriving module's own (#2240 4b, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `strategy_outcomes` was specced with the key `(signal_id, rule_set_version)` — the signal, plus the source hash of `outcome_resolver.py`. The argument was the ledger's own and is sound as far as it goes: a changed execution assumption must store a second outcome beside the first rather than overwrite it. What it misses is that **the resolver is not the only thing that decides the outcome.** Its INPUTS do too: `load_masked_series` masks high/low/close per `price_quarantine`'s rule set, and the resolver's masking contract is that an absent field REFUSES. Re-run the quarantine under a changed rule set and the same signal can resolve `unresolved(quarantined_bar)` instead of `tp_hit` with `outcome_resolver.py` byte-identical.
- Why it is dangerous: the failure is not "the provenance is unrecorded", which would be a documentation problem. It is that the corrected outcome is **UNSTORABLE**. The re-resolution collides on the two-part key; the table deliberately has no `ON CONFLICT` (a re-run must not overwrite a recorded classification); so the only way to write the correction down would be to edit the resolver's source so its hash moves — i.e. to lie about which module changed. A key that cannot express a legitimate second row does not merely lose information, it forces the next person to corrupt the identity scheme to get their work done.
- The tell: a versioned key on a derived table where the pipeline feeding it is ALSO versioned and that version is not in the key. Ask "what else, if it changed, would change this row?" — if the answer is a rule set with a `RULE_SET_VERSION` constant of its own, it belongs in the key. Here `price_quarantine.RULE_SET_VERSION` gates the loader and is stamped on the quarantine tables, so it was already a first-class version elsewhere in the same read path.
- Prevention: `strategy_outcomes` keys on `(signal_id, rule_set_version, input_rule_set_version)`, the third being the version stamp of the pipeline that produced the BARS.
