It is `TEXT NOT NULL` with no vocabulary CHECK (a version string is open by nature, exactly like `strategy_version`), a required writer argument with no default (#2288), and rejected when blank — `NOT NULL` does not catch a present-but-empty version, which is the #2286 shape. ⚠ Corollary for consumers, written into the table comment because it does not follow from the key alone: **every aggregate must pin ONE `(rule_set_version, input_rule_set_version)` pair**, since versions coexist by design and an unpinned `count(*) FILTER (WHERE outcome = 'tp_hit')` counts one trade twice.
- Caught by: Codex checkpoint 1, on the spec, before any code. No gate could have — the schema was internally consistent, every test would have passed, and the defect only surfaces the first time somebody re-runs the quarantine.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `sql/256_strategy_outcomes.sql` (header + `input_rule_set_version` column comment); `app/services/outcome_ledger.py` (module docstring); `tests/test_outcome_ledger_db.py::TestUniqueness` (parametrised over both version members).

### A guard looked redundant because a NULL-propagating sibling clause masked it on every payload the test used (#2240 4b, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `store_outcomes` writes through `INSERT … SELECT … FROM strategy_signals WHERE s.signal_kind = 'entry' AND s.verdict = 'fired' AND (exit_bar_date IS NULL OR exit_bar_date >= s.fill_bar_date)`. The revert probe that deletes `AND s.verdict = 'fired'` reported **NOT CAUGHT** against a test that offers a `not_fired` parent and expects a refusal. The test was right, the code was right, and the probe was right — the *test's payload* was the problem: it used a booked outcome, so `exit_bar_date` was set, and a `not_fired` signal has a NULL `fill_bar_date` (`strategy_signals_fill_matches_verdict` guarantees it). `<date> >= NULL` is NULL, so the surviving clause refused the row anyway and the verdict clause never had to do anything.
- Why it is dangerous: the clause is load-bearing on exactly the shape the test did not use. An `unresolved` outcome has `exit_bar_date IS NULL`, the fill-date clause short-circuits on `IS NULL`, and `verdict = 'fired'` is then the ONLY thing between an unfilled signal and a stored outcome — 7,780 of the 103,041 rows the full-population arm produced are `unresolved`. Had the probe been "fixed" by deleting the clause as redundant, or by retargeting it at a test that happened to pass, a real hole would have shipped with a green harness behind it.
- The tell: a probe that reports NOT CAUGHT on a clause you can talk yourself into believing is redundant. Also: a compound predicate where one conjunct can be NULL for the very rows the other conjunct exists to reject — SQL's three-valued logic makes "this clause never fires" and "this clause fires and is masked" look identical from the outside.
- Prevention: **when a probe reports NOT CAUGHT, enumerate the payload shapes the selected test can produce before touching anything** — the prevention-log rule already says read the injected source, and this is its second half: read the test's *inputs*, not only the code. Concretely, a test for a cross-table predicate must cover each nullity shape of every column that predicate mentions. `tests/test_outcome_ledger_db.py::test_an_unresolved_outcome_cannot_attach_to_an_unfilled_parent` is that case, and the probe's selector points at it with a comment saying why the general test is not enough.
- Caught by: `scripts/probe_2240_outcome_ledger.py` on its first run. No gate could have — every test passed, the full-population arm passed, and the clause is not dead code.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/probe_2240_outcome_ledger.py` (probe 5's selector comment); `tests/test_outcome_ledger_db.py` (the test's docstring carries the NULL-propagation reasoning).

### A 40x safety margin on a 3-series sample was 193 failures on the full corpus (#2240 2a, 2026-08-05)

- Symptom: `bollinger_series` computes a rolling variance. The O(1) one-pass form (`sumsq/n - mean^2`) is a known cancellation hazard on price data, so rather than assume, it was **measured** against the shipped two-pass form over **48,707 real bars from the three deepest corpus series**: max band difference **2.4e-11** against the module's **1e-9** equivalence tolerance — a 40× margin. Shipped on that evidence. The full-corpus acceptance sweep then failed it: **193 mismatches on `bb_upper` and 193 on `bb_lower`** across 7,354 series. The three sampled series were large caps; the corpus contains high-priced low-volatility names where `mean^2` dwarfs the variance and the subtraction eats the significant digits. A synthetic fixture at base=100,000 / amplitude=0.001 reproduces a **3.3e-03** error — six orders of magnitude past tolerance.
- Why it is dangerous: the sample was not small (48,707 bars), not cherry-picked (the three *deepest* series, chosen for coverage), and the margin was not marginal (40×). Every instinct that normally says "this is enough evidence" was satisfied. What the sample could not contain was the *regime* where the failure lives — and no amount of additional bars from the same three instruments would have found it, because the hazard is a property of the price level and volatility, not of sample size.
- ⚠ The author had already written "that's 3 series though, so the full-corpus sweep is what actually gates it" **in the same breath as shipping it**. Knowing a check is the real gate does not substitute for running it before you rely on the result.
- Prevention: for any numerical shortcut, the acceptance sweep is not a confirmation step, it is **the** evidence — do not commit the shortcut on sample evidence and let the sweep confirm afterwards. And when reverting, leave a **fast local reproduction** so the next person meets the wall in milliseconds rather than nine minutes: `TestBollingerNumericalStability` uses a high-price low-amplitude fixture and is revert-probed (injecting the one-pass form fails exactly those two cases and nothing else).
- Related: this is the same defect class as #2260, where a sampled measurement produced a 27-point phantom edge — and it was committed by the author *while implementing the phase built to prevent it*.
- **Standing rule for this module (review PREVENTION, #2310):** any new O(1) running accumulation over a rolling window — sum, mean, count, anything — must be paired with a full-corpus equivalence re-run OR a synthetic edge-case test **before merge**, not after. Sample evidence does not clear it.
- ⚠ **And check the TOLERANCE before concluding the accumulator is at fault.** Applying this rule to the SMA running sum immediately after found the opposite result: relative error ~1-2 ULP at every magnitude (1.4e-16 at base 1e2, 2.4e-16 at 1e9), i.e. as accurate as float64 permits. The alarming 2.4e-07 *absolute* figure was one ULP at that magnitude, which `technical_analysis.sma` also cannot beat. An intermediate commit added periodic re-seeding to "fix" it — cargo cult, reverted. The real defect was the harness's **absolute** 1e-9 on a corpus holding a close of 3.0e17: a tolerance the reference itself cannot meet either tests nothing or fails everything. Now relative (1e-12) with an absolute floor for near-zero closes, and verified still to catch the one-pass Bollinger bug by 2-3 orders of magnitude.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/indicator_series.py::bollinger_series` and `::sma_series` (both comments carry their measured numbers); `tests/test_indicator_series.py::TestBollingerNumericalStability` + `::TestRunningSumPrecision`; `scripts/verify_2240_indicator_series.py` (full corpus, relative tolerance, no sampling flag by design).

### A revert probe whose replacement is semantically identical passes every guard and proves nothing (#2240 4a, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: the phase-4a probe harness injects a defect by string replacement, asserts the anchor occurs **exactly once** first (the standing rule, because a probe that silently matches nothing proves nothing), then requires the named test to FAIL. The probe for "truncation absorbed into `expired`" replaced `return "window_truncated"` with `return None if False else "window_truncated"`. The anchor matched exactly once, the file changed, the harness ran — and the test still passed, because the replacement **returns the same value**. Reported as `*** NOT CAUGHT ***`, which read as a hole in the test rather than a hole in the probe.
- Why it is dangerous: `assert count == 1` is a guard against matching *nothing*. It says nothing about whether the replacement changes behaviour, and the failure presents as a *missing test* — so the natural reaction is to write another test for behaviour that is already covered, or to weaken the code until the probe "works". Both make things worse. It is the mutation-testing equivalent of an equivalent mutant, except here it was written by hand and believed.
- The tell: a probe whose replacement keeps the original expression anywhere inside it (`X` → `f(X)`, `X` → `cond and X`, `X` → `A if False else X`), or one that flips a branch the test input never reaches.
- Prevention: a probe must **delete or invert** the behaviour, not wrap it — and when one reports NOT CAUGHT, read the injected source before touching the test. ⚠ Some invariants cannot be broken at a single site: "truncation absorbed into `expired`" needed the window clamp AND the exit-index clamp changed together, because either alone still refuses. So the harness takes a **list** of (anchor, replacement) pairs per probe and requires every anchor to be unique. That a probe needs two sites is itself evidence the two guards are load-bearing together, which is worth knowing before somebody removes one as redundant.
- Caught by: the harness's own NOT CAUGHT report, on the first run. No gate would have — a probe script is not a test, and CI never sees it.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/test-quality.md` ("Revert-probing"); `scripts/probe_2240_outcome_resolver.py` (multi-site probes, per-anchor uniqueness assertion) — committed rather than left in `/tmp`, because an "enforced in" pointing at a scratch file names nothing, and the next person to touch the resolver would have to re-derive nine injections from prose.

### A branch commented "unreachable" was reachable ~3,900 times, and its label asserted something false about the data (#2240 4a, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: the phase-4a distribution harness skipped a hypothetical entry when `not stop < entry < target`, behind the comment *"Unreachable given the guards above — `_compute_stop_loss` clamps to at least 2% below entry and a positive ATR puts the target above it"*, and filed the skip under `atr_not_positive`. It is reachable, and the ATR is **positive** every time — so the label asserted something false about the corpus, in the census that criterion 9 exists to make honest.
- Root cause, and it is not a precision curiosity: **a flat price run**. On a run of bars with `open = high = low = close` the true range is 0 every bar, and Wilder smoothing (`atr = (13*atr_prev + tr)/14`) decays the ATR geometrically toward zero **without ever reaching it** in float. So `atr > 0` stays true while the ATR is economically zero — measured at 4.95e-22 against an entry of 1,651,206.625. Proximate cause: `Decimal` is arbitrary-**precision**, not infinite — the default context is 28 significant digits, so at that ratio `entry + tp_mult * atr` rounds straight back to `entry`.
- ⚠ **`atr > 0` is NOT a "this instrument has volatility" gate.** Anything downstream that filters on it — a strategy eligibility rule, a position sizer dividing by ATR — will admit flat runs. Named here because phase 5 is the next thing to write one.
- How it was caught, and this is the transferable part: **an assertion that every grid-independent skip reason is EQUAL across the parameter grid before printing one cell's count as if it stood for all of them.** The skip counts differed by 44 between the 1.0×ATR and 4.0×ATR cells, which a bar-and-ATR-only skip cannot do. That 44-signal *variation* is what exposed a ~3,900-signal *mislabelling* — the constant part was invisible, because a wrong label that is wrong uniformly looks exactly like a right one.
- ⚠ The generalisation: **a census that aggregates over a parameter grid should assert which of its buckets are grid-invariant.** Any bucket that varies when it should not is either a mislabelled cause or a real parameter dependence, and both are worth knowing. Collapsing the grid to one printed number hides the discriminator entirely.
- Prevention: no bucket in a rejection census may share a label with a different cause; a branch believed unreachable gets a distinct label anyway (if it is truly unreachable the count is 0 and costs nothing, and if it is not you find out); and grid-dependent buckets are printed PER CELL, never collapsed.
- Caught by: the harness's own cross-cell assertion, on the first full-corpus run. Not by any gate, and not by the 500-series partial run that preceded it — the divergence needs most of the corpus to show up.
- Reproduce (the counts above are this incident's evidence, not a live statistic — the harness computes them per cell so nothing goes stale by hand): `uv run python -m scripts.verify_2240_outcome_resolver --distribution`, then read the `levels_do_not_bracket` block. The flat-run example is `series_id = 2016` around `2018-07-20`; the query is in the `_levels` comment.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/verify_2240_outcome_resolver.py` (`_GRID_INDEPENDENT_SKIPS` / `_GRID_DEPENDENT_SKIPS`, the `levels_do_not_bracket` label, and the `_levels` comment carrying the reproducing query + command).

### An independent verifier that is only ACCIDENTALLY right — twice on one branch (#2240 4a, 2026-08-06)

- The pattern: a full-population equivalence arm re-derives an answer a second way and compares. Its value rests entirely on the second derivation being INDEPENDENT and COMPLETE. Twice on this branch the SQL arm was neither, and both times the full-corpus run was **clean anyway** — so the run passing is not evidence the arm is sound.
- **Instance 1 (mask tie-break, `f602a760`).** `_expected_class` treated a mask as decisive only when it fell strictly BEFORE the first touch (`<`). The resolver checks open/high/low for `None` before applying any rule, so a bar that is masked AND satisfies a touch predicate is refused — the mask wins the tie, and the correct comparison is `<=`. Unreachable on today's corpus because `range_usable = false` masks high and low together (so a masked bar matches neither predicate) and `select count(*) - count(open) from research_price_daily` is 0. Right by accident.
- **Instance 2 (the open missing from the touch filters).** SQL computed first-touch as `min(rn) FILTER (WHERE low <= stop)` / `(WHERE high >= target)`. The resolver's actual predicate is rules 1 OR 4 — `open <= stop OR low <= stop`. On a bar satisfying `low <= open <= high` the first disjunct implies the second, so the two agree — **but that identity is precisely what the harness's own acceptance 14 MEASURES rather than assumes.** The raw corpus holds 541 bars whose open lies outside `[low, high]`.
- ⚠ **The obvious stronger claim is FALSE, and I wrote it before checking** (commit `ea438eaa` says "the resolver can gap through at the open while a low/high-only filter sees nothing" — it cannot, today). All 541 of those bars are `range_usable = false` AND inside quarantine coverage, so the masked view both arms read contains **none** of them: acceptance 14 prints 0, and a masked bar refuses the touch test before any rule runs. Measured — `WITH bad AS (SELECT series_id, bar_date FROM research_price_daily WHERE open IS NOT NULL AND high IS NOT NULL AND low IS NOT NULL AND NOT (low <= open AND open <= high)) SELECT (SELECT count(*) FROM bad), (SELECT count(*) FROM bad b JOIN research_bar_quarantine q ON q.series_id = b.series_id AND q.bar_date = b.bar_date AND q.rule_set_version = <current> AND NOT q.range_usable)` returns **(541, 541)**. The correct statement is that the low/high-only form was right **by the quarantine's grace at this rule-set version**, not by construction — nothing makes the range rule target "open outside `[low, high]`" specifically, so a re-derivation that stops flagging one of these, or a caller using a different loader, breaks the implication silently. ⚠ **Catching the overstatement took one query, and I ran it only because acceptance 14 printed 0 where I expected 541.** A number that disagrees with your own claim is the cheapest correction available; the discrepancy was visible in the harness output all along.
- ⚠ **The tell, and it is the transferable part: the verifier assumed an invariant the SAME HARNESS was measuring rather than enforcing.** Acceptance 14 exists because `low <= open <= high` is definitional but not guaranteed (bar validity is `price_quarantine`'s, not this module's). Writing a comparison arm that silently relies on the very property you are measuring is circular, and a green run hides it completely.
- Prevention: when writing an independent arm, enumerate the source predicate DISJUNCT BY DISJUNCT against the implementation rather than reaching for the "equivalent" simplification — a simplification is a proof obligation, and it is usually resting on an invariant that holds *today*. If the arm relies on an invariant, either enforce it (exclude violating rows and count them) or encode the full predicate. ⚠ **A clean full-population run does NOT discharge this** — both instances were clean.
- Caught by: Codex at checkpoint 2, both times; no gate can see it, because the arm is not a test and its output is a number that looks right.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/verify_2240_outcome_resolver.py` (`_EQUIV_SQL`'s touch filters now carry the open, with the counting query in the comment; `_expected_class`'s `<=` tie-break).

### A flat fixture pins NEITHER operator in a conjunction, and two probes said so (#2240 S-1, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: S-1's entry rule is `close > sma_200 AND sma_50 > sma_200`, both strict per the parent spec. The boundary test was a flat series — `close == sma_50 == sma_200` — which reads as the obvious way to pin both `>` operators at once. The revert probes for "`close >=`" and "`sma_50 >=`" both reported `*** NOT CAUGHT ***`.
- Root cause: relaxing ONE comparison in an `AND` leaves the other one false, so the conjunction is still false and the verdict does not move. A fixture that makes *every* comparison an equality tests none of them separately. The same shape inverts for `OR`: a fixture where both disjuncts hold cannot see either operator.
- Prevention: **for `a > b AND c > d`, each operator needs its own fixture where THAT comparison is an equality and the other is strictly true.** Two further details made the fixtures evidence rather than approximation: (1) construct the equality **exactly** — integer closes whose window sums stay inside 2^53 make `sum/period` exact in float64, so `49*90 + 101*100 + 49*110 + 100 = 20,000` over 200 bars gives `sma_200 == close == 100` with `sma_50 == 109.8`, and a period-50 saw-tooth over 200 bars (four whole periods) gives `sma_50 == sma_200` exactly with the close far above both; (2) **assert the precondition inside the test**, so a later fixture edit that drifts off the boundary fails loudly instead of passing vacuously.
- Caught by: the probe harness's own NOT CAUGHT report, on its first run. No gate could have — the tests passed, and they look like the right tests.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/engineering/test-quality.md` ("A boundary defect inside a CONJUNCTION needs one fixture per comparison"); `tests/test_strategy_s1.py::TestStrictComparisons` (+ the `CLOSE_ON_SLOW` / `AVERAGES_EQUAL` fixture comments); `scripts/probe_2240_s1_momentum.py` (probes 1-2 carry the retargeting reason).

### A revert-probe harness silently contaminated a concurrent full-population run's version stamp (#2240 S-1, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: the S-1 full-population run printed `version strategy-registry-v1+7701ea87dced`. The module's actual hash was `9bfb0c60a3f2`. The two disagree because `scripts/probe_2240_s1_momentum.py` was run in the same window: it mutates the strategy source on disk, runs a test, and restores it — and `_source_hash()` **re-reads the file at call time**, while Python imported the module once at process start. The census happened to stamp itself during an injected-defect window.
- Why it is dangerous: the direction of the error is the bad one. The *behaviour* measured was clean (the import predates any probe), so every figure is right — and the **version string is wrong**, which is the field a later reader keys those figures on. A stamp naming a defect that never ran is worse than a wrong number, because nothing about it looks wrong.
- ⚠ It is not specific to probes. Anything that rewrites a hashed source during a long run does this: an editor save, a formatter, a sibling session. The rule "long-running work belongs in a worktree" covers the sibling case and does not cover your own harness.
- ⚠ **The obvious fix does not work, and it was written and shipped to Codex before being caught.** A start-of-run vs end-of-run hash comparison catches a *persistent* edit and misses the incident entirely: the probe RESTORES the file, so both ends agree and the run reports success with the injected version on it. Checkpoint 2 flagged it (P2). **A guard placed at the ends of a run cannot see a mutation that lives in the middle of it.**
- Prevention: **put the check where the READ is, against an anchor taken at import.** The only disk read is the version property, so `_stamped_version()` brackets that one call with `_source_hash() != _SOURCE_AT_IMPORT` on both sides, where `_SOURCE_AT_IMPORT` is captured microseconds after the module is loaded. A mutation cannot then be active for the stamp without being active for at least one check; and one active at import *and* at the stamp is not contamination at all — it is a run of the mutated code, correctly stamped. Generalises: any harness printing a version derived from a file hash must anchor on import time and check adjacent to the read, not at the run boundaries.
- Caught by: comparing the printed version against a freshly computed one while writing up the results — not by any gate, and not by the run itself, which exited 0. The inadequacy of the FIRST fix was caught by Codex at checkpoint 2.
- Verified: `_stamped_version()` returns `strategy-registry-v1+be94a31221c8` on the clean tree, raises `strategy source moved before stamping (expected 9bfb0c60a3f2)` with a byte appended to the module, and returns the original stamp again once restored.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/verify_2240_s1_momentum.py::_stamped_version` (+ `_SOURCE_AT_IMPORT`), whose docstring carries this incident and why the end-comparison form was wrong.

### A naive reference that IMPORTS the constant it checks is not independent (#2240 S-3, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `tests/test_strategy_s3.py` derives expected verdicts from a hand-written naive Wilder RSI, deliberately independent of the streaming `rsi_series` so that a window-boundary error or a seed off-by-one would have to occur in both to pass. The revert probe "the RSI period shifted by one" (`RSI_PERIOD = 14` → `13`) reported `*** NOT CAUGHT ***` against `test_every_bar_matches_the_naive_reference`, the one test whose entire purpose is to catch that.
- Root cause: the reference was fed the module's own constant — `from …s3_mean_reversion_in_trend import RSI_PERIOD` then `_reference_rsi(closes, RSI_PERIOD, i)`. Mutating the constant moved the reference with it, so both sides recomputed on 13 and every bar still agreed. **The independence was real for the ALGORITHM and absent for its PARAMETERS**, which is the half that gets no attention because the interesting code is the recursion. The same hole existed for `OVERSOLD_THRESHOLD`, `EXIT_THRESHOLD` and `TREND_PERIOD`.
- Prevention: **a reference implementation encodes the SPEC's numbers as literals, never imports the code's.** The question the test answers is "does the code implement the documented rule", and §4's rule contains `14`, `30`, `50` and `200` — so those are declared in the test file as `SPEC_*` constants sourced from the spec text, and the reference reads only those. Then add exactly one test that bridges the two (`assert s3_module.RSI_PERIOD == SPEC_RSI_PERIOD`, …) and let it be the ONLY place the module's constants are read as values — without it, a constant change makes every other test quietly wrong instead of failing one loudly. ⚠ Generalises past constants: any fixture value, tolerance or expected output imported from the module under test carries the same defect.
- Measured: with the literals in place, four probes that were previously impossible all report CAUGHT — period 14→13, oversold 30→35, trend 200→100, exit 50→60. Full harness 14/14.
- Caught by: the probe harness's own NOT CAUGHT report on its first run. No gate could have — the test passed, and it reads as exactly the right test. Same detection route as the S-1 conjunction entry above, which is now twice on one branch that the probe found a defect in the TEST rather than in the code.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `tests/test_strategy_s3.py` (the `SPEC_*` block carries the incident, `TestSpecConstants` is the bridge); `scripts/probe_2240_s3_mean_reversion.py` (probe 8's comment records why it exists).
- ⚠ Belongs in `.claude/skills/engineering/test-quality.md` beside "A boundary defect inside a CONJUNCTION needs one fixture per comparison" — not added there in this PR because the headless loop lacked write permission on `.claude/skills/**`. Copy it across on the next session that has it.

### Vectorising a Python loop DISCARDS the guard ordering the loop got for free (#2311, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: none in production — this one was anticipated while writing the diff and then proved with a revert probe, which is the honest provenance. It is logged because the class is invisible in review: the rewrite looks like a pure representation change and the arithmetic is identical.
- Root cause: `stochastic_series`'s Python form read `if nulls or close is None: unevaluable; else: k = 50.0 if hi == lo else …`. The flat-range convention was **structurally unreachable** for a bar with a NULL close, because the guard ran first and `continue`d. The numpy form is `np.where(span == 0.0, 50.0, k_at)`, and `np.where` evaluates BOTH branches and selects elementwise — there is no ordering left to inherit. On a flat window with a missing close, `span == 0.0` is True, so the convention wins and the bar gets a 50.0 oscillator reading it has no close to support. The correct form re-states the precedence explicitly: `np.where((span == 0.0) & ~np.isnan(close_at), 50.0, k_at)`.
- ⚠ Generalises past `np.where` to every branch-flattening rewrite — `np.select`, a boolean-mask assignment, a SQL `CASE` replacing an early `RETURN`, a ternary replacing a guard clause. **The test is: list the guards the loop `continue`d or `break`ed on, and confirm each one still gates the branches that came after it.** A short-circuit is a precedence relation, and flattening throws it away silently: nothing warns, nothing type-errors, and the fixture that would notice is the one where BOTH conditions are true at once.
- Measured: `scripts/probe_2311_indicator_vectorisation.py` probe 1 injects exactly that regression and reports CAUGHT against `test_a_flat_window_with_a_null_close_is_unevaluable_not_fifty`; the harness is 10/10. The corpus cannot see it — `research_price_daily` carries **0** bars with a NULL OHLC field (the sweep prints that count), so every unevaluable path on this module is fixture-covered and nothing else.
- ⚠ Second, cheaper trap in the same rewrite: `sliding_window_view` RAISES when the window exceeds the array, where the loop it replaced simply never entered its body. A 19-bar newly-listed instrument would abort a corpus sweep rather than report warm-up. New surface, not inherited surface — probes 3-5.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/indicator_series.py` (the ⚠ comment on the flat-range line states why the close guard is explicit); `scripts/probe_2311_indicator_vectorisation.py`; `tests/test_indicator_series.py::TestVectorisedWindowIndicators`.

### The review's "changes since last push" was diffing against the PR MERGE REF, so sibling merges to main arrived as your work (#2322, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: PR #2322 (S-3) drew review findings for two consecutive rounds against `scripts/autonomy/ta_loop.sh` and `scripts/autonomy/loop_status.sh` — files the branch does not touch. Round 4 was a **REQUEST CHANGES** entirely composed of them, which under the loop's own "never merge a round carrying your rebuttal" rule left a finished, green PR stuck. The natural reading — and the one I wrote first — was that the bot hallucinated a file. It did not.
- Root cause, and it is in the workflow, not the model: `actions/checkout` on a `pull_request` event checks out **`refs/pull/N/merge`** — a synthetic merge of the PR head into the *current* base. `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` then computed the incremental diff as `git diff "$BEFORE_SHA"..HEAD`, and that `HEAD` is the merge commit. So the range spans this push's commits **plus every commit merged to main since the previous push**, presented under the heading *"Incremental diff (changes since last push). Focus on changes in this push."* #2323 merged mid-flight; its six files became "this push".
- Measured, from the round-4 run log and reproduced locally against a real merge commit built with `git commit-tree $(git merge-tree --write-tree 88c43616 906ca0ec) -p 906ca0ec -p 88c43616`: the actual push was **41 lines, 1 file**; the model was shown **661 lines, 7 files**, 6 of them another PR's. CI's own log line `Incremental diff: 661 lines` had been printed on every affected round and read as normal.
- ⚠ **The full diff was never affected, which is exactly why this survived.** `gh pr diff` is three-dot from the merge base, so `git diff --name-only origin/main...HEAD` — the check used to rebut the findings — kept returning the correct six files. Author and reviewer were looking at two different diffs and both were internally consistent. The rebuttal was *right about the PR* and never located the cause, so the same finding came back the next round.
- ⚠ The failure is proportional to how long a PR stays open and how busy main is; a fast-merging PR sees nothing. It also silently gets WORSE the more the review is trusted, because the prompt ranks the incremental section above the full diff.
- Prevention: **end the range at the PR head sha, never at `HEAD`, in any workflow triggered by `pull_request`.** `AFTER_SHA: ${{ github.event.pull_request.head.sha }}`, then `git diff "$BEFORE_SHA".."$AFTER_SHA"`. `HEAD` in that context is not your branch. Generalises to every `pull_request`-triggered step that reasons about "what this branch changed" — lint scoping, changed-file filters, coverage deltas.
- ⚠ Second defect on the same line, fixed with it: the diff was captured as `> incremental.diff 2>&1`, folding git's stderr into a file that is **prompt input**. A git warning inside that file reads to the model as diff content. stderr now goes to its own file.
- Verified: the shipped step body extracted from the YAML and run in a worktree checked out at that synthetic merge commit — normal synchronize `41 lines (24fc80ff..906ca0ec)`; `opened` → no incremental; unreachable before-sha (force-push) → warning + clean fallback; zero sha → clean fallback. Old form in the same worktree: 661 lines.
- Caught by: reading the failing run's log after the finding recurred, instead of re-running the rebuttal. **A review finding that returns after a correct rebuttal is evidence about the REVIEW PIPELINE, not about the diff** — that is the transferable part, and it took two rounds to act on.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` (the `Get incremental diff` step's ⚠ comment carries the merge-ref reason and the measured 41-vs-661).

### The same versioning lesson recurred one layer up, three weeks apart in the same epic — fixing the DERIVED table taught nothing about its PARENT (#2333, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `strategy_signals` keys on `(strategy_id, strategy_version, instrument_id, signal_bar_date, signal_kind)`, and `strategy_version` hashed the strategy's module, params, universe and cost model — but not `indicator_series.RULE_SET_VERSION`. So editing how the SMA, RSI, ATR, MACD, Bollinger or the stochastic is COMPUTED produced different signals under an UNCHANGED version, and the uniqueness key treated the old and new rows as the same row. Found incidentally while shipping #2311, which changes `indicator_series.py` and therefore moves that constant.
- Root cause: the phase-4b entry above (*"a derived artefact's key must carry the version of every pipeline that can change it"*) was applied where it was found — `strategy_outcomes` — and the fix stopped at that table. The identical question one level up ("what else, if it changed, would change a SIGNAL?") was never asked, because `strategy_version` was already a hash *of several things* and so read as complete. **A version that covers four inputs looks thorough in a way that a version covering one does not, which is why the fifth omission survives review.**
- The tell, restated so it fires on a PARENT and not only on a derived table: for every versioned artefact, list the modules whose OUTPUT it consumed, not the modules that computed it. Here the strategy body consumes `indicator_series`' output and contains nothing else — S-1 is `sma_series(fast) > sma_series(slow)` — so an indicator definition IS the strategy's filter logic under parent criterion 11.
- Prevention: **the identity carries a registry-wide `INPUT_RULE_SETS` mapping, and its completeness is CHECKED rather than declared.** A per-strategy `inputs=[…]` field was rejected: the failure being fixed is an author not thinking about indicator versions at all, and a field they must remember to fill is the same omission with a nicer name. `tests/test_strategy_registry.py::TestInputRuleSetsAreComplete` ast-walks every module in `app.services.strategies`, imports each `app.services.*` it names, and fails if any of them exposes a `RULE_SET_VERSION` the registry does not carry. ⚠ Direct imports only — a strategy reaching a pipeline through a helper is not caught, and that limit is written into the test rather than left to be re-derived.
- ⚠ The remedy differs from 4b's on purpose and the difference is worth keeping straight: there the input version is a KEY member, because it sits outside `outcome_resolver`'s own hash and without it the corrected outcome is UNSTORABLE. Here it is INSIDE `strategy_version`, so the corrected signal already has a distinct key; the stored `input_rule_set_versions` column is the queryable half only, exactly as `universe` is. Copying 4b's shape literally would have put it in the uniqueness key and let one strategy identity span two indicator rule sets — the very thing sql/255's header rejects for `universe`.
- Measured 2026-08-06, and it is the reason this was worth doing immediately rather than scheduling: `select count(*), count(distinct strategy_version) from strategy_signals` → `(0, 0)` and `select count(*) from strategy_outcomes` → `0`. Both ledgers empty, so the fix is a hash-payload change plus a re-run of nothing. Once rows exist, the indicator version behind them is recoverable only by guessing which historical module source hashes into a 12-hex digest — i.e. not recoverable, so the column could never be backfilled honestly.
- Caught by: not by a gate. `#2311`'s author noticed the coupling while editing the hashed module and filed it instead of folding it in. **A version constant you are about to change is a prompt to ask who keys on it** — nothing in the test suite or the review bot can raise that question, because every test passes both before and after.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/strategy_registry.py` (`INPUT_RULE_SETS`, and `StrategyIdentity.input_rule_set_versions`); `sql/257_strategy_signals_input_rule_sets.sql` (header + column comment, incl. why it is NOT key material); `app/services/signal_ledger.py` (`LedgerRow.input_rule_set_versions`); `tests/test_strategy_registry.py::TestInputRuleSetsAreComplete`; `scripts/probe_2333_strategy_identity_input_rule_sets.py` (8/8).

### Zero is not NULL — a sentinel every consumer already rejects was winning the SELECTION (#2232, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `instrument_share_count_latest` returned `latest_shares = 0` for 25 instruments, and `instrument_dilution_summary` reported 5 of them at exactly `-100.00%` YoY with posture `buyback_heavy` — a ranking-engine quality sub-score input reading "they repurchased every share" off a filer's tagging artefact. On the ownership card the same zero routed the instrument to `no_data(reason="absent")`, whose banner tells the operator to trigger a fundamentals sync; the sync re-fetches the same zero, so the state could never clear.
- Root cause: `share_count_history` derives the point-in-time count as `COALESCE(MAX(val) FILTER (concept = dei), MAX(val) FILTER (concept = us-gaap))` and `instrument_share_count_latest` then takes the newest period with `WHERE shares_outstanding IS NOT NULL`. **Zero is not NULL**, so a zero-valued fact won twice: it beat an older positive row on `period_end DESC`, and inside its own period it beat a positive row of the OTHER taxonomy through the DEI-first `COALESCE` (VIPS: DEI 0 masked us-gaap 111,665,972 at the same `period_end`). Every consumer already encodes `<= 0` as unusable — `get_ownership_rollup` short-circuits on `outstanding is None or outstanding <= 0`, `instrument_dilution_summary` guards its ratio with `yoy_shares > 0` — so the rejection rule existed and was simply applied one step too late to be able to offer the usable row instead.
- The zeros are the filer's, not our parse. Confirmed against SEC EDGAR directly: `data.sec.gov/api/xbrl/companyconcept/CIK0001795586/us-gaap/CommonStockSharesOutstanding.json` (Chime) returns `val=0` at 2025-06-30 / 2025-09-30 / 2025-12-31 and `66,950,736` at 2024-12-31, matching `financial_facts_raw` row for row. Post-IPO the classes are tagged dimensionally and companyfacts strips dimensional facts (sec-edgar §7.17), so the undimensioned line we receive is the zero.
- Prevention: **when a consumer rejects a value as unusable, the REJECTION belongs in the selection, not only in the consumer.** A `WHERE x IS NOT NULL` that picks "the latest row" is a ranking decision, and it must exclude every value the reader will throw away — otherwise the unusable row consumes the slot a usable one would have taken, and the failure presents as *absence of data* rather than as *bad data*, which points the operator (and the next session) at ingestion instead of at selection. Self-review prompt: for every `ORDER BY … DESC LIMIT 1` / `DISTINCT ON` over source values, list the values the consumer treats as unusable (`0`, negative, empty string, epoch date) and confirm the selection predicate excludes each. ⚠ Do it per COLUMN CLASS, not blanket: here the point-in-time STOCK columns are definitionally positive while the FLOW columns (`shares_issued_new`, `buyback_shares`) must keep zeros, because "issued nothing this quarter" is a real value — a blanket `val > 0` on the CTE would have turned real quarters into gaps.
- ⚠ Second lesson, from the revert probe: **two of the five injections reported `*** NOT CAUGHT ***`, and they were right to.** Once the base view's `FILTER`s are positive-only, `shares_outstanding` can only be NULL or positive, so the `> 0` predicates in the two dependent views are RESTATEMENTS of an invariant their base guarantees — flipping either back to `IS NOT NULL` changes no result and no test can construct a case that distinguishes them. Same shape as the #2314 "validator mirroring a SQL CHECK is unreachable through the writer" entry. The resolution is to keep the local predicate (it is never weaker than what it replaces) but pin the ENFORCEMENT POINT with its own test and say in the migration which lines are redundant by construction — claiming coverage for an unreachable line is the actual defect.
- Measured, full population on the dev corpus (4,641 instruments carrying a denominator): 25 change, all of them the zero cohort — 17 zero → positive, 8 lose the fake zero and become genuinely absent, and **0 instruments whose count was already positive moved at all**. 5 fake `-100.00%` dilution readings → 0. Reproduce with `SELECT count(*) FROM instrument_share_count_latest WHERE latest_shares = 0;` (25 before, 0 after) and `SELECT count(*), count(DISTINCT instrument_id) FROM share_count_history WHERE shares_outstanding = 0;` (231 / 156 before, 0 / 0 after).
- Caught by: measuring the #2232 cohort before designing anything. The issue framed the defect as "implausible denominators — issuer XBRL errors and subsidiary-scoped facts", and its own worked examples (`AVAL = 7`, `BRUN = 1`) are all NON-zero. Ordering the cohort ascending put ten zeros above them that the issue never mentions, and those are the ones with a mechanical fix. **The bottom of a sorted cohort is worth reading even when the ticket has already named the cause.**
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `sql/259_share_count_views_positive_only.sql` (rationale header carries the SEC cross-source and the A/B); `tests/test_share_count_history_views_post_swap.py::TestPositiveOnlyShareCount`.
- ⚠ Belongs in `.claude/skills/data-engineer/SKILL.md` (`instrument_share_count_latest` read-side view entry) and `.claude/skills/metrics-analyst/SKILL.md` §"Dilution / share-count metrics" — not added there in this PR because the headless loop lacked write permission on `.claude/skills/**`. Copy it across on the next session that has it.

### A `-m db` failure with no SQL change between runs: two worktrees share ONE test template (#2342, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: a `-m db` run over six files passed; the next identical run failed five tests in `tests/test_share_count_history_views_post_swap.py` asserting the PRE-migration view behaviour, with nothing changed between them but a Python file in an unrelated module; a third run passed again. Read exactly like "the new migration is wrong".
- Root cause: `tests/fixtures/ebull_test_db.py` decides template staleness from `_migration_hash()` over the INVOKING WORKTREE's `sql/*.sql`, caches it in one machine-global file (`platformdirs.user_cache_dir("ebull")/test_template_hash`), and builds one database, `ebull_test_template`, on the shared port-5433 cluster. Two headless loops were running in different worktrees with different pending migrations, so whichever ran pytest last rebuilt the shared template from its own `sql/` and wrote its own hash. Measured at the failure: `select max(filename) from schema_migrations` on `ebull_test_template` returned `257_…`, while the invoking worktree's `sql/` contained `259_…`. The advisory lock serialises concurrent BUILDS; it does not make one template's content correct for two worktrees.
- Prevention: **before disbelieving a db-test failure that appeared without a relevant change, check what schema you actually tested against** — `psql`/psycopg into `ebull_test_template` and run `SELECT max(filename) FROM schema_migrations`, then compare it to `ls sql/ | tail -1` in your worktree. If they disagree, the run is void; force a rebuild and re-run before touching the code. ⚠ The dangerous direction is the quiet one: a sibling whose `sql/` is a SUPERSET of yours makes your migration's tests pass in a worktree where the migration is absent. Generalises to every machine-global cache keyed on per-worktree content — the parallel-session rules in `.claude/CLAUDE.md` cover corpus jobs and the dev stack, and this is the same class one layer down.
- Caught by: the failure being non-monotonic (pass → fail → pass) rather than by any assertion message, all of which pointed at the migration.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; #2342 (options: namespace the template + cache per worktree, or assert post-attach that every local `sql/*.sql` is in `schema_migrations` so a wrong-schema run fails loud).

### A DERIVED calendar silently stood in for a DESIGNATED one, and the miss was invisible because the miss looks like "not published yet" (#2234, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: none. `finra_short_interest_refresh` reported `job_runs.status='success'` on 61 of 64 runs and held a contiguous 30-date history. Nothing was failing.
- Root cause: FINRA Rule 4560(a) requires reports "no later than the second business day after the reporting settlement date **designated by FINRA**" — the rule *delegates* the calendar and FINRA publishes it as per-year settlement/due/publication tables. There is no formula. `_settlement_dates_to_fetch` derived one anyway (15th + last calendar day, walked back over **weekends only**), so on a US market holiday the probe lands on a date the CDN does not serve.
- Why it could never self-heal: the CDN answers a wrong-date probe with **403**, which the provider correctly maps to `FinraNotFound`, which the job correctly treats as "not disseminated yet" and skips. The skip is logged at INFO and retried forever. A permanently-missing settlement date and a not-yet-published one are the *same observable*.
- Measured, full population — every half-month anchor from 2021-07 (the `finra_short_interest_observations` partition floor) to 2026-07, `scripts/audit_finra_settlement_calendar.py`: **122 anchors probed, 118 derived == designated, 3 holiday-shifted** (`2022-04-15→04-14` Good Friday, `2024-01-15→01-12` MLK Monday at a 3-day shift, `2024-03-29→03-28` Good Friday), **1 unresolved** (`2026-07-31`, genuinely not yet disseminated). Those 3 dates were unreachable by any number of future cron fires.
- ⚠ The transferable shape: **when a source DESIGNATES a calendar, deriving it is a data-treatment decision made from first principles, and "source-rule before design" fires.** It does not look like one — it looks like date arithmetic, which is why it went in without a citation. The tell is the word *designated* (or *as prescribed by*, *as published by*) in the governing rule: that is the source telling you the answer is a list, not a formula.
- ⚠ Second-order: the fix probes, so it GUESSES a date. That is only safe because the file is self-describing — `ingest_settlement_file` now asserts every row's body `settlementDate` equals the date it was fetched under, mirroring the RegSHO sibling's `Date` assertion. Before this the parser **discarded** the body's own date and stored the caller's, so a mis-fetched file would have been silently mis-dated. Validated on the full corpus first: 0 body mismatches across all 122 files.
- ⚠ Third: `.claude/skills/data-sources/finra.md` §2.7 asserted the bimonthly CDN returns **404** and that its provider "only maps 404". Both false — it returns 403 with a 111-byte body, and the provider had mapped both since #916. The skill had drifted away from the code in the direction that makes the bug harder to see.
- Prevention: before deriving any reporting calendar, grep the governing rule for *designated*/*prescribed*/*published by*; if present, the calendar is a list to be read or probed, not computed. Where probing is the only route, validate the fetched artefact against its own self-description before storing it.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/jobs/finra_short_interest_refresh.py` (`_fetch_designated_file` + the `_MAX_ANCHOR_WALKBACK_DAYS` rationale comment); `app/services/finra_short_interest_ingest.py` (body-date contract in the module docstring); `scripts/audit_finra_settlement_calendar.py` (re-runnable full-population scan).

⚠ **RECURRED THE SAME DAY, INDEPENDENTLY (#2240 S-4, 2026-08-06).** Recorded here
rather than as a second section, because a recurrence is worth more than a
restatement — and because the two happened in parallel branches, so S-4's tests
were written before this entry existed on `main`. `tests/test_strategy_s4.py`
imported `COMPRESSION_WINDOW` and `BREAKOUT_LOOKBACK` into its reference and the
probes for "window shortened 100 → 50" and "lookback shortened 20 → 10" both
reported `*** NOT CAUGHT ***` — the identical failure, in a reference that was
independent in algorithm (`sorted(window).index(v)` against the module's
count-of-comparisons) and shared in parameters. Two strategies out of three hit
it, which is the number that says this is the default mistake and not a slip.

Two things S-4 adds to the prevention above:

- **Scope.** The rule binds the numbers the ASSERTION depends on, not the ones a
  fixture is built from. Using the module's constant as scaffolding — sizing a
  window, indexing a warm-up boundary — is fine and often clearer; it is the
  expected-value path that must carry hand-transcribed literals.
- **Write the probe even when the constant looks inert.** Neither instance was
  visible until a probe mutated the constant: 35 tests, `ruff`, `pyright` and a
  clean 32.5M-bar full-population arm all passed in the S-4 case, and the arm was
  clean *because* it was fed the module's constants indirectly too. A spec
  constant is the single thing a reference is most tempted to share, so it is the
  one most worth probing.

### A widened allow-list must be pushed to every CONSUMER, not just the new writer (#2213, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `external_identifiers` gained `provider='openfigi'` CUSIP rows at #1233 PR-1b (2026-05, the approved fallback per the settled decision of 2026-05-22). The new writer and every call site PR-1b itself touched were written `provider IN ('sec', 'openfigi')`. Two **pre-existing** consumers were not — `institutional_holdings._resolve_cusip_to_instrument_id` (#740) and `cusip_resolver._select_resolvable_via_extid` (#836, `630a2b6b`, 2026-05-03), both authored before OpenFIGI existed. Measured cost on dev 2026-08-06: of the 1,503 CUSIPs mapped by OpenFIGI only, **1,483 had zero `institutional_holdings` rows**, against 3,062 of 3,083 for SEC-mapped ones. GOOGL, GOOG, XOM, TSLA, BAC, KO and BRK.B all sat at zero, and `GET /instruments/TSLA/institutional-holdings` returned its no-coverage payload — the endpoint the data-engineer skill uses as its own worked example.
- Why nothing caught it for ~11 weeks: **every component reported success.** The daily `cusip_extid_sweep` logged `promoted=0` *truthfully* — its SELECT genuinely matched nothing — and the resolver returned `None`, which routes to `unresolved_13f_cusips`, which is the documented destination for an unresolvable CUSIP. No exception, no counter, no failed job. The only tell was a table that should have had rows and did not.
- ⚠ The docstrings actively pointed the wrong way. `_resolve_cusip_to_instrument_id` asserted *"CUSIPs use `provider='sec'`"* — true when written, false since PR-1b — and a comment in the sibling bulk path listed the alternative mapping routes as *"all provider='sec'"* directly above an `EXISTS` that already read both providers. **A stale docstring on a narrow filter reads as a deliberate scope, which is what stops the next person widening it.**
- Prevention: **when adding a value to an enum / provider / status allow-list, grep for the OLD literal across the whole tree and classify every hit as widen-or-justify — do not stop at the sites the feature touches.** Concretely, `grep -rn "provider = 'sec'" app/` and check each: a *writer* of that provider stays narrow; a *consumer* that resolves an identifier almost certainly must widen. Record the classification. The same shape is already in this log as "contract-field wired into one model not its sibling" (#1955) — this is that class, with the twist that the drift was **backwards in time**: the new code was right and the old code was left behind, so reviewing the feature diff could never have found it.
- ⚠ Widening a lookup that previously returned at most one row can make it 1:N. Here 75 CUSIPs carry both providers, so a bare `JOIN ... IN ('sec','openfigi')` emits each twice — double-counting the sweep's `promoted` and rewashing the same accession twice. A `LATERAL (... ORDER BY <priority> LIMIT 1)` keeps it 1:1 by construction while preserving the outer ordering that a `DISTINCT ON` would destroy. Production held no dual-provider CUSIP that was *also* pending, so this state was unreachable through a dev query and had to be constructed in a test.
- ⚠ Do NOT widen the siblings reflexively. `n_port_ingest.py:691` and `blockholders.py:435` carry byte-identical copies, but both measured clean (0 of 8,137 NULL-instrument `blockholder_filings` have an OpenFIGI mapping; fund observations cover 1,433/1,503) because each has a provider-wide **bulk twin**. Measure each consumer separately — identical code does not imply identical exposure. Tracked as latent in #2329.
- Caught by: a full-population crosstab of `(has_sec, has_figi)` against "does this instrument have any rows in the typed table" — a two-arm natural experiment where provider is the only variable. Not by any gate, and not by reading the diff of the feature that introduced the provider.
- Verified: full-population A/B over all 68,694 distinct CUSIPs — 1,503 gained, 0 lost, 0 re-attributed to a different instrument. 1,426 of the 1,503 gained appear in SEC's own `13flist2026q1.txt`; the 77 that do not are OTC / foreign / post-bankruptcy tickers, the documented limitation of that list.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/data-engineer/SKILL.md` (§ "Widening an identifier allow-list"); `app/services/institutional_holdings.py::_resolve_cusip_to_instrument_id` + `app/services/cusip_resolver.py::_select_resolvable_via_extid` (both docstrings carry the incident); `tests/test_institutional_holdings_ingester.py::TestCusipResolutionReadsBothProviders`; `tests/test_cusip_resolver.py::TestExtidSweepReadsOpenFigiProvider`.
### A revert probe's ANCHOR is guarded and its SELECTOR is not — `NOT CAUGHT` has three causes (#2240 S-2, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: the probe that deletes S-2's decile tie-break (`sorted(..., key=lambda item: (-item[1], item[0]))` → `key=lambda item: -item[1]`) reported `*** NOT CAUGHT ***` on two consecutive runs, for two different reasons.
- Cause 1 — **the fixture agreed with the defect.** `sorted` is stable, so an all-equal score dict built in ASCENDING key order comes out in ascending key order whether or not the secondary key exists. The tie test asserted `{1, 2}` and got `{1, 2}` either way. **A tie-break test must build its input in the order that DISAGREES with the rule** — highest id first, here. Same family as the S-1 flat-fixture entry above: a fixture the defect satisfies pins nothing.
- Cause 2 — **the probe's `-k` selector named a test that could not observe it.** After the fixture was fixed the probe STILL reported NOT CAUGHT, because it pointed at `test_insertion_order_does_not_change_the_answer`, whose scores are DISTINCT: both forms sort identically there, so the test passes under the mutation. The anchor had matched exactly once on both runs, and the harness's guard 1 was satisfied throughout.
- ⚠ The transferable half is cause 2. **Guard 1 proves the mutation LANDED; nothing proves the selected test READS the mutated path.** `NOT CAUGHT` is therefore ambiguous between three causes and they must be triaged in this order, before touching the code: (1) does the selector name a test that exercises the mutated branch at all; (2) does that test's fixture distinguish mutated from original; (3) only then, is the code genuinely not load-bearing? Getting this order wrong deletes a real guard because a mis-aimed probe said it was dead.
- Measured: with the fixture rebuilt highest-id-first AND the selector repointed at it, the full harness reports **21/21 CAUGHT** (`uv run python scripts/probe_2240_s2_cross_sectional.py`). The tie-break is not decorative — the full-population census puts the decile cut on an exact tie on **5 of 762** rebalance dates.
- Caught by: the probe harness's own NOT CAUGHT report. No gate could have: every test passed, the anchor guard passed, and the probe looked correct. Third time on this epic that a probe found the defect in the TEST rather than in the code (S-1 conjunction, S-3 imported constants, this).
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/probe_2240_s2_cross_sectional.py` (probe 9 carries the retargeting reason); `tests/test_strategy_s2.py::TestSelection::test_ties_break_on_the_lower_instrument_id` (the docstring carries why the dict is built backwards).
- ⚠ Belongs in `.claude/skills/engineering/test-quality.md` beside "A boundary defect inside a CONJUNCTION needs one fixture per comparison" — **not added there in this PR because the headless loop still lacks write permission on `.claude/skills/**`** (the #2240 S-3 entry above records the same block). Copy both across on the next session that has it.

### An independent arm that ranks on a SURROGATE key re-implements a different tie-break (#2240 S-2, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: the S-2 `--ranking` arm re-derives the whole decile cut in SQL and compares it set-for-set against the module. It failed on **2 of 762** rebalance dates, each by one name.
- Not float drift, which was the first hypothesis and is the expected one when two arithmetics disagree at a cut. Measured instead: both pairs are **exact** ties in *both* arithmetics — `2.8125/1.875 - 1` and `2.0625/1.375 - 1` are 0.5 on 1989-01-03; `450/225 - 1` and `41.71875/20.859375 - 1` are 1.0 on 1999-10-01.
- Root cause: the rule breaks ties on **instrument id ascending**; the SQL ranked on `series_id` and joined to `instruments` only at the end, so it broke ties on a **surrogate key** that happens to order almost identically. `ORDER BY score DESC, series_id` is a different rule from `ORDER BY score DESC, instrument_id`, and the two agree on every date where no tie reaches the cut — 760 of 762 here.
- ⚠ The transferable part: **an independent re-derivation must re-derive the TIE-BREAK KEY, not just the ordering statistic.** A surrogate id is the natural thing to carry through a SQL pipeline (it is what the corpus is keyed on), and substituting it is invisible until a tie lands exactly on a boundary. Same family as the "verifier only accidentally right" entry above, with the twist that this one *did* fail loudly — because the arm compares SETS per date rather than counts.
- Prevention: when an arm re-implements a ranked rule, resolve the ranking key to the SAME identifier the rule names, before the `row_number()`, and say so in the SQL. If the two identifiers are 1:1 today, that is a property of today's corpus, not of the rule.
- Caught by: the arm's own per-date set comparison on its first full-population run. A count-only comparison (`762 dates, 101,318 picks` on both sides) would have reported PASS — the two selections have the same SIZE on every date.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/verify_2240_s2_cross_sectional.py` (`_RANKING_SQL`'s `eligible` CTE carries the incident and the two dates).
### An HTML-table parser that reads `<td>` position as column index has not implemented the table model — and the symptom looks like bad DATA, not bad indexing (#2175, 2026-08-07)

- Symptom: DEF 14A Item 403 multi-series tables stored the *Title of Series* ticker
  as the beneficial owner — `LLYVB`, `FWONK`, `LBRDK`, `QRTEP`, `BATRK` — with a
  share count taken from whichever cell the value-recovery scan happened to reach.
  103 rows / 40 distinct ticker-shaped names / 50 accessions
  (`SELECT … WHERE holder_name ~ '^[A-Z]{3,5}$'`). Four prior tickets patched this
  family per-case (#2088 / #2094 / #2097 and #2169's lineage) and a fifth was
  scoped as a name-rejection test.
- Root cause: `_parse_table_html` extracted cells with
  `<(?:t[hd])\b[^>]*>(.*?)</t[hd]\s*>` and used each cell's ordinal as its column.
  That identity holds only for a table with no spans. Liberty Media's holder cell
  carries `rowspan="6"` over the issuer's six share series, so rows 2-6 are three
  `<td>`s short and every cell in them sits three columns left of where the parser
  looked. Nothing about the output says "indexing"; it says "the parser thinks a
  ticker is a person", which is why four rounds went after the names.
- ⚠ **The reuse check answers this in one command and was worth running.**
  `pandas.read_html` (already a dependency) implements the model: on that exact
  table pandas 3.0.2 returns a uniform 24-column frame with `Chase Carey Director`
  repeated across all six series rows where our parser returned 24 cells then 21.
  Not adoptable wholesale — it also expands `colspan` and re-does cell-text
  extraction — but it settled the diagnosis in minutes and supplied the reference
  algorithm (`_HtmlFrameParser._expand_colspan_rowspan`).
- ⚠ **Half the table model is worse than none, and only the full-population A/B
  said so.** The naive form (carry by markup index, expand every row) passed 118
  unit tests, the AAPL/GME/MSFT/JPM/HD panel with ZERO drift, and Codex — and
  regressed three classes:
  1. **Carry placed by markup index.** With `colspan=3|6` before a `rowspan=4`
     caption the carry landed three columns early, wrecked the promoted header and
     took `0001628280-26-025998` from 16 holders to **0**. Fix: read `colspan` for
     the layout arithmetic, but emit the carried cell ONCE — expanding it would
     multiply captions inside `score_headers`, which SUMS keyword weights.
  2. **Cell-less spacer `<tr>`s materialised.** EDGAR's generated markup wraps
     header rows in `<tr>`s with no `<td>` at all; filling them with carried text
     inserted a phantom one-cell row that the two-row-header promotion then adopted
     as `column_headers`. Fix: such a row emits nothing but still CONSUMES a row of
     every live span — consuming is the table model, emitting is not.
  3. **Stacked name/address rows inherited the holder's figures.** Issuers put
     `rowspan="2"` on the VALUE cells; the address row previously died on the
     "neither shares nor percent" guard and now inherited real numbers, so
     `0000107140-24-000176` gained `New York, NY 10055` at BlackRock's exact
     6,782,743 / 14.97%. Fix: a row that inherits a cell and contributes no value
     of its OWN is a continuation, not a holder. ⚠ The first cut of that rule also
     matched the second row of a two-row HEADER (own cells are captions, inherits a
     spanning caption) and took the same accession to zero a SECOND time — the
     inherited cells must themselves be values.
- ⚠ **A shared cell extractor is shared with a caller that already compensated for
  the same defect.** `parse_summary_compensation_table` uses the same
  `_parse_table_html`, and the Item 402(c) path has carried its own rowspan-shift
  compensation since #1945. Restoring the spans re-based it: **580 accessions**
  drifted, all the same way — `executive_name`, `fiscal_year`, `salary` and
  `total_comp` identical, `principal_position` repeated once per continuation year.
  Fixed by keeping that caller's input byte-identical (`expand_spans=False`) rather
  than re-tuning an arm this ticket has no mandate over. Same shape as the #2356
  `_strip_inline_html` incident: **a fix placed at a shared chokepoint reaches
  callers that never asked for it, and the second caller's compensating machinery
  is exactly what makes the damage invisible to its unit tests.**
- Prevention: (1) before adding an Nth per-case patch to a markup parser, ask
  whether the parser implements the format's own model — for HTML tables that is
  `rowspan`/`colspan`, and `pandas.read_html` is the one-command oracle for
  whether it matters on YOUR failing document; (2) a span-restoration change is a
  corpus change at the top review rung — its regressions are re-SELECTION of which
  table wins, which no panel and no unit test can see; (3) when the fix lands in a
  shared extractor, enumerate the other callers FIRST and decide per caller whether
  its input may move.
- Enforced in: this prevention log;
  `app/providers/implementations/sec_def14a.py::_expand_row_spans` (both deliberate
  departures from the pandas reference carry the measured accession that forced
  them), `::_row_contributes_only_inherited_values`, `::_CITY_STATE_ZIP_ONLY_RE`,
  `::_resolve_columns` (the mirrored percent-caption guard) and the
  `expand_spans=False` comment at the SCT call site;
  `tests/test_sec_def14a_parser.py::TestRowSpanExpansion` (8 revert-probed
  invariants); `scripts/audit_def14a_rowspan.py` (arm-3 mechanism audit).

### A monkeypatched parser entry point must forward the FULL signature — the harness reported a total collapse the code never had (#2175, 2026-08-07)

- Symptom: the DEF 14A full-population A/B reported `sct_rows 67,828 -> 0` — every
  Item 402(c) exec-comp row in the corpus gone. The parser had not regressed: the
  same filing (`0000004904-25-000043`) returned 17 SCT rows when called directly.
- Root cause: `scripts/ab_2140_def14a_parser.py` monkeypatches
  `parser_mod._parse_table_html` with `def traced_parse_table(table_html: str)` to
  instrument header promotions. The branch under test had added a keyword to that
  entry point (`expand_spans=False` at the SCT call site), so every SCT call raised
  `TypeError`, the harness's per-accession `except` swallowed it, and the counter
  stayed at zero. The Item 403 arm passes no keyword, so its numbers were fine —
  which is what made the output look like a targeted collapse of one arm rather
  than a harness fault.
- ⚠ The direction is the dangerous one. **A harness bug of this shape produces a
  DRAMATIC number, and a dramatic number reads as a finding.** Ten minutes went on
  "which of my changes emptied the SCT" before running the parser by hand. The
  existing skill rule ("treat a clean result with more suspicion than a dirty one")
  covers the false-negative direction; this is the false-POSITIVE one, and it costs
  the same.
- Prevention: (1) any monkeypatch of a function under test takes `**kwargs` and
  forwards them — a wrapper that narrows the signature is a silent behaviour change
  in every caller that uses the part it dropped; (2) when a harness reports a
  total-collapse figure for ONE arm, reproduce it with a direct call to the parser
  before reading it as a result; (3) changing a parser entry point's signature means
  grepping `scripts/` for monkeypatches of that name in the same commit
  (`grep -rn "parser_mod\._parse_table_html" scripts/`).
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `scripts/ab_2140_def14a_parser.py`
  (`traced_parse_table` and `patched` both take `**kwargs`, with the measured
  `67,828 -> 0` in the why-comment).

### Cite a migration by its SLUG — a renumber re-aims every bare "sql/NNN" at someone else's SQL (#2304, 2026-08-07)

- Symptom: `#2304`'s migration was authored as `sql/257_unresolved_13f_openfigi_invalid_identifier.sql` and its rationale was cross-referenced from five places as "sql/257" — two service docstrings, an inline comment at the tombstone write, and two spots in `.claude/skills/data-sources/openfigi.md`. The PR then sat unmerged through the 2026-08-06 GitHub Actions outage. While it sat, the TA loop merged `sql/257_strategy_signals_input_rule_sets.sql`, so the file was renumbered to `260` on rebase — and the five pointers were left behind.
- ⚠ **It then happened AGAIN, to the same file, during the round that fixed it (2026-08-07).** `260` was free when the branch was renumbered and taken by `sql/260_strategy_signals_thin_cross_section.sql` (#2345) before the fix could be pushed, so the migration moved to `261`. Twice in two days, same PR, same cause. That is the number that says this is structural and not a slip: with two loops merging, the number you reserve is contested for as long as your branch is open, and how long that is has nothing to do with you — the first collision was caused by a GitHub outage.
- ⚠ The failure is worse than a dangling reference. `sql/257` still RESOLVES, to a real and completely unrelated migration on `main`, and `grep -rn "sql/257"` now returns this branch's stale pointers interleaved with `signal_ledger.py`, `test_strategy_signals_ledger.py` and this log's own #2333 entry, all of which cite `257` correctly. A reader tracing the `openfigi_invalid_identifier` CHECK lands on a strategy-signals rule-set table with nothing to say about it, and nothing anywhere reads as broken.
- Why the gates missed it: a migration number is prose everywhere except the filename. `ruff`, `pyright` and the whole test suite are blind to it, the numbering scan only checks for duplicate numbers on disk, and Codex checkpoint 2 reviewed the branch when the number was still right. The review bot caught it — and had to raise it **three rounds running** before it was fixed, because the first two answers only renumbered the file and its own header.
- Prevention: **cite a migration by its full filename (or at minimum its slug), never a bare `sql/NNN`.** The slug is unique, survives renumbering, and makes `grep` find every site at once, which is exactly what a bare number does not. Concretely, write ``sql/261_unresolved_13f_openfigi_invalid_identifier.sql``, not the bare number. When a renumber does happen, grep the OLD number across the whole tree and classify every hit — some of them belong to whoever now occupies it, and those must be left alone.
- ⚠ Two loops merge into this repo now, so the window between "pick the next free migration number" and "merge" is where this lives. A branch blocked for hours is not an edge case; it was the ordinary outcome on 2026-08-06. Assume the number you reserved will be taken and write the reference so it does not matter.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/cusip_resolver.py` (`OPENFIGI_NEGATIVE_STATUSES` docstring + the `OpenFigiInvalidIdentifier` branch comment); `app/services/openfigi_resolver.py` (`OpenFigiInvalidIdentifier` docstring); `.claude/skills/data-sources/openfigi.md` (§4.3 table + the negative-status prose).
### A hard bound expressed only as a CANDIDATE stops binding the moment the candidate cannot be produced (#2240 5a, 2026-08-07)

- Symptom: `position_builder.build_positions` offers each close source as a candidate and takes the earliest. C3 (max-hold expiry) was one of them: `entry index + max_hold_bars`, priced at that bar's OPEN. When that bar's open is masked, C3 contributes **no candidate** — and a later C1 exit signal is then the earliest surviving one, so the position books at it. S-3 entering at bar 2 with `MAX_HOLD_BARS = 10` and a masked open at bar 12 closes at an RSI exit on bar 15: a **13-bar hold against a declared maximum of 10**, priced off a bar the strategy could never have reached.
- ⚠ The transferable part: **C1/C2 are opinions and C3/C4 are facts, and "earliest wins" flattens the distinction.** A signal-pair exit and a resolver verdict are things that MIGHT close a position; a declared `max_hold_bars` and a rebalance drop-out END it by construction. Modelling all four as candidates in one list is right for the ordering and wrong for the bound — the forced ones need a CEILING as well as a candidate, so that failing to price them removes the close without removing the limit.
- Fix: the forced sources set `ceiling` whether or not their bar can be priced, candidates are filtered to `<= min(ceiling, window.end)`, and a position whose forced bar exists but is unpriced is left open under a new reason `close_bar_unfillable` (ours, flagged as an addition beside the spec's two). ⚠ Its `open_until` is the CEILING, not `None`: the hold ended there and only its price is unknown, so treating it as eternal would drop every later trade in that instrument over one masked bar — a narrowing far larger than the defect.
- ⚠ When the forced bar IS priceable the ceiling changes nothing, because "earliest wins" already picks it. **That is why no test caught it**: every fixture with a working max-hold bar passes either way, and the defect needs a masked open AND a later exit signal in the same fixture.
- Caught by: `codex exec review --base origin/main` at checkpoint 2, before first push. Not by 51 passing tests, not by the full-population sweep (which reported 0 invariant violations — I1-I4 check overlap, conservation and provenance, and none of them expresses "the hold respects its declared maximum").
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `app/services/position_builder.py` (the `ceiling` block carries the incident); `tests/test_position_builder.py::TestMaxHoldClose::test_an_unpriceable_expiry_bar_does_not_let_a_later_exit_book_the_trade` and `::test_an_unbookable_hold_does_not_suppress_the_rest_of_history`; `scripts/probe_2240_position_builder.py` probes 20-21.
- ⚠ The invariant sweep should have had a fifth check — `bars_held <= max_hold_bars` wherever one is declared. Added to `_check_invariants` as part of I4. **A property no assertion states is a property the full population cannot falsify**, however many rows it walks.

### "The caller must mask it" is a docstring, not a guard — and no caller had ever done it (#2240 5a / #2354, 2026-08-07)

- Symptom: the first full-corpus sweep of phase 5a crashed after ~20M bars on `exit on 2026-03-31: fill_price must be > 0, got 0.0`.
- Measured: `select count(*), count(distinct series_id) from research_price_daily where open = 0` returns **16 bars / 9 series**; `open < 0` returns 0. Joining `research_bar_quarantine` on `(series_id, bar_date)`, **all 16 already carry a quarantine row with `range_usable = false` AND `return_usable = false`**. The quarantine knew the bar was unusable on both axes.
- Cause: `load_masked_series` masks `high`/`low` for a bad range and `close` for a bad return, and carries `open` through unmasked — correct for a bad wick. `outcome_resolver`'s docstring already names the gap and assigns it: *"that is the loader's to close, and a caller reading `B4` bars must mask the open itself."* **Every caller since has read that sentence and none has done it**, because until phase 5a nothing downstream of `resolve_fills` refused a zero price. `signal_ledger.resolve_fills` branches on `fill_open is None` alone, so a stored `open = 0` becomes `fill_price = 0`, and `sql/255` has no positivity CHECK to stop it.
- ⚠ The state was inconsistent in the direction that hides: the WRITER would have stored the row and every READER refuses it (`outcome_resolver`: *"entry_price must be > 0 … gross_return_pct divides by it"*). Nothing was stored only because the ledgers hold 0 rows.
- ⚠ **An obligation written in the docstring of the module that CANNOT discharge it is the weakest form of a rule there is.** It is read by people working on that module, and it binds people working on a different one. If the producing layer cannot enforce it, the consuming layer needs a runtime refusal — which is what caught this, three phases later and one full corpus in.
- Caught by: a full-population sweep, on bar ~20,000,000 of 23,339,583. A 60-series smoke run over 615,940 bars was clean, and so was every fixture.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; **#2354** (the upstream fix, deliberately not folded into the 5a PR); `app/services/position_builder.py::EntryFill`/`ExitFill` (the runtime refusal); `scripts/verify_2240_position_builder.py::_to_series` (the caller obligation discharged, with the count reported); `tests/test_position_builder.py::TestCallerContract::test_a_zero_fill_price_is_refused`.

### The category an operator wedge names must be derivable from the source, or the wedge is a lie regardless of how it is seeded (#2214, 2026-08-06)

- Symptom: `institutional_filers.filer_type` is `ETF` for exactly 1 filer and `INV` for 11,464, so the `etfs` wedge on `/instruments/{symbol}/ownership-rollup` is zero on **3,071 of the 3,151** instruments that hold any 13F row at their latest `period_of_report`, and zero on all five golden-panel names. ⚠ It is NOT "empty for every instrument", which is how the first draft of this entry and the issue title both put it: **80** instruments do render a slice. All 80 are the single seeded filer — `GEODE CAPITAL MANAGEMENT, LLC` (`0001214717`) — and they show it only because their latest period is `2026-03-31` or earlier, which is Geode's last 13F-HR; the rollup pins each instrument to its own `max(period_of_report)` (`app/api/instruments.py:4326`), so at `2026-06-30` Geode is simply absent. ⚠ That absence is **reporting lag, not a missing label** — Geode files at the 45-day deadline (Q1 2026 13F-HR filed `2026-05-15`, SEC EDGAR direct), so its `2026-06-30` report is not due until `2026-08-14` and the panel's zero fills on its own. The panel would have confirmed the false version: the refutation only exists at full population. The ticket read as a seeding problem — "the chain already exists, this is about populating it, not designing it" — with acceptance criteria naming Vanguard / iShares / State Street / Invesco as the CIKs to seed.
- Root cause is one layer below the empty table, and there are three independent reasons, each sufficient:
  1. **The classifier asks the wrong registrant.** N-CEN is filed by the RIC trust, not the 13F manager — the same disjointness #963 already documented for N-PORT in `sec_nport_filer_directory`'s module docstring. `ncen_classifier` walks `institutional_filer_seeds`, which holds 13F-manager CIKs. Measured, full population: 8 CIKs appear in both `institutional_filers` (11,465) and `sec_nport_filer_directory` (2,036), and all 8 are internally-managed closed-end funds that file both because they *are* the fund.
  2. **The N-CEN rung can never emit `ETF` anyway.** `compose_filer_type`'s second tier maps `investmentCompanyType`, and every ETF is `N-1A` — the same code as an ordinary open-end mutual fund. The structured ETF flag lives one level down, per-series, at N-CEN Item C.3.a (`FUND_REPORTED_INFO.IS_ETF` in the DERA data sets).
  3. **13F consolidates the ETF adviser away at source.** Of the 152 13F filers that canonical-name-match a primary adviser of an `IS_ETF = Y` series, **38 file 13F-NT and carry zero rows in `ownership_institutions_current`** — BlackRock Fund Advisors, SSGA Funds Management, Invesco Capital Management, J.P. Morgan Investment Management among them. Their holdings are reported inside a multi-mandate parent's 13F-HR. The mass sits in `VANGUARD GROUP INC`, `BlackRock, Inc.`, `STATE STREET CORP`.
- ⚠ **Seeding it anyway would have shipped a wedge whose LABEL is false, not merely coarse.** The same measurement priced it: typing all 152 matches `ETF` moves 13.03% of the observations layer into `etfs` (4,001 of 4,514 instruments gain a slice), and `VANGUARD GROUP INC` **alone** supplies 11.39%–17.91% of the golden panel's institutional shares — while its own N-CEN filings split 137 `IS_ETF=Y` against 138 non-ETF series-filings. One filer, whose book is about half non-ETF, would be ~80% of the wedge.
- Prevention: **before populating a category, check that the source can express it at the granularity the category is keyed on.** 13F is filed per MANAGER and carries no fund breakdown, so no amount of filer typing separates ETF-held from actively-managed shares; that is N-PORT's job, at series level (settled decision #1577, "ETF identity = series/class"). The trigger is a taxonomy whose only remaining input is a curated list — a hand-maintained seed table is often a signal that the derivation was never available, not that nobody got round to it. Ask what would populate it automatically and why that does not exist.
- ⚠ **Scope the negative claim precisely, or it overreaches.** What is unavailable is an *exact* ETF/non-ETF partition of a 13F book. A caveated `ETF-affiliated manager` bucket IS buildable from the 152 name matches, and whether an imperfect labelled wedge beats an empty one is a product-labelling call, not a data one — so it goes to the operator with the numbers, not decided here. Codex made this correction to the first draft of this entry, which said the wedge "cannot be built".
- ⚠ **The Vanguard series split (137 `IS_ETF=Y` / 138 not) proves the book is MIXED. It does not price the over-attribution**, because a series-filing count is not assets. The load-bearing number is the 11.39%–17.91% panel share, which is measured; the count is a sanity warning beside it. Two unexplored bridges, recorded so they are not re-derived: Form ADV / IAPD via `ADVISER_CRD_NUM` / `ADVISER_LEI` (not an EDGAR dataset, would improve the identity join only), and N-PORT series-level holdings (a different and better ETF-ownership figure, but not a partition of the 13F total, so it cannot satisfy this issue's conservation invariant).
- ⚠ Related trap: **"the wedge" has two tables and they disagree, so name the one you measured.** The rollup buckets `institutional_holdings` JOIN `institutional_filers.filer_type` pinned to the latest period; the observations layer (`ownership_institutions_current`) carries its own denormalised `filer_type` and is not period-pinned the same way. Today the same Geode rows read **0.0000%** on the golden panel through the rollup and **2.30%–3.16%** through the observations layer (`ETF` 2,522 rows / `INV` 2,305,362). Neither is wrong; they answer different questions. A figure quoted without its table is unfalsifiable.
- ⚠ Related trap, hit on the way: `ADVISER.tsv` identifies the adviser by name, 801-file-number, CRD and LEI, and **never by CIK** — verified in the live N-CEN XML tag set as well as the DERA extract. A structured field being present does not mean it is joinable to your key.
- Caught by: measuring the join before building it. The issue's own acceptance criteria would have passed — a "non-trivial ETF filer population cross-checked against Vanguard / iShares / State Street / Invesco" is exactly what the 152 matches are — and the invariant it proposed as the gate (`institutions + etfs` sum unchanged) holds for a re-partition no matter how wrong the partition is.
- ⚠ **Every figure above is an as-of, not a constant.** All of them are the 2026-08-07 run of `uv run python -m scripts.audit_ncen_etf_advisers`; the 2026-08-06 run of the same script read 153 matches / 13.06% / HD 15.99%, because 13F ingest keeps adding filers to the name-match pool. Re-run the script rather than quoting these — the argument is the *shape* (one multi-mandate filer dominating a slice its own book only half belongs to), and no decision here turns on the third significant figure.
- Enforced in: this prevention log; `.claude/skills/data-sources/sec-edgar.md` §2.2.1 (N-CEN + the DERA data sets) and the §2.1 13F-NT note; `scripts/audit_ncen_etf_advisers.py`, which recomputes every figure above.
- ⚠ The two-tables clause above belongs in `.claude/skills/data-sources/sec-edgar.md` §2.2.1 as well — **not added there in this PR because the headless loop's write on `.claude/skills/**` was denied** (same block the #2240 S-3 entry records). Copy it across on the next session that has it.
